# Chapter Summaries

One compact entry per drafted chapter. Volume 01, Batch 0001 (days 1–11), files in `workspace/volume-01/batch-0001/`.

## Volume 01 — The White Furrow

**1. Iron in the Water** (day 1, well head, ~3,400 words)
The pump test comes in seven short (41/48, static lift, collar shut) and the machine is sound. The outer rows beyond the yard have gone over sideways, lying along the row, white at the top, uncut. Marek opens the well head with Ilsa Kern, gets an arm to the elbow, and finds the wound held not by a seal but by a **compound pad** — a base coat four seasons old with a thinner dressing laid on it every year since, never replaced, all of it sitting on the scarwood by weight, with warm iron-smelling water coming up through the middle of it at the six o'clock position. Kellan's log records a proper seal. The warmth is written into the notebook as a guess, because he has no instrument and cannot defend a difference found with his palm. Marek also notes the pad beating at about one beat in four seconds, out of step with the pump. **Then he rebuilds the draw by hand**: the foot valve and gland are fouled with a season of iron off the wound, a Crown mechanic would be thirty shillings a day from Fennmere, and the cooperative has no such money. He clears it in his wet coat, runs the pit with the collar off, and fills the dry yard trough; four men drink from it and the water tastes of iron. The counter stays at 41/48 and he says why out loud: forty-eight was nine feet into good gravel. **That is the pride beat and the dread in one object.** Nia brings the season's board and tells him the third of the three things a charter can do is the one nobody in the yard says out loud, and will not say it either. Clerk Perrin Osse takes the walk-through, explains that a charter condition is a thing to be *shown* and not repaired, and records seventeen days. Marek announces, in front of the crew, that the fault is in the root line and he will prove it in front of an inspector rather than in a shed. Ends with the wrapped fieldbook's needle refusing to settle while the head is open. **Change:** the fault is located, the log is exposed, and the pump runs. **Cost:** a public promise, and the discovery that a perfect pump fixes the part of the problem that can be fixed with tools.

**2. The Loaned Steward** (day 1, seed house yard into evening, ~3,300 words)
Tova Reed of the Dryfoot Trust arrives at midday with eleven crates of the Longneck rye, the only dry-land line the Salt March has left. She has read the cooperative's filed numbers before she met anyone. In the yard, in front of the members, she makes Marek do the sum: the log claims 1,100 turns/day delivered to Lantern Reach ground, the ground can take 720, and 832,000 turns over six years went somewhere the cooperative does not own. She names four conditions: a measured draw she may witness and file alongside his; published numbers open to any member, stranger, or clerk; her own veto on any use of the seed vault; and a copy of everything to Cray whether or not it helps. She will look at the cooperative's records for two days and say nothing until they are out. She notices the mill-keeper's hand tally board and asks nothing about it. **Change:** a hostile, precise reader is inside the books. **Cost:** the fiction that the paperwork is clean.

**3. The Coupling** (day 3 afternoon, the closing hours of Tova's two-day look, ~4,200 words)
Marek's loading test on the seed house return line. Tova cites a witnessed, minuted condition in the seed house book; Marek denies the entry is there, and the prose marks the denial as deliberate, because it is — the entry is in the book and he will not concede it. She refuses; Ilsa refuses without understanding it. He starts it anyway. Cold iron water comes up through the vault's rootwood bed; three trays of the Longneck go translucent. Tova kills the draw and shears the coupling with his own spanner, then tells him to shut the rig down properly, which he does himself. **No rootwork occurs and none is available**: he wants the wood to say something and it is wood, wet and cold, doing nothing, patiently, for as long as he keeps asking. The **dial and the stroke tally are kept as two columns** — pressure and volume — and the result entered is *one hundred and thirty-four turns of the handle, needle at three-eighths under the mark*, a rate and not a day. Two records come out of it that agree without knowing about each other — his test says the return will not pass what the log claims, Tova's tape says the return is 22 rods of 8 inch against a specified 41 rods of 12 inch — and the missing margin is compost. Tova rules three trays spoiled and does not soften it. **The beat is shame, not relief**: he was right about the number and reckless about the cost, and both are true at once; and he discovers that respecting a refusal is not the same as agreeing with it, and had known that for eleven seconds and gone on anyway. Kellan's crew applies the last compound top-up to the pad that evening; Renn Tallow says, *you cannot close a thing with tar.* **Change:** Kellan's number is false for a physical reason, not a clerical one. **Cost:** the coupling is destroyed and unreplaceable before the eighteenth, part of a family's seed is gone, and two days of the season are gone and cannot be got back.

**4. The Vote and the Ledger** (day 3 evening, seed house, ~4,100 words)
Tova states in advance what the March will and will not lend and **withdraws nothing**: the vault veto stands, the cooperative broke it this afternoon, and the record is to show a written condition agreed and then broken. Marek owns the spoiled trays in the first thirty seconds. Dunmar Fitch will not enter a true cause without a licensed hand behind it, and when a member from the north row says *put the wrong number in the book and let the man in Fennmere end it*, the room agrees and Dunmar refuses. **The claim in the log is moved and lost, 9 for and 22 against with 4 abstentions, out of thirty-five hands in a room of forty.** Bram Oskell arrives in person with Kellan's offer: a free gasket and a proper seal in exchange for a two-year exclusive extension **and** the cooperative's confirmation of six years of maintenance. Tova explains that the offer buys a signature, not a seal, and refers to a paper on the shape of these offers that she refuses to describe in the room — **no number, no date, no place**, so that nobody in the room can start behaving like the people in it. Lissa refuses it in front of the room, then insists it be entered as a motion rather than left as a woman's speech; **Marek moves it himself, in his own name, and it carries 31–4**, with the second page annexed and unsigned. The trays motion is carried 34–0. He then orders the cooperative's own draw figures, log, work book and loading test opened to anyone who asks, with his own error on the same page in the same hand, as a standing act of the meeting and not a motion — so the open book is a private act and not a filing. Oskell mentions, in passing, that Sera Quill is on the fen road on the sixth day. **Change:** leverage traded for exposure, and the truth is kept out of the book by a vote. **Cost:** the open book, which is not a rule but a smaller and more dangerous thing, and a chamber that has just chosen the charter over the record.

**5. The Woman With the Old Book** (day 6, fen road, morning, ~2,800 words)
Sera Quill is walking to a one-day job at the salt cistern and will not stop. Marek offers a written scope instead of a favor. She names her terms: his raw notes, not copies; he does not touch the instrument unsupervised; she signs only what she has seen, with her license and her name behind it. Fee: fourteen pounds, paid personally, second visit another fourteen. She tells him one thing for free: a branch on this fen was cut on Crown money eight years ago while Aldren was in the ground, and she has spent six years failing to find out by whom. She has papers she will not show him. She has not asked for the fieldbook and tells him she will ask, in front of witnesses, when she asks. **Change:** a licensed witness with a fee, a scope, and her own agenda. **Cost:** fourteen pounds he will not get back, and the dread of being asked about the kit.

**6. Measured Water** (days 7–8, well head, the batch midpoint, ~4,800 words)
Two days of quarter-hourly logging. Sera brings two hundred hundredweight of compost for the return and insists on it, because logging a wound without feeding it makes the wound worse. **It is £40 at the 4 s/cwt rate, which the cooperative cannot pay and which she pays out of her own fee money and enters in the open book under her own name** — consistent with her Ch 5 rule that a document with a document in it is a document with an opinion in it. The kit is unwrapped in front of the crew. The copper contact clip is the path; the fieldbook bead and the well's conduit are the anchor; the compost is the return, and she makes him say all three aloud. The brass gauge and the fieldbook's needle disagree, and the margin widens each hour; she forbids him to reconcile them. On the second afternoon the pressure comes up in events of two, three, four and five beats about four seconds apart, she is sent for a second dial, Gael goes to the sump, and in four seconds with nobody in the pit Marek gets a work sequence — valve, hand, wait, valve, lamp, valve — **with nothing looking back and nobody in it saying anything**; the well's own older rhythm runs under it, and *inside* it, between the second thing and the third, is a shaft: a lamp on a chain and his father's hands, older, with a torn cuff, on a wheel. The fieldbook's last page carries the line of figures, the column head and figure *confirmed — set — 4.*, and a year eight months after the disappearance. **No serial number is on that page.** He ends the session himself, writes three findings, records his own reading as UNVERIFIED before she can ask, states the impossible date is probably a copying error and must be treated as one, and hands her the raw notes instead of keeping them. Costs: nosebleed, disorientation, and a **memory bleed** — the term she gives him. No mark on his skin; she says it will not stay that way. **Change:** the fault is proven to be upstream, and the reading cannot be proved. **Cost:** the first private instrument, surrendered, and £40 of compost nobody in the cooperative can replace.

**7. Who Cuts a Branch** (day 9, Weir Row, all day, ~4,600 words)
Marek and Gael walk onto ground that the charter's fourth schedule puts under a maintenance order — a trespass against the cooperative's own charter. A Crown sign on the fifth post reads *BRANCH MAINTENANCE — DISTRICT SCHEDULE. NO LOCAL HOLDER. REPORT CUTS TO THE SUB-OFFICE, FENNMERE.* Cart ruts sit eight feet back on the soft berm. The branch was cut, healed, and cut again, with two die-struck serials: **N-2214/K** on the old healed face and **N-3318/K** on the fresh one. Marek dates it as a range, writes *both* possible dates side by side, draws it to scale with a scale bar, and has Gael Senn sign as a second witness. He finds a marker ninety yards away — **Ilsa Kern's** grandmother's, HANSA KERN, 1802, with a cup dish worn into the top and a later line about the short road to the weir — and does not tell Gael until he has to. Ilsa arrives at two, makes him put her grandmother's road in the margin in his own hand, and tells him to say her name to the inspector not because it helps him. Renn Tallow waits at the road fork with an offer: a full parts kit for the season for the notebook page. Marek refuses aloud, in front of Gael, because he wants it on paper. A boy rides in at dusk: the annual inspection has been brought forward to tomorrow. **Change:** the enemy is a schedule, not a man, and the ground is somebody else's. **Cost:** an enemy who now knows what he is looking for, and one day left.

**8. The Inspection Came Early** (day 10, batch climax, first half, ~4,900 words)
Inspector Ansel Bray arrives with Perrin Osse and the emergency-seizure clause: seal the head, take the seed stock, or refer for relocation. **In the open pit he times the pulse twice, does not feel it, and decides not to write it down** — he will not put an officer's pen to a thing one man felt and a district inspector did not, and the discretion is his and is recorded in the prose, so the clue is protected by a decision rather than by an oversight. He reads the open book for an hour and puts the district's own filing beside the cooperative's work book page. The pad, the trespass, and Marek's own signatures make this look like cooperative negligence — until the schedule extract. Bray identifies **N-2214/K** as a maintenance-schedule number, one item executed twice on a K three-year cycle, and a half-sheet of a table older than the cooperative's charter. The cut is not unregistered; it is on a schedule no local holder was told about, and notification is not in the schedule as drafted. Marek unwraps the fieldbook in front of the clerk; **Sera Quill puts her nine raw pages of quarter-hourly figures on the table first**, squared to the edge, with the page of four strokes past the refusal on top, and states that she took every figure from the operator by dictation and never opened the book — so the document he reads aloud is in the room. He reads Finding 3 aloud, states in the same breath that it is his own unreliable reading and cannot be proof of anything, and asks Bray to enter it as an unverified observation. Bray picks up the fieldbook, reads the last page and the impossible year twice, sets it down like something that has turned out not to be his, then takes **Marek's weir notebook** and reads N-2214 and N-3318 off the to-scale drawing where they were actually copied out. **Sera asks for the page, reads the two numbers twice, says she has seen the shape of that table, refuses to say where she saw it or what it is, and asks for both refusals to be entered with her name** — the payoff of Ch 5. She still names no branch and no office. Bray enters the serials as fact and not as inference, enters the finding, declines to seize, and moves the decision to the eighteenth — the charter's own expiry, so nothing is solved. Kellan arrives at noon, takes his hat off for the first time in six years, and says his crew pulls the compound kit out of the fen tonight. The glass has dropped two tenths. **Change:** the instrument is on a Directorate record and can be seized, and a licensed engineer has been drawn past her own six-year silence. **Cost:** eight days he already had, plus the only thing he owns that has ever told him the truth.

**9. The Thing at the Weir** (day 10 night, storm, batch climax, second half, ~5,300 words)
A storm comes in off the west. Gael Senn goes up the fen road with a hooked pole to save the north lamp; Ilsa sandbags the pit. **Ilsa reports the round trip in full**: the satchel to the third gate, the north post gone over with the bulb still in it, the lamp moved onto the wire and lit, a coat and most of a can of oil gone — and then the way back is shut, the drain ditch and the causeway both up, so Gael is on the far side of the race with the weir steps and the maintenance plank for ground. The pasture flock — 130 head, ninety ewes and thirty-six lambs and four rams — comes down the embankment crown with a drover's dog and something walking beside them, keeping them off a line on the ground. **Lissa Vale names it: a pathhound.** It herds, it does not hunt; it cannot be called off or tied up, and killing the thing it stands on does not get rid of it — she has known two men try that and both were still herding in the spring. The only thing that moves it is a change in where the water goes, and she has not said the word in this yard in thirty years. The animal stops 130 head dead on a twelve-foot crown. Gael is pinned on the maintenance plank over the race. Renn Tallow passes with the compound kit and does not stop, and says *I have got the load.* With no supervisor, no license, and no time, Marek puts his bare hands on the scarroot's cut face and gets **four seconds that are wrong** — the flow reads as going away instead of coming up. Damaged wood lies. He then does the actual work with a crowbar and his own graft shears, levers the healed seam open, and vents the dead branch's stored pressure into the old drainage channel. The animal loses the route it can feel, breaks off, and leaves on its own, going to look for the water. It is not commanded. Gael crosses whole. Marek collapses, is sick, cannot account for the last hour of the walk, and finds his own handprint on a stone he does not remember touching. He knows the name of what happened to him now: **memory bleed** — the same four seconds as the first and dearer than the first, because the length is not the price. The seam will not close and the branch bleeds from now on. No animal comes back. The storm drops nine tenths, the pit floods, the pad lets go all at once, and **four sacks of fiber and fortyweight of compound** are on a cart up the fen road. **Ilsa then says the second thing, at dawn, in the pit:** the flock has been the seed house's compost return for eleven years and there is nothing else to put in the bed. **Change:** the flock and the seed vault's compost return are gone, and the reason is now on the page in the climax chapter instead of surfacing in the aftermath. **Cost:** animals, a compost heap, a branch that will bleed for years, and the strongest evidence the Crown will later have against Marek.

**10. Seven Days** (day 11, seed house, morning into evening, ~5,000 words)
The cooperative makes a costed list on the back of a seed order. Tova gives the return arithmetic: 4 hundredweight a month of bought compost at 4 shillings is eighty pounds a month against a treasury of five pounds eleven, and a bed with no return kills seed in a fortnight. Lissa asks, in front of everyone, whether Marek intends to hand the fieldbook in. Tova's return arithmetic is **confirmation, not revelation** — the compost-return link is already on the page from Ch 9. Perrin Osse serves two documents: the district's filing note, and Kellan's second letter — the gasket and the seal offered free, exclusivity removed, with the accusation left in. **The cooperative votes four things and one refusal.** Four: a seal built by hand with the date, the hands, and a witness, signed by someone recorded as never having done the work; one shared draw with a written return plus a standing pressure log open to anybody and a monthly root-change report filed with the sub-office; a repair attempt Sera Quill can certify, on her signed scope, with *as it stands tonight* on the front of it; and Tova's seed annex, entered in the cooperative's book and offered to the district as a condition of renewal. The refusal: Kellan's letter, declined in front of the district's own clerk, who asks that the refusal be recorded. **The bypass is not a fifth vote** — Marek writes it in the margin with a figure against it and no money, and Dunmar rules under section nine that a note by an officer is evidence of what the cooperative intended and not of what it did, so it is carried as a note, unnumbered and unsigned by anyone else. On Tova's evidentiary rule — any reading taken by an unlicensed hand is logged in the seed house and may be checked by anyone who asks — the fieldbook goes into Lissa's custody under a written use log, and Marek does not argue and gets no speech about it. Sera signs the scope. Nia says the cart is booked for the twentieth and asks for a written list of what she takes and where it goes, with a column for destination and a column for who stood there when it went. **The chapter closes in a scene, not a ledger**: the room empties, his mother stays at the table with the book closed in front of her and makes him say out loud what he has, which begins *I do not have a plan* and then gives the list and then the fieldbook in her keeping and the fact that he has to come to this table and ask for it; she tells him a custody is not a forgiveness and would rather he learned the difference here than at a hearing, and leaves him the lamp, a warning about the compost page, and the night. **Change:** he is the man in the room who is not holding the instrument, and he says so out loud to the one person who will hold him to it. **Cost:** the fieldbook, and an obligation the cooperative voted for itself.

## Volume 01, Batch 0002 (days 12–20), files in `workspace/volume-01/batch-0002/`

**11. What Her Name On It Costs** (day 12, Thursday, well head, all day, ~3,300 words)
Sera Quill comes down the pit with a lamp on her belt and sends Marek up the ladder before he can get his boots off. **The examination produces two findings and only one of them is the cooperative's.** First, the pad is not a seal and the log is a lie — known since the first morning. Second, and worse for everyone: there is a **callus on the wound, a clean pale lip of new wood across the top third of the cut face, with a wet line on its lower edge**, because a man dressed four seasons of tar over it once a year and the coat kept the wood alive. Untreated, the wound would be a foot longer than it is; cut back and dressed in the first season it would have closed by the third. Kellan's log is a lie and Kellar's work is real, and Sera says she does not know how to write both in one line. **Then the dangerous job, priced out loud**: she cannot certify a repair, only supervise one; her certificate carries her personal liability because she is a freelancer with no employer, no district behind her, and no office that would stand in front of her at a hearing — "the charge is that a licensed engineer's name on a node is a promise that the node is somebody's to answer for, and the somebody is me." **Marek is refused the pit**, and told why at length: two contacts, four seconds each, the second dearer than the first, and a third would either give him something true that no clerk can use or take a piece of him he cannot identify. "I cannot let you do it, because you cannot be a witness at your own well." She signs first, he signs under her, and the order of the two names is the most expensive thing he does all day. The **second visit is £14, payable in advance, asked for on the fifteenth**. Ends: Ilsa tells him to tell the five people at a table, not at the pit, because a man told at a table has come to a decision and a man told at the pit has been informed. **Change:** the liability is named and the cost is priced. **Cost:** the instrument is his no more, and he cannot go in the pit again.

**12. The Column With Nothing In It** (day 13, Friday, the gate, the low acre, the well head, ~3,200 words)
The crew is assembled by asking, and three of the four say yes for the wrong reasons. **Jonas Byre** (the north-row man with the cough, named here for the first time) says yes because he is tired of being a man who is surprised, and asks that the reason be written down somewhere. **Anneke Roos**, fifty and fifty-one in the spring, farms the low acre, and names the real objection: not that it is dangerous but that four unlicensed people will be the four names in a document about a thing that went wrong, while the two who arranged it are not in it. She will go, on one condition, spoken and then repeated: *I know less about this than the man who is asking me and I am going in anyway, and if I am wrong I want to be able to point at the hour.* **The first lesson is that a record is not a description** — four things in every entry, what was done and nothing that was not, a name against every hand, the hour and the day the work happened and not the day there was time to write it, and what a stranger could check without asking. "If you cannot date it, you write *cannot be dated* in those words and you do not guess." The **standing pressure log's first page** is ruled by Lissa Vale with eight columns; the last is *licensed person present*, and it is filled with the word **none**, underlined, because a record with a blank in it is a record with a hole in it. Anneke identifies it as the only part of the page that cannot be argued with. The log's first entry runs two to four o'clock. **The pulse arrives at 3:40**, five beats about four seconds apart, and is logged as *cause not established* on Sera's instruction — "do not look for one between now and the eighteenth, or you will find one and it will be the wrong one." **Change:** a crew that is not a queue. **Cost:** the day ends with a column full of the word *none*.

**13. The Line She Would Not Sign** (day 14, Saturday, the pit, ~2,500 words)
The pit is bailed out by hand — nine buckets a round, some three hundred rounds, the iron water tipped on the embankment and never into the return main, because Tova's veto on the vault is in writing. Then **the first refusal to sign, and it is correct.** The third hour's entry has been drafted as *head opened for examination of the wound. Wound inspected. No adverse condition observed.* **Anneke Roos will not sign it.** There was no examination: the pad is on and nobody has touched the wound. Four unlicensed people were within reach of a living conduit for the better part of an hour and the entry does not say so. She rewrites it at nineteen lines instead of four, including that she put her left hand to the elbow on the cradle, that the cradle moved, and that she did not like it. Sera Quill tells Marek it is the first entry in six years she would put in front of a hearing officer unaltered, and that a cooperative has never written one for her. The rule about **an entry made at the hour it describes** is written on the inside cover, attributed to Anneke Roos, signed by Gael Senn and initialled by Sera Quill, with Jonas Byre's name round the edge of the last page in pencil. Tova refuses the pit water for the trays on smell and on her veto, and the mill floor is written into the return column as labor twice a day with two names on it. **Marek closes the day's log himself, gets to the fourth hour, and does not improve a line that is nearly true.** **Change:** the rule that will convict him. **Cost:** he learns the lesson on Saturday and breaks it on Monday night.

**14. Four Seasons In Four Layers** (day 15, Sunday, the pit, all day, ~3,200 words)
The pad comes off in **four layers, not as a lump**, because it never bonded to anything and each season's coat ran down over the one below and set a lip. The top two come off whole in hard black shells about a foot across over six hours; the third has gone to soup in the flood and comes off with hot water and a flat knife and four hundred buckets; the base is still on. **Ilsa Kern holds the flat knife** and does eleven seasons' worth of it in a minute. **Jonas Byre goes down to take the bar and cannot get his leg**, and has to be hauled out on his hands and knees, and says he is not going down four feet again, and asks that it be written up as a man in a pit who should not have been in a pit rather than as a thing about his chest. **The crew is four.** The base coat — the fourth of the four — comes off at half past two in one piece, with a shallow bowl in it, and the bowl is the shape of the callus that has been growing over the top of the cut for four seasons. Sera Quill does not leave the rim all day and comes down the ladder herself to help haul Byre up, and the working notes for the fifteenth name her against the hour. Underneath: pale, clean, dry-looking scarwood with a ridge of new wood across the upper third and a wet line on it. **The material is the pad itself** — four baskets of the chopped fiber from the lower coats, which came out of the flood dry inside a black soup and snap rather than squash, and is the coarsest pack in the county. Sera's method, given in the shed: a seal is a fill *and* a restraint, and the restraint is what does the work; four green withies into **the four unused sockets in the wire cradle, which have been there since before anybody alive knew what they were for**, woven and tied, sacking over, tied down, nothing tarred because withies can be cut out in a year and a pot of tar cannot. The return is labor and compost and neither exists, so the return will be **monthly maintenance with a name on it**. Then she names the price: **£14, on the twentieth, in advance, asked in the shed in front of the four, and if it is not there she will not come.** **Change:** the material problem is solved by taking the dead pad apart, and the wound is a third closed. **Cost:** the crew is four, and there is no tar in this fen.

**15. A Letter With Somebody Else's Name On It** (day 16, Monday, the gate and the pit, ~4,000 words)
**Bram Oskell serves the district's monitored-repair offer at eight:** material, a two-person crew for six days, a district-retained licensed engineer, monthly inspection for twelve months — **for exclusive access to the head, the housing, and the node for the term.** Kellan has written to the sub-office disclaiming it, which Oskell notes is the first time in nineteen years a charter holder has written that sentence to him. Marek asks Oskell for **Kellan's compound book** — batch weights, pot temperatures, four seasons of dressing dates — and Oskell takes it back and is back the same day. **The refusal is taken at the pit, in front of the four, in the rain, at ten o'clock, and it is a struck-through clause and a marginal note in Marek's own hand, not a motion and not a table.** The material is not the reason; the condition is. Perrin Osse records it as the **third refusal this cooperative has entered since the third day**, and says the district does not admire it. Anneke Roos makes him write the cost where they will find it in February, initials it as an opinion rather than a record, and says: refusing the tar is why the seal is withies and sacking, and if it goes it goes because they said no. **The Undercommons courier comes at two** — **Marda Vole**, a mender of implements on the old peat roads, who will not be asked about anything and says the number unprompted: **N-2214 is die-struck into branches on four holdings in three other provinces, the same die, three of them from men who had never met.** She refuses to say whose branches, gives her own name unprompted, and — the actual gift — **the procedure: nobody gives a cooperative the table, but every renewal comes back with the district's works-schedule extract attached, because the form's first box asks what is already scheduled and a clerk will not send ninety forms with an empty box.** Her last line: fill the box as the number says, because that box is the only part of the form anybody in his position will ever be believed about. **Oskell returns at dusk with Kellan's own hand refusing the compound book**, and the reason is correct: the book is the record that there was never a seal in that pit, and handing it over would let a district officer read a holding repair as a Crown specification — "a man who has dressed a wound once a year for four years for nothing is not a man anybody should be able to turn into a witness for a coat, and you have been trying to do that to me since the fourth day." **Kellan has never apologized and nobody asks him to.** Ends with the levy: not a motion, a paper at the table at nine, sixteen households, **fourteen pounds does not buy a week of compost, compost is eighty pounds a month**, and **Anneke Roos signs first and makes him write the sentence: *it is a woman's fee, and the woman is putting her own name on top of his, and we are raising it out of sixteen houses to pay her.*** **Change:** Crown money refused, the courier's fact and procedure in hand, the levy open. **Cost:** the last tar in the fen, and the district's third refusal on file.

**16. Ninety Rows And No Headings** (day 17, Tuesday, the seed house table, ~3,700 words)
Dunmar Fitch files the renewal out at eight with the cause Marek gave him in a shed on the third day and would not enter in the cooperative's book: *return branch as built falls short of the specification; cause not established.* He states the difference between a minute and a filing and says he will write *we chose not to* anyway. **Perrin Osse rides out with the answer**: the application endorsed four days late, and attached to it a printed **works-schedule extract, six columns, no headings, ninety rows**, produced because the form's first box asks the applicant to list works already scheduled and no clerk sends ninety forms with an empty box. It is not to be kept, quoted, or copied. **Row forty-one is N-2214/K and then five more figures across: four dates, all marked, and a fifth figure that is also marked and is a hold nobody in the room can identify.** The two behind today are the two stamped cut faces; the next execution is the twenty-seventh, a Friday. Tova Reed works out what it is from the arithmetic rather than the shape: it is not a maintenance log, it is a **forward work program with a horizon on it** — the two dates that are behind are the last two rungs of a ladder, and the schedule is not slipping, it is **on time**. **Sera Quill gives ground, and it costs her:** she says, unprompted and with the district's clerk in the room, *I have worked with district schedule forms.* Perrin Osse enters it with the hour. Then she reads the columns — first the item, second the **order** date, third, fourth and sixth executions, and the fifth **not a date at all but a hold, suspended until released** — and gives the batch's central discovery: **an order is not a permission. An order is a man in an office, on a date, deciding this cut is due, so the date it was done is not the date it was necessary, and the first order in that row is older than Marek's oldest cut.** She refuses a third time, by the same two names, and both refusals are entered. Marek's cost: **nine days building a case against carelessness, and the truth is that the cut was on time.** The pad is still a pad, the log is still a lie, the wound is still open; the wound is a *program*, and it comes back in nine days. The second **section-nine margin note** goes in beside the bypass note — unnumbered, unfunded, signed by nobody but Marek, dated, *evidence of what the cooperative knows and not of what it has done.* Dunmar also confesses in the margin that he wrote *none known* in the first box of his own application at seven that morning, and that the cooperative's survival rested on Bray happening to be interested in the wrong thing. Perrin Osse returns the sheet and says she will report that the cooperative handed it back faster than she offered it. **Change:** the batch's discovery, and the twenty-seventh. **Cost:** the case he built was aimed at the wrong thing, and the district has a form on which he is now a liar.

**17. The Half-Of-An-Hour** (day 18, Wednesday, the yard, ~3,700 words)
Bray opens with the three things at the front and says he has no immediate-risk finding and that the tenth day's finding stands. **The seal is two days old, four pounds of willow, withy and seed-house sacking, a date cut into a stake and the five names that worked it under it — four hands in the pit and Sera Quill's own name cut in a different hand, because she stood over the whole working day and wanted to be on the wood that would still be there in February — and nothing on the stake says what comes next, because on the sixteenth nobody had written down yet what comes next.** It is the first thing of its kind he has seen in twenty-two years. He reads the working notes twice and finds on their first page the schedule of four seasons of compound pad in four hands and then a fifth line in a different hand that is not about the pad at all — *a confession with a time on it*, the first document of its kind he has had out of a cooperative. Then he reads the standing log, and **last the inside cover, and stops**, and says the rule there is his own practice and is going to cost them the morning. He asks one question. The work was finished at four on the sixteenth. **The completion entry was made at nine the next morning, alone, unwitnessed, and does not disclose it.** Marek answers: *because I wanted to know whether it held before I wrote down that it was done.* **Bray rules under the fourth part, second clause — a record made after the hour of the work is a recollection and not a record — that the completion is not evidenced as maintenance and that the node is not shown to be maintained, and says plainly that he is not the officer who determines the referred application and does not pretend his finding settles it. The charter lapses at midnight on its own terms whatever the referral does next, and after midnight there is nothing to refer.** He is explicit that the ruling is about a line of ink and not about four pairs of hands, that the district does not contest the workmanship, and that he is sorry to Mrs Roos **as a man and not as an officer**. **He does not seal the head, take the seed stock, or refer the charter** — there is no immediate risk — **but the renewal is referred rather than determined by him and the charter lapses on its own terms at midnight regardless, the emergency clause is neither spent nor extinguished, any report from anybody reopens it, the file does not travel with him, and a Crown-funded monitored repair sits in the cooperative's own file that the district does not let offers die.** Kellan Rusk, who came because he is charter holder of record and stood at the back for two hours, is asked by nobody and says two sentences. **Sera Quill puts a Crown seal specification into a district file as a thing to be compared against, at her own professional risk, and asks for it to be entered then and not after.** **Change:** the charter, from a date into a siege. **Cost:** everything, and the loss is entirely the cooperative's own hand.

**18. Eight Trays And A Letter** (day 19, Thursday, the seed house and the loading bench, ~3,300 words)
The thing that keeps Marek awake is not the charter: it is that **the pump still runs and there is no longer any document in Tarrow Fen that says it may.** The seed house has 1,100 turns a day because nobody has sealed the head, and any report from anybody — including a neighbor who tastes the water and finds iron — reopens the clause. Ilsa Kern's reduction: *it is not a siege, it is a watch, and a watch wants a person on it.* Sera Quill confirms the reading of the lapse: no charter means no right to draw, to work, or to enter anything in a district book, and the cooperative's standing procedure from today is that it **hands any district man its own keys and lets him do it on his own authority.** She also explains, unasked, why she has been in the yard six days past a fourteen-pound scope. The **levy is counted with the door open**: **£12 6** from sixteen households, **fourteen signatures, nine of them putting a round share in, and the other five of the fourteen with nothing in the line** — two of them nothing at all and both of them people Marek had taught to read a dial, one Hesk's daughter with a shilling and *and I am sorry* entered separately, and two who signed only so as not to be the ones who had not — **two of the sixteen never signed and their names are not recorded, because the paper forbade it, and £1 14 made up by Marek out of his own money, both amounts entered.** The **seed annex is made real as eight trays on a loading bench**, read aloud line by line and then the whole page again, in columns — crate, quantity, condition, destination, who is standing there, witness. **Lissa Vale will not let the trust's tin go on a hired cart** and it goes into the press under the ledger, *retained at risk, and the trust knows where it is*, initialled by Tova Reed and signed by Lissa in that order. Tova names the condition out loud in a yard: the March's last dry-land line is going into the last place in the fen with a living comparison, and if the fen goes the March has nothing to compare for four years and nine thousand people eat what that crop is. At four Marek writes **the letter to Kellan Rusk** — he will not ask for a reduced draw, will not ask for a return, will not speak about an unmeasured log, and will not say publicly that the acceptance column was a lie, because Kellan's refusal was correct. The letter goes on Nia's list, because it must be delivered by hand in Fennmere and the cooperative has no business riding to a gate there. **Change:** the annex exists as objects, the money exists, the letter exists. **Cost:** £1 14 of Marek's own, and a condition accepted out loud.

**19. What He Went To The Table For** (day 20, Friday, the seed house table, the well house, ~3,900 words)
Marek asks for the fieldbook at his mother's table with five people in the room, and **asks it badly first.** Lissa says no twice, instantly, and then makes him say what he actually meant: he does not need it, he cannot read it, and the last two times he was alone in a pit when it happened. The use-log line is written in the seven-column form with the purpose, the licensed person, and *M. Vale will not touch the instrument and will stand outside the housing*, and **Anneke Roos reads the whole line aloud before she signs it.** The choice at the load test is between the cooperative's honest 720 turns and the log's 1,100, and Marek chooses 1,100 in front of the crew and on the record, because 720 is a lie about a load. **The single Fieldbook panel of the batch appears here, in the book, in Sera Quill's hands, at the seam, with Marek on the boards outside the collar: *The dressing is holding. / Full load will show you the wood. / Choose.*** She reads it out, because a reading taken in a pit is not a reading until somebody else has heard it, and enters that he asked her for it before he chose, which in thirty years is the fourth time and the first three men did not ask. The load runs in three stages from half past ten to one — 720, then 900, then **1,100 from about twenty past eleven, held to one, and nobody stops the pump**, with the load entered in the log before the reading. At 950 the frame hums; **the beat comes up about ten minutes into the full draw in runs of seven and eight**, counted by Gael Senn, who declines to give it a name because *pressure pulse* is the licensed word and a finding, entered by Sera Quill as *cause not established*. **The seal holds.** At half past twelve there is a **quarter of an inch** of seepage at the six o'clock corner — not a leak, a weep, the pack going in where it always went in — measured with a rule twice and written down before the rest of the sentence, with **half an inch named as where Sera stops calling it a weep**, and a warning that a leak under sacking at full draw goes into the housing and not into the pit. **Then the reading, and it is Sera's and not Marek's, and she reconciles it with Finding 3 herself before anybody else can:** the eighth gave valve, hand, wait, valve, lamp, valve, which is maintenance; the twentieth gives **cut, wait, cut**, with no well in it at all and nothing in the order that belongs to anything above ground. Her explanation is that a fieldbook reads what a node has recently had done to it. She refuses a third time where she saw it and what it is, and Dunmar enters all three refusals **and a fourth line in her words: *a licensed reader at the same seam did not obtain the sequence obtained on the eighth; both are logged; the difference is unaccounted for.*** **The certificate is worth one day's evidence about one wound and cannot be entered as a charter condition, and Sera says so before anybody builds a winter on it.** The return she writes into it is **monthly maintenance with a day, an amount and a name, on the first of every month, entered in the standing pressure log** — and when nobody volunteers, **Anneke Roos names Gael Senn and gives the reason, and Gael insists the entry read *nothing found* and not *all well*.** The certificate carries **three** names — Sera Quill's, then Gael Senn's, then Marek's, who signs without being asked, with Lissa entering the hour at the foot. Sera checks his arm for the third time, finds nothing, says the same thing, and says she will not say it a fourth time. **Change:** the seal is standing and suspect, and the person who holds it in January is not Marek. **Cost:** the one instrument in the world he may not open, and the largest name on every page of it is not his.

**20. The Last Line Of The List** (day 20, Friday, dusk, the gate, ~2,600 words)
The carter waits all day because the fen road is not a road after three in the afternoon, and Nia Vale keeps the list herself: **item, crate, quantity, condition, where it is going, who is standing there when it goes, witness.** Twenty-two lines, read aloud as they are entered and the whole page read again from the bench to about thirty people. The Longneck's eight living trays; the trust's tin, which Lissa Vale will not put on a hired cart and which goes into the press instead; six lines of the cooperative's own winter appropriation and a bolt of cloth, and **a hundredweight of the thinnest bought compost, a fortnight, entered in the columns and not going** — a hundredweight of compost is not a thing a consignment carries to a counting house, it is a thing that goes into a return main on the first of every month, and it was the only line the other twenty-one were in service of; the March's two bundles of paper; and one sealed letter to Kellan Rusk to be delivered by hand. **The last line is Nia Vale: one person, one bag, one box, no crate, Cray by the Salt March road, seven days, and the witness is Lissa Vale.** Lissa signs the witness column and says nothing, and goes inside, and Nia tells her not to do it in a yard because she has spent nine days making a document a stranger can read in five years. Nia then says the only thing she is actually leaving behind, to about thirty heads: it is not a success, nothing worked, and what she is taking to Cray is a *documented bad season*, which is a different thing. Then the **first monthly root-change report**, written out by her by lamplight **on the back of a seed order, on the seed-house table, with a broken chisel** (not the mill's board, which is the mill's and is one of the four records at the climax), 240 words, honest and entirely bad news, including that N-2214/K will be executed on the twenty-seventh and that the cooperative cannot object. The cart goes at ten past five; the gate shuts at half past five. **Ending type: departure.** Marek counts four crates he stacked against the seed house wall — the appropriation in three, which came back off the list in Nia's hand when the cart filled, and the compost in the fourth, which is going to stand against that wall until somebody opens the return main with it — and finds a seed-house lamp still hooked over one of them, and Anneke Roos tells him she is not the one who will be on the first of every month, and the volume does not get Nia back.

## Volume 01, Batch 0003 (days 21–30), files in `workspace/volume-01/batch-0003/`

**Reviewed and repaired in place.** The fourteen review findings are applied and listed in `state/continuity.md` under *Fixes applied to the drafted Batch 0003 on review*. No scene was replaced and no planned plot changed. **Dates: the month boundary stated here — that season day 21 is the first of a calendar month — was WRONG, and the Batch 0004 review found it. Months run thirty days, season days 1–30 are October, and day 31 is the first of the next month. Every date in this batch that called a day in 21–30 the first … the tenth of a month has been corrected in the prose and in these entries, and the governing table is in `state/continuity.md` under *Batch 0003 day clock*. The day numbers and the weekdays are unchanged. Originally the batch said: season day 21 is the first of a calendar month, so days 1–20 belong to the previous month and read *of last month* or *of this season*, never *of this month*; days 21–30 read as the plain day number or as the ordinal in the new month.** The full table is in `state/continuity.md` under *Batch 0003 day clock*.

**21. Ten Days Early** (day 21, Saturday, the twenty-first, the pit and the seed house, ~3,110 words)
**Gael Senn goes down the ladder ten days before the certificate's first-of-the-month return, because her name is second on the certificate and nobody else in the building would have gone.** The examination in this chapter is hers and not the certificate's monthly return, and the log now dates it *the twenty-first*; the return the certificate calls for falls on the first of the coming month, which is Ch 31. **The chapter's title was *The First Of The Month* and is now *Ten Days Early*.** She measures the weep at the six o'clock corner with two rules — **a quarter of an inch, measured twice, unchanged since the twentieth, not a leak at the load stated** — and writes it down before she says the rest of it. She writes **nothing found** and insists on the wording. The beat is logged a fifth time, eleven counts in the quarter hour, felt at the coping stone and not at the rail, and the words **cause not established** go in again. The hundredweight of bought compost that stood against the seed house wall since the twentieth goes into the return main: **one cwt against a specified four hundred a month**; a fortnight for the bed, one day in four hundred for the node. **Marek puts the graft**: the return main is 22 rods of 8 inch against 41 of 12 with a fence post at the eleventh rod; the outer field is 22 acres and has been going over sideways for three seasons; the repair is a new run of 31 yards from above the eleventh rod along the ditch line to the field's own root line. Sera Quill asks the three questions — anchor, path, return — and **licenses the work with the 399-hundredweight shortfall written into the return line in her own hand, her name first**, and says what that will cost her at a hearing. She states that **Marek is not making it**: crew work under her license, four unlicensed hands, the engineer of record on the boards saying numbers. **Change:** the repair is licensed and the third exchange requirement is openly missing. **Cost:** a licensed document with a hole in it in Sera's name, and four days' warning of the risk.

**22. The Eleventh Rod** (day 22, Sunday, the ditch line, ~2,910 words)
Four hands in the line, one on the boards, and the licensed engineer on the rail. **Ilsa Kern changes the plan inside ten seconds from the rim** — take the run off the main *before* the eleventh rod and leave the fence post alone, because the stone is not the fault and the fault is 22 rods of 8 inch and no charter — and Marek's diagram goes into his coat. The fiber goes in as a bed along the whole 31 yards; four willow sets, cut with the root plate on off the south embankment windbreak, driven and lashed. **The windbreak is cut down to the old wood for a hundred feet and that cost is entered in the working notes with the hour.** Ilsa does not write it; Marek does and reads it back; Anneke Roos initials it as an opinion. **Anneke forces the second cost onto the page**: the new run does nothing for the seed house or the mill race, the fence post is still in the only main there is, and this is a *run*, not a repair. Water is let in at half past four and reaches the scour in eleven minutes. A willow stake carries the date, the length, the four names, and **anchor: willow, four sets — and deliberately not a licensed engineer's name.** Sera sends them to the top of the field and tells them not to describe it. **Change:** the graft exists and Marek was not needed. **Cost:** the windbreak, and a license that has not improved the water the seed house actually drinks.

**23. Two Hundred And Fifty-Four** (day 23, Monday, the field, the pit, the seed house door, ~2,810 words)
The water goes the whole 31 yards overnight. **The field greens from the ditch and stops at a hard line: nineteen acres of the twenty-two come up and three do not**, and the three are Marek's and he is glad about it. The beat is fainter at the coping stone in the morning and back at the rail by noon, logged again with no cause. **The pump runs clean: the head now holds 1,100 where it would not hold 900 before Saturday**, entered as a dial reading and not as a day of water. **Rowan Teale's boy rides eleven miles up the fen road with the number: 254, seven short of the best figure on a board read for thirty-one years, against 131 on Friday and 126 the Wednesday before**, with the miller's own hand on the chalk twice and an offer to tell the cooperative first, before the district. The relief is then killed in the same afternoon: **nineteen acres of green fodder, fenced for sheep, with no sheep, no cart and a winter coming**, and the flock that has not come back since the tenth. **Jonas Byre ends the chapter** — the clamp-on reed gauge on the return main is on the peg and no one wrote down what it read on Saturday, and nobody in the building can say what it was. **Change:** the repair works and the cooperative believes it is finished. **Cost:** the return has already gone short and the only instrument that would have shown it is unrecorded.

**24. Three Hundred And Ninety-Nine** (day 24, Tuesday, the seed house, the pit, ~2,930 words)
The return is measured, and the measurement is of the compost, and the number is 399 hundredweight a month — **£79 16, against a specification of £80 and a box of £4 5**. Tova Reed follows the loop to its only hole: labor goes onto the mill floor, the floor warms the bed, the bed is kept by what goes in the bin, and **the bin was fed by 130 head of sheep that went up the fen road on the tenth.** The certificate's monthly return, the new run's return and the seed bed's return all pass through one bin that has been empty since the middle of October. Ilsa Kern says the sentence Ch 9 left unspoken: *there is nothing to put in it; that is not a difficulty, that is the whole of what there is.* Dunmar reads **Sera's single-page license sheet** aloud in full, including the line where a licensed engineer puts a shortfall in her own hand and calls it an act and an arithmetic on the same page. The use-log line is written at his mother's table; **Lissa Vale says no once, not twice, and names the change.** Then **the batch's one Fieldbook panel**, in Sera's hands, at the copper seam, read aloud to four witnesses: *The new run is fed. / The new run is fed from below. / Below is not yours.* She will not say whether it is a description or an instruction, and forbids the words *the book told us to* anywhere. **Marek keeps the run open in spite of it**, and the reason he enters is the field, not the book, so that neither reason can be used without the other. Teale's next chalk: **231, down twenty-three in a day.**

**25. A Bucket And A Slip Of Paper** (day 25, Wednesday, the gate, the seed house, ~2,670 words)
Two things come up the fen road in one morning. **Corvin Slee walks four miles from Fennmere with a pail** of tea-colored water and will not come past the gate posts: four wells on the Slee and Warne ground off since the twenty-first, first taste, then color, then smell; forty-one houses on the low quarter; a water-company man with a stick who says the well is sound and the water is bad. Marek can name the branch and **cannot say a word of it**, because the cooperative holds the node and does not hold the branch. Then **Bram Oskell rides out with a notice of a received report** dated the twenty-fourth: the node file is **reopened** and the referred renewal application is **recalled**. Oskell's warning, off the paper: somebody in Fennmere has been waiting eight days for a reason to open that file and is never going to be interested again in the hour a man wrote a line down. Anneke Roos forces two things into the book: that the cooperative was told on the twenty-fourth and came on the twenty-fifth, and that the cooperative is going to arrive in the low quarter as a body that held no charter on the nineteenth telling forty-one households that the district is slow. **The man with the stick is the Fennmere water company, not the district, and he came on the Tuesday, the day the second Tarn well went off.** **Change:** the low quarter is a political problem before it is a hydraulic one. **Cost:** the last procedural door — a referred application — comes off the desk.

**26. A Dog On The Bottom Of The Quarter** (day 26, Thursday, Fennmere, the low quarter, ~3,120 words)
Sera, Marek, Gael Senn and Jonas Byre walk eleven miles. **The adult pathhound** — an old black-and-white collie with one ear off, in a dead channel at the bottom of the quarter, *holding* a line four days after the water went off it, keeping a boy and a goat and everything else off the ground. It is not the weir animal: it is a dog, it is old, and it belongs to a dead woman's grandson who brings it milk. Lissa Vale's rules all hold and none of them helps; **Marek wants to put a crowbar into the bog and Sera refuses it twice before he has finished the sentence.** The wells: the pail came up in a count of ninety on the sixteenth, three minutes on the twenty-second, four and a half on the twenty-fourth, five minutes forty on the twenty-sixth, and **Gael Senn times it four times because she does not trust a number one man has timed.** Sera opens the bed: **the branch is dead, dry, mineral-bloomed, not cut here, killed from the top, and the outlet that turned the gravel under forty-one houses has not turned it since the spring before last.** **Renn Tallow** says one flat true thing from the mill race — the branch has had nothing coming up it since the spring before last, every man in the quarter knows it, and the one who can say so has a charter and will not come — and nothing else, and refers to nothing. The chapter's hard end: **they can prove the dead bed and not the lost head, because the document that would prove it is in a satchel in Fennmere and the route to it runs through Dunmar's *none known* box.** **Change:** the cause is unprovable with what this cooperative lawfully holds. **Cost:** a walk that bought certainty about itself and nothing for forty-one households.

**27. A Third Face** (day 27, Friday, the yard at first light and the crown of Weir Row, ~3,230 words)
**The bypass is argued at half past five in the morning and refused by all five, one at a time, each correctly** — Sera (a second license this month on an unfed node doubles the shortfall under one signature), Anneke (no contractor without a charter, no money, and a Crown-format repair would be a second Crown-shaped document), Gael Senn (*I would do it. That is the difficulty*, wanting it is the objection), Jonas Byre (he will not go down four feet), and the fifth reason which is Marek's own, that a bypass is a ditch before it is anything and the only two men in the county who have offered anything have already been refused. **A third section-nine margin note is written and signed by nobody but Marek** and is worth nothing against a cut. On the crown from eight: two men, a handcart, a die, an item number read aloud once, **a hold released on Monday** that nobody may see the job sheet for, and *you are welcome to stand there, do not stand in the fall*. The face comes off at the mark — **where Marek's to-scale drawing said it would, which is the bitterest part** — and is stamped **N-2214/K** for the third time in a decade, by a man who says, cheerfully, that somebody did them a favor by opening that seam. At the fourth hour the **seventeen-day** bleed from the opened seam **is not reduced. It is not. It stops.** Cause not established. Halfway down the bank the same collie is standing in a channel that has water in it for the first time in five years, herding nothing, because there is nothing on the line to move. **Change:** the branch is cut a third time and the column goes out onto the ground in one morning. **Cost:** about a month of head under forty-one houses, and a bypass that is now a note with five names against it.

**28. Sixty Pounds Ten And Fourteen Days** (day 28, Saturday, the Fennmere sub-office counter, ~3,335 words)
Perrin Osse's note is a *request*, not a summons, and the difference is the whole afternoon. At the counter she has served the schedule of damage on eleven houses by hand and gives the cooperative the true shape of its position: **you do not have to pay sixty pounds ten and you cannot refuse it**, and the schedule starts on the twenty-first. The arithmetic is entered on the page: **39 houses at £1 10 for fourteen days, two public pumps at £1 — £60 10, payable in fourteen days, representations in fourteen days, no appeal and no power of appeal.** **Inspector Bray** comes out of the back room for twenty minutes, states that he does not chair this and cannot be appealed to, repeats the three things, and then reads the cooperative's own book back to it in three extracts — the fieldbook's three lines, the *local conduit* entry, and **Sera's shortfall line** — and tells the room that the third of those is the one that finishes them, because a cooperative that did not know what it was doing does not write that down, and **he would rather not carry it and is obliged to.** Dunmar prices the schedule: the only document that would prove the branch is Crown-held is behind the *none known* box, and filing it means explaining it. **Perrin Osse, off the register:** *the district will not produce that schedule, not to you, not to a hearing officer, not ever, and not as a privilege and not as concealment, because the table has a column that says whether the work is Crown-held.* Marek enters *not established* in the box and a paragraph saying why, in front of the clerk. **Change:** a bill exists, the referred application is gone, and the Crown's own schedule is structurally unavailable to anybody. **Cost:** the siege gets more expensive and the last open door closes.

**29. Three Acres At The Top End** (day 29, Sunday, the field and the seed house table, ~3,250 words)
**The three acres at the top of the field — Marek's own ground, worked on his own account for three seasons, four hundred and ten yards of ditch dug by hand — go pale, lie over, and crust in a day and a half.** The repair that greened nineteen acres is what starved them. Marek finds a fourth option and kills all three himself: the bypass (dead, five signatures); **compost** (400 cwt to hold the crust to the frost, at 4 s, against £4 5); and **the draw**. **Tova Reed refuses**, at the table, in advance of him, with the arithmetic written on the back of his own seed order: 380 turns a day for four days, 1,520 turns out of one body of shallow flow that is under the north annex as well as under his three acres; **three days is nothing, the fourth morning takes about a foot of head off a bed that is already at four degrees with an empty bin and the March's last tin in the press**, and the fourth day is the day the bed goes under. She invokes the seed-vault condition on the ninth page, never withdrawn, and then says the thing that is worse: refusing him is *cheap* for her, and **the second time is easier, because the numbers do not care that it is you.** The **fourth option is his own body**, offered on the Wednesday at the pit and refused in eleven seconds: a body is a return, not a source — he would burn himself out to move nothing. **He lets the refusal stand**, does not go round her, and enters it at the eighth hour with the lateness declared in the entry; Tova initials it and reads it aloud, which is Anneke's habit and not hers, and does not look at him. The ground goes: the banks slump, the ditch he dug becomes a crack, and **the only thing nobody else in the yard has to look at is the one thing he cannot enter anywhere.** **Change:** the outer field is paid for and three acres of his own ground are gone. **Cost:** three seasons of his own work, uninsurable and unrecorded.

**30. A rumor With A Witness In It** (day 30, Monday, the well house bench, the gate, the field, ~3,740 words)
Ilsa Kern will not have it in the seed house; it is done at the well house bench because that room has one door. **Sera states the proof rule.** A reading is not a fact; a fact is a thing a stranger can check without believing anybody; **an unlicensed reading is a rumor with a witness in it** — and the Ch 3 and Ch 20 readings are exactly that, and hers is a witness who will not testify. The cure is two things and there are only two: **a second licensed reader** on the same node, through her own anchor, on her own date, without being told what the first read — and the Assembly's roll for the district has two names, so the man is Idris Vane, and he is available, and **he cannot read inside the fourteen-day notice, so there is no second reader in this month, not because they cannot pay for one, because the month does not have one in it** — and **dated records that a clerk can take away and hold.** Dunmar prices the second half: the log is a holder's book and there is no holder, the open book has never been a filing, and the schedule is behind his own confession. Anneke Roos reports that **in twenty-six days not one person has come to read the open book**, because a notice about four wells is more interesting than a cooperative's account of its own mistakes. **Marek's decision:** the cooperative enters, in its own name, under its own hour, and sends to Fennmere with the form, **the statement that its whole account of the cause of the damage is a rumor with a witness in it** — not because it will help, because a thing that is entered can be denied but not lost. Sera makes three corrections, refuses for a fourth time — by a different pair of names, and she does not give it a number — the rule she made for herself nineteen years ago in a different office, that a book does not get to be a reason. **Her arm is checked a fourth time: nothing.** She says the licensed reading is hers and she will be examined about it first and she will not have him say so anywhere. **Final image:** the four willow sets are alive, nineteen acres are standing with white heads and no sheep, three acres are the color of a wet road, and he does not put his hand on the anchor, because he is the engineer of record and there is nobody licensed standing next to him. **Change:** the rule is on the page and cannot be satisfied this month. **Cost:** the cooperative enters its own unprovability and signs it.

## Volume 01, Batch 0004 (days 31–40), files in `workspace/volume-01/batch-0004/`

**Movement 4, *Water Above the Embankment*. Reviewed and repaired in place** — see `reviews/batch-0004.findings.md` and `state/continuity.md` under *The Batch 0004 review pass*. No scene was replaced and no planned plot changed. **The batch's dating rule, corrected by that review: months run thirty days, season days 1–30 are October, and day 31 is the first of the next month.** A day in 1–20 is dated by its plain number or *of last month*; **a day in 21–30 is the plain number only, never the first … the tenth of a month** — an earlier version of this file said otherwise and was wrong. No month is named in any document. **No Fieldbook panel was used; the batch's allowance is unspent and one remains in the volume.**

**31. Nothing To Put In** (day 31, Tuesday, the first of a calendar month, the pit and the well house, ~3,145 words)
Gael Senn keeps the second monthly examination, and it is the first one in the volume with **no licensed engineer in the fen** — the licensed person present column says *none* and she says so out loud before she starts. She goes down herself with both rules, measures the weep at the six o'clock corner — **a quarter of an inch, pump on the idle, measured twice, two rules, no change since the thirtieth of last month, not a leak at the load stated** — and insists the load be written under the figure, because a quarter inch at an idle and a quarter inch at eleven hundred turns are not the same sentence. **The beat is logged a seventh time** (eight in ten minutes, one run of five), with *cause not established* and no name. The examination entry reads **nothing found**. The real problem is the certificate's return, which is two things and not one: an examination, **and something put into the return main** — and there is no compost, there are four pounds five in the box, and four pounds five at four shillings is twenty-one hundredweight and a quarter, which is a twentieth of one month's return on a node at eleven hundred turns — which is not nothing, and she asks for that to be written as plainly as the hundredweight was. She invents the second half of the rule: **nothing found is what you find at the wound; the return line records a thing that did not happen, and nobody can check it, and a book that can record a thing that did not happen is worth more than a book that cannot.** Anneke Roos makes Marek write the difference down in his own hand. **Rowan Teale's boy brings 181 and a method**: no water in the race at the second hour, the wheel turned by the miller's own arm for a count of ninety — *a number without what I did to make it is a thing somebody will use against me in a year*. Dunmar enters it as a thing reported. Marek's decision: **he will walk eleven miles and give Idris Vane the notice in person**, because a notice served by post is a notice a man can argue with, and both answers are in Vane's own mouth. He walks the long way round, past four miles of the low quarter's turn-off, and enters why. The three acres are the color of a wet road and the crust has not moved. **Change:** the cure's clock is started. **Cost:** an honest entry in a public book saying the body cannot do what it promised, and two days of work.

**32. The Name Of The President** (day 32, Wednesday, the gate and the yard, ~2,290 words)
An Assembly circular comes up the fen road in a brown envelope addressed to *the licensed engineer of record*, and that woman left on Monday. Perrin Osse's note says four hundred went out to every address in a district water record and **Lantern Reach is on the list because of the report of the twenty-fourth and she did not add it**. Dunmar Fitch reads it twice from the loading bench in front of the yard: three requirements, a clause turning a licensed engineer's unfed graft into **a question for the Assembly and not a question for the engineer alone**, a clause inviting reports of unlicensed grafting **to the district office and not to the Assembly directly**, and **H. Pike, President**, with an embossed stamp, form A/4, dated the twenty-ninth. Ilsa Kern says it is correct and it is a ditch. Anneke Roos says the sentence is hers, from the fourteenth, and has never once had a letter about it. **Tova Reed finds the tell: the circular says nothing about the ditch and three lines about the engineer.** The subject is never asked about by any member in four days and that is entered. Lissa Vale asks the real question: the cooperative **has no address** — the charter paid for a box at the Fennmere post and the charter is gone — so it cannot answer as a body. Marek decides to send **a page, unsigned, from the seed house, with the three hundred and ninety-nine hundredweight and the license sheet and the hour on it**, on the same reasoning as the thirtieth's statement. The circular goes on the well house door, which now carries three documents that are none of them the cooperative's. **Change:** the professional body's claim on Sera Quill is on four hundred doors. **Cost:** the shortfall is now on a second office's file, and the trust's honest arithmetic is the evidence.

**33. We Cut It First** (day 33, Thursday, the yard and the south embankment, ~2,929 words)
Perrin Osse rides out with the district's **hazard measure**: a controlled burn of **nine hundred yards of the south embankment windbreak** between the two old fence stakes, firebreaks cut to the mill race, a crew of nine, a licensed man in attendance, at the district's cost, **to be executed between the sixth and the twelfth day of this month on a day of the district's choosing**, entered on the node's record as an executed item — and the only price is **a consent, form EM/2, a page and a half that says six times in different words that the root line beneath the windbreak is not a line the body relies on and its severance is not a damage to the node.** They walk the whole nine hundred yards and find the low growth on the sheltered side: milfoil, sweet gale, a mat of fen sorrel — **Tova Reed's four years of seed ground and nine thousand people on the March road.** The crew splits and is not healed: **Ilsa Kern is for the burn** (a controlled burn is cheaper than a gale, and she has seen the other way once) and **Jonas Byre is for it** (the race has read nothing since the twenty-fourth); **Anneke Roos is against** (a second Crown-shaped document in one month, in a file that already holds Sera's shortfall); **Tova Reed is against** on the four years; **Gael Senn cannot say**, and says the real thing — a burnt windbreak is a thing that will be *found*, and she is the woman who writes *nothing found* on the first of every month. **Sera Quill refuses in the same sentence she used on the twenty-seventh and says so when told it is the same sentence.** Marek's decision: **he will not sign, and he will not enter a refusal, because a refusal is a document too** — so the offer goes back unanswered on the sixth. And the chapter's last beat, in the dark, past the third stake: **Ilsa Kern's own hundred and six feet of withy, entered in the working notes on the Sunday with the hour on it, read aloud by Anneke Roos** — the cooperative broke the fence's feet first, and the district read it out at a counter, and the burn is coming to a hedge they already cut. **Change:** a signature, not money, is the only currency left, and the crew is split. **Cost:** the first hundred feet of the damage is theirs and is in writing.

**34. Eleven Miles And Fourteen Days** (day 34, Friday, the fen road and a kitchen eleven miles off, ~2,660 words)
Marek walks eleven miles out and eleven back in a day with the notice written out by Lissa Vale in her seven-column form and dictated to her across the seed house table, witnesses L. Vale, D. Fitch, A. Roos, G. Senn, **Out. Back.** at the end. **Idris Vane**: two rooms and a leano, a row of pegs with two things still on them, a kettle on before he arrives. He has **a fieldbook of his own on the table** and neither man offers nor asks, and that is the whole conversation about it. He is not going to say no, and he gives his reasons first: *I am not being paid to help you; if I read that node and it says below, I have made your case worse by one.* His **three questions**: **who pays for the compost** (the honest answer is nothing and there is £4 5); **what is the notice for** — the charter, not the hearing, and it is the wrong week; and **what did Ms Quill read**, which Marek tells him, and which is sealed in an envelope under Lissa's name and may not be opened before he has read the node. His four conditions: fourteen days' notice, in person; not to be told what to find; **his figures unedited, in his own hand, to the district and to the cooperative, both or neither** — which hands the district a second licensed reading before the form is in; and **fourteen pounds ten**, not moved in four years, one day, and he does not do it in one day. The echo is said aloud in a stranger's kitchen: fourteen pounds ten, sixty pounds ten, four pounds five. He reads the form for nothing and makes one change that is the chapter's only gain: **cross out *amount disputed*** and write *the cooperative does not dispute the amount* in Marek's own hand, so the amount is given up to keep the cause. **Change:** the notice is served, which costs them the hearing and may buy the charter. **Cost:** they have traded away the only thing they can still trade, and the cure now has a date after the water.

**35. A Courier With No Seat** (day 35, Saturday, the gate and the seed house table, ~3,082 words)
**Mear Sallow** walks nine days from the Assemtry, a settlement with no charter, no seat and no name on any roll, and asks for a night before she is talked to. Lissa Vale feeds her and **enters two loaves in the annex book with the hour**, because the rules are the rules. She puts the district's own hazard-measure form on the table and refuses **first**: a consent is an answer, an answer has to be given where it is heard, and there is no seat at the Fennmere hearing for a settlement that has never asked for one — *so we cannot consent and we cannot refuse and we are not going to pretend to either, and the measure is void as against us.* Then the fact she walked nine days for: **the long line runs under about two hundred yards of the windbreak and comes up at the toe in nine places** and runs north-west to the weir and on into ground that is theirs; **the fence's feet are the willow, the long line is under it, and a fire takes both and a long line cannot be replanted.** Then the refusal of the record: her name, the settlement's name, and the two hundred yards go in nobody's book, and the reason is structural, not mysterious — *a name in a book is a thing a clerk can find, and the day it is findable we have lost the only thing that is ours.* **Marek asks for one word** — *the settlement holds no seat*, five words, checkable, about a hearing and not about a line — **and she refuses him that too**, because the shape of her refusal is that a settlement of ninety people has no seat, and a record of that is a record of a settlement. Cost named: in eleven days he will write *not established* in a box while knowing what is under the toe of the windbreak and not being able to say so. He enters **the fact of a refusal and not its terms**, plus Anneke Roos's sentence under it: *this entry records the fact of a refusal and not its terms, and the terms are not held by this body.* At the gate she says the only reading she will give: *you will not sign. That is not a reading of you. That is a reading of the form.* **Change:** the burn is now known to be worse than advertised and un-consentable. **Cost:** an unrecordable fact, and even the shape of the refusal is forbidden.

**36. They Were Going To The Low Quarter** (day 36, Sunday, the peat road and the yard, ~3,014 words)
**Ivo Tarrant**, a peat cutter, comes up the peat road with a whetstone and no cart, and says in the first two sentences that he is not from the Undercommons because there is no such a place, and that **he has read the cooperative's book because it was read aloud at a counter**. Four conditions for digging the bypass in three nights: **it is a return, not a favor**, and their entry goes in their book and the cooperative's log must go entire into theirs, once, and the two books must say the same thing; **they keep it**, and four men are answerable for the ditch in three years; **it is for the low quarter, not for Lantern Reach**; and **you cannot get it back** — there is no office, no man to write to, nobody who can be told the arrangement is irregular, the undertaking runs one way, and the only way it ends is if all four of them stop, and they will not. The refusal is four correct reasons plus Marek's fifth: **it is the third exchange requirement** — four men's labor is a return for their work and not for this node, and writing it into the log as though it went into the node is what cost the cooperative its charter once already. **Sera Quill gives the same sentence as the twenty-seventh and says yes when told it is the same sentence.** Gael Senn asks that her refusal be put in the terms the others have, because otherwise it will be about her.
