Tarrant does not argue, gives the date — **the ninth, at night, the low quarter is on the way and there is nowhere else to come up** — and one practical thing: **there is a dog in that quarter, an old collie with one ear off, standing in the dead channel, and if the ditch puts water back in that bed the dog will not be there in three months, and nobody knows what it will be doing instead.** Entered in the standing log as a thing reported at the gate, licensed person present *none*, nothing written underneath. **Change:** the bypass is unbuilt for a reason nobody in the yard can argue with. **Cost:** four men, a barrow of tools and three nights were available and were not taken.

**37. A True Copy** (day 37, Monday, the Fennmere sub-office counter, ~3,016 words)
Perrin Osse clears the counter and states the terms: the district will copy the standing pressure log entire, in her hand, **at no fee**, and the copy goes into the file and **cannot be recalled by anybody who is not an officer of this office**; and she **will not certify the cause**. Dunmar Fitch states, and has entered, that the claim in the log was moved **on the fourth of last month** and **lost 9–22 with four abstentions**, that the open book is a standing act of a meeting and not a filing, and that a clerk of an applicant is not a witness. Then the afternoon of hand-copying, page by page, read back aloud by Lissa Vale, and the two entries that matter: the thirtieth of last month's eleven-hundred-word statement, and **the twenty-fourth of last month's three lines, which Perrin Osse copies into a district register in her own hand and reads out first, with the reason entered — a copy with three lines left out is a copy of something else.** The chapter's engine: the district will certify **three stamped faces** against item N-2214/K as a fact about its own completion record, without producing a page of the schedule — **on condition that the cooperative disclose its source, which is Marek's own section-nine margin note of the twenty-seventh morning**, which will sit on the same page as the inspector's sentence that the cooperative was two hundred yards from a Crown work party and did not say. **Dunmar Fitch detonates and then does not detonate *none known*:** at the counter, with a witness, he states the box, the hour, and the margin note, and asks Perrin Osse for the date of the sitting, and keeps it shut because **a clerk who files a false statement is a man who cannot testify to the four true documents in the same file**, and *he is protecting four documents.* At four o'clock Perrin certifies, in five words: **so far as it goes** — which means the log can never be completed again without a district officer in the room. **Change:** half the cure is bought. **Cost:** the cooperative's own note on the twenty-seventh is now the district's exhibit, and the clean record is now a certified thing that only grows in front of a clerk.

**38. The Line From The Tenth** (day 38, Tuesday, the Fennmere sub-office, ~3,690 words — the batch's one long chapter)
**Hester Carrow**, appointed hearing officer, is in the district for one day because three other districts have dates and Tarrow has a tray. Bray states the three things and says none of them is what he is here about. He does not use the emergency clause: he asks for the instrument to be produced into the district's custody for preservation under a **records power**, because the copy certified on Monday describes a fieldbook at the copper seam twice, is unrecallable, and is certified *so far as it goes* — **so a page could have gone in after the certificate and only he can initial one, and he cannot find out from the counter.** He says he would rather do it in front of the officer because an act the officer has seen is an act. **Lissa Vale refuses before she is asked**: the instrument is in her press under the ledger; moving it to a Fennmere file means a man takes it out in a cold room in the forty-second with no licensed engineer within eleven miles, *and that is what happened to my son on the tenth of a month.* **She wins on a lock, not on a speech**: the phrase *so far as it goes* means a page can never go into the certified copy without the officer in the room, and therefore **the book Bray actually wants is Lissa's use log, which is not in the copy and never can be without the officer.** She will produce the instrument **on a form** and the log **entire, copied, read back, minuted with the hour**, and she will read it. The officer rules that **nobody in the room may open the instrument, including her, and it binds the next officer too.** Lissa announces there is a line from the tenth before she starts, and reads the use log's first line: **out at the seventh hour and ten minutes, back at the eighth hour of the day — fifty minutes, in this building, no licensed person present, in the hands of a district inspector who is not a rootwright.** The officer asks who held it. *The district. Not a person. An office.* **And then Lissa enters against herself, before anybody looks for it: the first line of that book was made on the eleventh day of a month and describes the tenth, and the lateness is one day** — she has broken the cooperative's own inside-cover rule in the first line of its only clean book, and a clerk of a cooperative lost a charter over one hour on one entry, and she will not be surprised to be asked. **Anneke Roos names the parentage: the district put the instrument on a record on the tenth, and a woman in a seed house made a book on the eleventh, so the district's claim is the parent of the document.** The officer fixes the sitting at **the twenty-first of the month**, holds that the inspector's application will be heard on the cooperative's copy and not the original, and says the one thing nobody has said for three weeks: the application, the representations and the sixty pounds ten all fall due in the same week. Lissa says the officer will have to come and get it, that it will come on the twenty-first, that she has spent the afternoon teaching the officer what is in the log so that the reading of the tenth is the second time and not the first — and then names what she lost: for eight years the only account of that instrument was in a press, and at two o'clock it went on a form to a district file and is not coming back, and **she is not sorry and will not pretend to be.** Bray does not withdraw and says the truth: the cooperative's honesty made this possible and it is going to be used, and he would rather it were him than the officer. **Change:** the instrument survives, unopened and unused, and a sitting is dated. **Cost:** the clean record. **Lissa wins and the cooperative loses, and neither is a victory.**

**39. The Name On The Annex** (day 39, Wednesday, the seed house and the annex, ~2,915 words)
Four crates come up the fen road with the **Dryfoot Trust's winter-sown rye, four pounds, out of a counting house at Cray**, and **it is not the dry-land**: the dry-land is lost, three trays went into a coupling on the third of a month, and the March's last of it is still a tin in a press at risk. What is in the crates is the March's **wet-line reserve moved north four months early**, so the exchange runs both ways or it does not run. The annex is two sheets. Tova Reed's four terms: the trust's copy condition, the right of entry twice a year to be told the true answer, the trust's material stays the trust's — and **the Dryfoot Trust is a named party to the seed arrangements of the node at Lantern Reach**, which puts *Salt March* on a document attached to an unchartered body carrying a bill of sixty pounds ten. Lissa Vale refuses to sign until a soil iron is read: **the bed is at three and a half degrees, the bin has had sweepings in it since the first of last month, and there is no money for four hundred hundredweight a month** — and makes Tova write it on the annex **in her own hand, against the trust's own interest**, entered in the trust's book as well: *the seed described on this annex may not come up.* The signature takes most of the day. **Dunmar: Lantern Reach cannot sign, because a body with no charter cannot execute an instrument**, and he writes a limitation into the body of the annex. **Tova Reed makes him take it out and signs anyway**, for nine thousand people not in the room, knowing there is no remedy and no party to sue, *because a seed steward signing for people who are not in the room is not a fraud, it is the job.* Jonas Byre: *it is the first thing anybody has signed in six weeks that is worth anything.* Signed at four in the afternoon: Lissa Vale for the seed house, Marek Vale for the node, Dunmar Fitch as a witness and not a party. **The arithmetic of copies: three sets of the cooperative's records in the world, and the one going to Cray is the first complete copy that has ever left this fen on purpose** — which is half the cure, and the other half is a man of sixty-one at fourteen pounds ten. The tin stays in the press under the ledger, twenty days at risk, it having gone in on the nineteenth of last month, not moved, with Lissa's own undertaking under her signature. At the door Tova names the fourth unwritten thing: she will put the copy in the counting house and will not say whose hand it goes into, and nobody asks, and she is glad about that one. **Change:** the trust's name is on the record whether or not the crop lives. **Cost:** nine thousand people have signed for a comparison against a body that cannot be compelled, on a bed at three and a half degrees.

**40. A Front Out Of The West** (day 40, Thursday, the yard, the well house door, the gate, ~2,966 words)
Jonas Byre says it at breakfast: the glass going down since the second, the eels up the north ditch, a smell off the high ground to the west. **Rowan Teale's ninth: *No wheel. The sluice is shut and the race is out of water below the second sluice and there is nothing to chalk.*** Dunmar enters it as a thing reported and says a man who has chalked a figure every milling day for thirty-one years has decided the honest number is that he has got one. The district's **caution, water-supply bylaw second part, form C/2**: rain on the main drain, three days, **from the twelfth day of this month**, and a rise in the low ground of Fennmere on the second night. Perrin Osse's note is not in her hand: the form has two boxes and there is no holder, so it went in **no holder, affixed at the works** — *the correct thing has produced a warning nobody is obliged to obey, and that is not a scandal, that is a form.* **The crew is still split and the split has gone institutional: Ilsa Kern and Jonas Byre rode to Fennmere on the ninth, without leave, to ask whether a shorter burn on the east forty yards was available, and the answer is that a measure is varied by the district, by a new form, on a date nobody has.** Anneke demands Marek say in one sentence whether he objects; he says he has no standing to object with and that those two are not people he could have stopped. Dunmar enters it. The **fourth section-nine margin note** goes in on the same page, same hand: thirteen days, the bypass refused on five correct grounds, offered free on the sixth and refused on five correct grounds, **four men came up the lane at the first hour on the night of the ninth and were not asked in and went on down the fen road and nobody knows what they did**, the bypass will not be built before the water, the caution is on the door. Dunmar offers to change *this is worth nothing against a cut* to fit a flood and Marek says no; Dunmar enters his own refusal, and Lissa Vale initials it. **Gael Senn finds the certificate's real hole: the return on a wound is due on a date twenty-one days away and the rain is on the twelfth, and nothing in this fen says what a wound owes a river** — and refuses to write an entry in advance, because she has worked out that it is the same rule as *nothing found*. Marek's decision is the only cheap one left: **at the fourth hour tomorrow, Jonas, Gael and Ilsa read the reed gauge at the eleventh rod and write the load under it, because it went on the peg on the twenty-first of last month, Jonas read it that morning and wrote nothing down, the one entry that exists says that reading cannot be supplied, and there is no baseline in any book to read it against**; then all four on the embankment at the low corner at the first hour, with bars and boards, and not one of them in the pit. **Change:** a forecast, not a storm, and an obligation with a clock on it. **Cost:** the bypass is unbuilt, the burn is unagreed, the offer lapses unanswered, and two members of the body have left it to act on their own account.

## Volume 01, Batch 0005 (days 41–52), files in `workspace/volume-01/batch-0005/` — the last batch of the volume

**41. The First Figure On The Peg** (day 41, Friday, the eleventh rod at the fourth hour, the yard, the gate, ~2,890 words)
Jonas Byre, Gael Senn and Ilsa Kern go down to the eleventh rod at the fourth hour with one lamp between them, and Marek stands out of the light on purpose. **The first figure ever written for the reed gauge: three-sixteenths of an inch above the mark, pump on the idle, and the mark itself measured for the first time at nine and a quarter inches off the sill of the main.** The one entry that exists about the gauge says in its own words that the reading for that morning cannot be supplied, and it stands. **And then the chapter's hard fact: the log cannot be written in.** It was certified on the seventh *so far as it goes*, and a certified book can never have a page put into it again by anybody but a district officer in the room — so everything from the seventh onward goes in the cooperative's own book, which is not a filing, and Dunmar Fitch says so out loud in a room once. **The load stated has still not been applied, and the reason has changed shape: it is not that a licensed woman is eleven miles away, because one arrives at two o'clock that afternoon. The reason is that the load test was the twentieth day's act, on a certificate, with three names on it, and there is no charter to hang a second one on.** Marek says a number with no baseline and no load is not evidence and is not a claim, and it is a mark, and the next figure will mean something and this one does not. **Sera Quill comes up the fen road on the four o'clock cart, unasked, for the book and not the pit.** And in the afternoon **Ivo Tarrant comes up the lane with four men and a barrow of tools and turns them round at the gate**, because a cooperative which could not afford a ditch cannot take free labor off a peat road in a flood, and Anneke Roos writes the refusal down in a book that will still be here in a year. The burn's window shuts at midnight and nobody signs and nobody refuses. **Change:** a first figure, a licensed engineer in the fen, and the last free labor in Tarrow Fen refused for the right reason. **Cost:** the baseline does not exist and never will, and the four men are gone for good.

**42. What Fell Due On The Forty-Second** (day 42, Saturday, the twelfth, Fennmere, the yard, the seed house table, ~2,880 words)
The rain comes in at four in the morning and the mill race fills in nine hours. Dunmar and Marek take the form and the statement up the fen road and are wet through by the second gate. **Perrin Osse reads the top of the page first** — *the cooperative does not dispute the amount* — and then the consequence nobody planned: **there is nowhere on that form to say the amount has not been paid**, because the *amount disputed* box was struck out in a stranger's kitchen on the fourth. She writes it in the margin, reads it back, and says it will be in the register and will be read on the twenty-first. And then the structural last move of the season's politics: **a debt is enforceable against a body, and a body is a thing with a charter, and this body has had none since the eighteenth of a month, and there is nobody in the world you can serve sixty pounds ten shillings on.** The statement goes in entire, eleven hundred words, at the fourth hour, and Perrin says what it does in that room: *it does not help you; it is going to be the exhibit.* Back at the yard the new run is **full and not draining** and the four willow sets are under at the toe. The burn lapses at midnight by its own terms. And at nine o'clock the second levy is called, and **the Salt March Dryfoot Trust buys the second reader: fourteen pounds ten, out of the annex, in Tova Reed's own hand, against the trust** — over two refusals that are both correct (Anneke on a creditor in a document, Marek on the third exchange requirement), and the cost is named out loud: the March's first row of its own line is thinner by that sum. **Change:** the reading is possible and the money has a name on it that is not the fen's. **Cost:** a creditor, a sum against nine thousand people, and £60 10 entered and unpaid with nowhere in the world to serve it.

**43. It Is Not A Weep Anymore** (days 43–44, Sunday the thirteenth and Monday the fourteenth, the pit, the gate, the seed house, ~3,060 words)
Sera Quill goes into the pit with Marek at the seventh hour of the Monday, in daylight and not in a doorway, which is what she said on the eleventh she would do, and it takes her eleven minutes. **The wet place at the six o'clock corner is half an inch and a sixteenth, measured twice, with the pump on and continuous for nineteen hours — and Sera Quill says the sentence she has said three times in six weeks and will not say again: *it is not a weep, it is a leak.* Water is passing into the housing, the pit has four feet of iron water in it and is not up a quarter of an inch, and the housing is the only thing between this node and those four feet, and no licensed person in four counties can see inside it.** She states the second law — *a leak under sacking at full draw goes into the housing and not into the pit* — and that the reason is a casting of unknown date in a cooperative's own well, and the fen applied this load and nobody applied anything. Sera then describes the choice in two pieces of ground and refuses, twice, to help him find the sentence. **Corvin Slee walks nine miles**: nineteen inches standing in the bottom row of the low quarter, in six cellars, on the second night of the rain, and **not moving**, which is the part he came to tell them. **The second levy raises four pounds five — the same sum, twice, out of the same sixteen houses** — and the two who put nothing in last time put nothing in this time, the shilling and the three words under it are the same hand for the second time running, and the two who never signed never signed. **It is one delivery, not two: the twelfth's money is in the box and this one goes to compost.** **It goes to compost on the fourteenth, not the first: twenty-one hundredweight and a quarter, a twentieth of a month's return, worth about a tenth of a degree, and Gael Senn uses the second wording for the first time on a day that was not the first of a month** and does not sign it until morning. **Change:** the wound becomes a leak, the choice is set, and the return is paid a fortnight early at a twentieth of its size. **Cost:** the fen's own head is now on that seal, and nobody can take it off without stopping the pump.

**44. The Low Corner** (day 45, Tuesday, the fifteenth, Vane's kitchen eleven miles off, the yard, the seed house table, ~2,690 words)
Marek goes out with the fourteen pounds ten in his coat and walks the last two miles because the causeway below Weir Row is under. **Idris Vane takes the money and asks who pays, and says he will not do this for a creditor and he is not doing it for Marek either** — he is doing it for a woman who put a number on her own paper where it hurts her. He refuses her condition (the reading goes to the district first and the applicant second) and says so by the cart post on the sixteenth, because *a person who buys a second opinion and then decides what it was going to say has bought a signature*. **He says what the reading is worth before he has read anything.** And then, before anything else, **he raises his own broken condition himself**: on the fourth Marek told him what Ms Quill had read, which was the first of Vane's four conditions, and it has been broken eleven days and he has said nothing until now — *a man who takes money to read a node and has already been handed the other reader's finding is not a second reader, he is a second signature.* **So the envelope is not to be opened at all, by anybody, ever**, and he will write to Lissa Vale to say so and will answer a man in Auberron with the truth. The date is the eighteenth, which is the fourteenth day of a notice served in person on the fourth. **Back in the yard the water has come over the toe of the low corner at four in the afternoon and is standing, and not moving, four yards inside the bank.** Gael Senn takes the figure again and it has not moved. **Four facts go on the table in order**, read aloud before they are written: the corner is in the yard, the nineteen acres are the only cropping ground and the bed stands at three and a half degrees, the three acres are the only gravel on the place and take the water first, and the seed house holds the fieldbook, the use log, the March's tin, the open book and four crates of nine thousand people's rye. Tova Reed finds the sentence and rules out the vote, and **nobody says it in this chapter**: Dunmar Fitch rules the line, enters that no motion was moved and no division taken, and refuses to enter the sentence because a book cannot hold a thing before the man who owns it has said it out loud in the yard where it is going to happen. At midnight Marek stands on the bank with a lamp and does not go down into it. **Change:** the choice is set, and held back in the one chapter where saying it would have made it the body's. **Cost:** a second reader's independence is gone and cannot be got back.

**45. The Same Hole, Made Bigger** (day 46, Wednesday, the sixteenth, the low corner, the top of the field, the old channel at Weir Row, the seed house, ~3,250 words)
The crest comes up on the corner at six in the morning and Ilsa Kern is on it at ten past five. **Marek says his six words and then gives them a method.** The corner is opened at the toe by hand: **nine feet long, four feet wide, four feet deep, cut through the toe and down through the nine inches of chalk that Ilsa put there in the year of the great frost and never wrote down.** The **fifth section-nine margin note** goes in, closing with the same sentence, and under it four lines in the same hand that have no precedent in the book: *I am flooding three acres that are mine and that cannot be entered in this book as a loss to this body.* **Anneke Roos reads it all back and initialled the first five lines and refuses to initial the last four**, because initialling an opinion about a man's ground would let a stranger in six weeks say the clerk and the witness stood behind it. **The gravel takes it** — three acres going brown and then dark in sheets in about three hours — and by four o'clock the four hundred and ten yards of ditch are running the right way, downhill, to the crack, to the old channel, to Weir Row. **And at seven in the evening the trouble comes as a number, and it is not the corner:** the corner is taking a third of what is coming at it, the field is clay in the middle, and by morning there will be four inches of moving water between the corner and the well house. Marek's own diagnosis is that **the old channel to Weir Row has gone quiet behind a season of silt, a fortnight of rain and a drowned alder, and the water is standing in the top of the field again** — and he has spent six hours watching a ditch fill and calling it the ditch. So **he proposes no new work at all and says he will not ask for a sixth refusal**: the same hole made bigger, nine feet to fourteen and four foot to four foot six with the lip off the inside of the toe, and **a hundred and forty yards of old ditch and a culvert rodded out with a hook and a bar by five people between the ninth hour and the fourth hour.** And he says, in front of everybody, that **it is not the bypass and that the bypass will be unbuilt on Monday morning, and that a bigger hole in a bank he has already cut is the same hand on the same bank in the same hour** — and that a man in a room in four years will be right about it and it will be the whole of what he finds. Jonas Byre will not go down four foot six and says so a third time and says what he *will* do: hold the channel end, count the minutes aloud, and write down whatever the hook brings up and how big. **Ilsa Kern says she was wrong about the west side of the windbreak, and that is the whole of the healing of the crew split.** They go out at the ninth hour of the evening. At the eleventh hour a mill boy of fifteen walks nine miles in the dark with a slate: **the wheel read four hundred and nineteen**, and nobody here is counting. At the second hour the channel runs again and the well house stays dry. **Change:** the corner is open, the outer strip is under water on purpose, the cost is named in a book, and the work done in the dark is this holding's own old work. **Cost:** the three acres, and a bigger hole in a bank, entered and unarguable.

**46. A Work Order** (days 47–48, Thursday the seventeenth and Friday the eighteenth, Weir Row, the low corner, the bench in the mud, ~4,340 words — the volume's long chapter)
At the fourth hour, at Weir Row, the night's work in the old channel has brought the crown of the branch up out of the silt and it lies across the old ditch like a spar, and the third stamped face of it is out of the water on one side and nine inches under it on the other. **Marek puts his hand on the cut face and gets four seconds, and they are dearer than four seconds, and the third thing in them is not a record of a cut but the order a man works in when he means to bring a thing about.** He goes down on the bank and is sick, and Sera Quill does not ask him what he got and says it is the correct state of affairs and will be for the rest of his life. At the seventh hour, on a bench in the mud, **the four records are laid out in the order they were made**: the cooperative's own log of the head, **Marek's own gauge — cheap reed, tin, a brass mark scratched into it, whose reed went under at the fourth hour of the night and which therefore has no second figure and proves nothing except that a man built one for four shillings** — the miller's chalk, four hundred and nineteen, brought up on a slate by a boy of fifteen, and the dated seed-house ledger, the oldest book in that yard. **Lissa Vale has the fieldbook** — out of the press at four in the morning, **the tin left on the shelf**, the hour written on the back of the use log and initialled by herself — **and she declares on the bench, in one answer and unasked, that she has broken her own written undertaking to produce it on the twenty-first and not before, entering the breach as the lateness in the same words she entered the lateness of the first line of the use log, and giving the reason in her own hand because the reason is hers: the water was over the fourth yard of that yard at four in the morning, the press is on the inside wall of the seed house, and she is the woman who keeps it.** **Bray objects as an officer, cannot put a rule behind it, says so, and steps into the mud with a wet notebook**, because to use the three things he would have to name a body last responsible and the caution in the district's own hand says there is none — and he says he will be asked in six weeks by a man in Auberron and will have to answer with the morning and not with a form. **She reads the use log entire in nine minutes — it is four lines, and the fourth is four o'clock this morning — and the first line, and the two hours on the first line again, and the lateness of the first line that she entered against herself on the eighth before anybody looked for it, and the lateness of her own undertaking, entered beside it.** Nobody opens the instrument except Sera Quill, who fits her own clip, tests the contact with a plain wire gauge and writes the reading down first. **And the fieldbook's three lines are spent, once, and they are the instrument's and not hers: *Cut the upper and leave the lower. Wait a season, and the ground will come back. Cut again, and put your name to it.*** She says before the lines are read that they are not the three of the twenty-fourth of last month, and forbids a second copy and any document that quotes them as a reason or as an instruction. **Her own finding is then entered separately, in her hand, under her name: *This was set, and not remembered. It is a pattern to keep to a season that has not come. And the keeping of it is the work, and nobody is coming.*** And then, plainly, for Volume 01's whole answer: **a node does not remember a season that has not happened; what is in the bead and in that cut face is a work order — a target pattern, and the word for a target pattern is not a prophecy, it is a specification. She has seen the structure of one before and has not wanted to say so for twenty-three years, and she will not say where, or what, or why, and those three go into the record as questions.** And she cannot be corroborated, because Marek is not licensed, and what he got is worth nothing in front of anybody. **Kellan Rusk comes at eleven with his crew and his log and does not come into the yard.** **Renn Tallow puts down a docket** — a tool kit drawn against item N-2214/K on the eighteenth of last month, no order date, no work description — and Dunmar Fitch says out loud that it is not the schedule and the schedule is still never produced. **And then the sum, in the mud:** the log says eleven hundred at the head, the ground takes seven hundred and twenty, the difference is three hundred and eighty, and **Kellan's own book says his side draw has never been above three hundred — eighty a day, a hundred and seventy-five thousand two hundred turns over six years, never once on the same line of the same page** — and the built main passes rather more than a third of what the specification would, **the board is nine miles off in Rowan Teale's own yard and the highest figure on it in thirty-one years was two hundred and sixty-one, last night's wheel read four hundred and nineteen and is higher than anything on it in thirty-one years**, and **the extra sixty came up by something that is not the return main and nobody in that yard knows what.** He asks Bray to enter all of it, including the eighty, because a thing in a man dies with him and takes a fen with it. And then **the name: an office, not a man. Schedule clerk, fen drains. The post has existed longer than Kellan; the man in it has changed four times; he does not come out. He signs the paper. The paper goes to a store. The store gives out the kit.** **Sera Quill heard it and said nothing**, and Lissa Vale entered that she said nothing and not why — *she has answered that question in writing three times this season and has got shorter every time and a seed house is not going to be the voice that asks her a fourth time* — and nobody asked her again. On the eighteenth, **Idris Vane's figures arrive in duplicate** — the same three lines, his own bead, his own clip, his own date, told nothing, **a line at the foot saying the applicant's copy was not shown to him first, and a line under that saying he was told what the first reader read before he read the node, with the date of the telling**. **Change:** arithmetic becomes proof, the wood is named a work order, and a shape with no man in it is in four books in four places. **Cost:** the cooperative's own eighty-turn discrepancy is now the district's exhibit, and Marek's account of the fourth hour is a rumor with a witness in it and the witness did not see it.

**47. A Docket Off A Nail** (days 49–50, Saturday the nineteenth and Sunday the twentieth, the three acres, the seed house, the sub-office, the low quarter, ~2,590 words)
The water goes off the three acres in a day and a night and what it leaves is not the crust, because the crust is under a foot of gray silt. **Five days saturated and the bar goes in eleven inches and the water in the hole has not moved.** *It will not crop until the third season after this one, and nobody on this holding can tell anybody which reason it was, and I am not going to write one.* **Renn Tallow comes up the fen road on foot with five lines in his own hand, dated the tenth day of last month** — the night of the storm, the compound kit, the line he asked to have recorded that nobody recorded — and **Lissa Vale enters it on the nineteenth with the lateness declared: thirty-nine days, and it is a shortcoming of this building and not of the man who made it, and it goes in the seed house book and not in the certified standing log, because that book cannot be added to without a district officer in the room.** She says she knows exactly who did not record it, and it was not him, and she will not be thanked. **Perrin Osse comes up on the twentieth and spends four hours doing the thing her five words cost: reconciling the certified copy from the original with the custodian standing there**, writing *added after certification* at the foot of each new page, and Lissa Vale reads every line back after her, which is the rule of the fourteenth of last month and which no district clerk in twenty-two years has been asked to submit to. **The last page is the return line of the fourteenth with the twenty-one hundredweight and a quarter and the wording for a thing that did not happen**, and Perrin Osse reads it twice and says *it is a better rule than ours and I have never seen one*. **And while she is writing, a collie comes.** An old black-and-white collie with the left ear off, up the fen road and into the gateway, which will not come in and does not go away, and Joss Pyle's grandmother's dog is nine miles off in the low quarter. **Gael Senn looks at it from the step for a minute, then writes in the cooperative's own book that she has decided to write nothing about the animal, with her name on the decision and not the animal's, and reads it back before anybody else has read it.** Nobody goes out to it and it goes back up the road. **The low quarter maps its own line** — four men chalking the height of the standing iron on every gatepost, door step and cellar wall on the Saturday and the Sunday, at their own hand and their own cost — and **Marek hears about it at the gate on the Sunday and does not go, and says in the book that nobody in this yard is going to walk it and that nothing is ever going to be written underneath the collie report.** **And the letter comes back.** It came into the seed house on the nineteenth, **by hand, sealed, unopened, brought by a person who went on up the road and did not stop and is not in this building, and nobody is going after her.** Anneke Roos enters the fact in her own hand with the hour — *this body built a list and sent a woman out with a sealed letter in her coat and did not ask her afterwards whether she had done it, because the answer was easier not to have* — and does not enter the reason, because she was not given it. Marek does not touch the letter and says one sentence about it, **and the sentence is about himself: he has not asked where it went or how long it was in a cart, and he is not going to.** **Nia Vale does not appear in this chapter or anywhere after the twentieth of last month.** **Change:** the ground is gone for good, the refusal that was never recorded is on the page at last, the certified copy catches up with the book, a collie comes to a gateway and is not written about, and the letter comes home. **Cost:** the letter is still not delivered and nobody knows why, and the March's tin is still in the press.

**48. The Terms** (day 51, Monday, the twenty-first, the Fennmere sub-office, ~3,180 words)
Six on the district side of the table and ten on the other, and the sixth chair is empty all morning. **Hester Carrow** comes in at half past nine, squares her bundle to the edge of the table, reads back what she has written and then the order, and says she wrote the order at four that morning and will not pretend in front of the district's own inspector to know how the day will run. **There are five things on the file and the fifth is Idris Vane's attested reading of the eighteenth, which reached the sub-office before the applicant's copy did**, and she says plainly that a room which has been told for five weeks that a thing nobody can check is worth nothing has just been handed a document somebody was paid fourteen pounds ten to disagree with, and that the two arguments are the same argument from opposite ends. She puts the schedule of damage third and not last, because a hearing that ends on a debt is a hearing that has decided the debt. **Bray's application is refused on the officer's own ruling of the eighth** — heard on the copy, the copy now reconciled, and a records power is not needed to take a thing that is being handed over in front of her — **and it is not withdrawn and stays on the file**, and so does the ruling that nobody in her room may open the instrument, binding the officer after her. **Lissa Vale produces the fieldbook on a form with the hour, the place, two witnesses and the page number, and the officer does not open it either.** The use log goes in entire, copied, read back, minuted in nine minutes; the first line and its two hours again, and the lateness again. And the officer records that **the applicant taught this office its own file on the eighth.** **Then she puts Ms Quill's document of the seventeenth and Mr Vane's of the eighteenth face up side by side and reads from both:** the same node, four days apart, both sets of instruments preserved and traveling, **and they do not agree, and she is not going to decide between two licensed readers on a Monday morning and the district will not be able to either without a form.** And she reads out, in the reader's own words because he put them there himself, **that on the fourth of this month, before he had read the node, he was told what the first reader had found, with the date of the telling, and that in his own judgment it makes his reading no better and no worse and he would rather have it in the file than out of it, and that the applicant's copy was not shown to him first.** *A room in which two men who were paid to be careful have both written down the thing that was done to their independence is a better room than one in which it was not done.* **Kellan Rusk gives evidence for an hour and ten minutes and gives it badly, and it is worth more than anything else said.** He gives the shape of a works order, the post, the man who does not come out, the docket, and the three stamped faces; he says he chose forty-one houses over a book every month for six years and **if it was for money he would not be able to tell you what it was for**; the Lantern Reach contract is gone and he does not appeal it. His private charter survives, monitored: a gauge **below the split** any party may read, an open log, a monthly return, a person may be sent, no variation without a form, and he asks for every word of it to be minuted. **The schedule of damage is not discharged, not remitted and not reduced** — the officer has no power over it, a debt is enforceable against a body, and there is nobody in the world you can serve sixty pounds ten on — and the sum is entered and not owed, and **the file stays open, the emergency clause stays live, and the Directorate reserves the right to reopen on any new report.** Then, at two o'clock, the **conditional charter**: four clauses and a fifth sheet, for the season, expiring at the end of the season without notice. **The node stays open and the seed stock is not taken. The standing pressure log and the monthly report of root changes are adopted as charter conditions at the applicant's request, and the district did not invent them.** The return main to specification and fed, with the 399 hundredweight shortfall carried forward and not discharged. The seed annex as a condition, the Dryfoot Trust a party, the copy-to-Cray term a condition. And on a **fifth sheet**, argued for nine minutes, **Gael Senn's second wording is put into district charter law**: where a thing required by the charter did not happen, the entry for that line records that it did not happen, in the same book, on the same page, in the same hand, with the hour. The first examination under it is the **first of next month, ten days away, and it may be under a foot of fen**, and Gael Senn says in the room that she will be there for it and has written nothing in advance, and the officer minutes that too. **Change:** a node, a standing log and a monthly obligation, all three at once, and a new obligation to hold a copy. **Cost:** the charter expires in a season, the file stays open, and the Directorate keeps the right to reopen it on any report at all.

**49. Leaning Sideways** (day 52, Tuesday, the twenty-second, the seed house door, the table, the north annex, the lamp, ~2,780 words)
The charter goes on the seed-house door over the district's copper inspection tag, which has been on that door since the eighteenth of last month and was never taken down, under a caution, and nobody takes any of it down. **Lissa Vale puts the fieldbook and the use log on the middle of the table and does not put them in the press** — the press stands open with the March's tin under the ledger, thirty-three days at risk — and says anybody may look and anybody may ask to have the log read to her, and that this is the fourth thing she has refused in six weeks and she did not number it, and Marek does not ask her about it. **The compost is settled last and is not settled: four hundred hundredweight a month at four shillings, twenty-one hundredweight and a quarter received on the fourteenth, one hundredweight on the twenty-first of last month, and a flock of a hundred and thirty head that was a machine for turning grass into nitrogen and is alive somewhere in this fen.** Tova Reed: *it is bought, the heap is thinner every month, I have written the number on the annex for the third time, and I want somebody in four years to be able to find out that this holding fed a graft at about a twentieth of what the graft was designed to be fed, and that nobody in this building ever said the graft was working.* **The March's copy of the annex goes into a public ledger anybody may read and copy, and Tova marks it herself**, and puts on the record that a hundredweight is worth about half a degree, that twenty-one hundredweight and a quarter is worth about a tenth of a degree, and that **a soil iron cannot read a tenth of a degree and she is not going to write a degree she cannot stand behind** — and that the March's line is still lost and the tin is still in that press. The first row of next season's line goes in on the twenty-second: **the Longneck, eleven trays, in a wet drill, up in four hours, leaning eleven degrees off the vertical along its whole length**, and Gael Senn measures it and enters it and **does not write the word *failed***, because a woman who writes *failed* in a public book about a row of seed in a wet drill is writing about herself. **And Marek enters the sixth section-nine margin note, the only one of the six that is not about a cut**: nobody will be able to see the starve from the top of the field, they will see it in the second week of next August, and by then it will be somebody else's log. **The lamp is still burning on a living bulb somebody has to feed tonight, and it was Lissa Vale, and she did not make a ceremony of it.** Nia's cart ruts are still in the gate road and they point out. Sera Quill goes down the road on the afternoon cart with her fee on an invoice and leaves the certificate on the table, and nobody in this fen has told her she was right. **Marek kept nothing** — no instrument, no private log, no private advantage — **and he can feel a pressure in a piece of wood now, and it is not reliable, and it cost him the ground under his feet and an hour and a stone at a weir, and it cannot be used in a room without a licensed woman standing next to him, and there is no mark on his arm and he has looked at it twice since the seventeenth and both times it was nothing.** **Change:** the volume is closed on a charter, a row of seed leaning sideways, and an obligation nobody wanted. **Cost:** the outer strip is gone, the graft is unfeedable, the dry-land line is lost, the file is open, and the only person who knows what the scarroot felt like is a man who cannot say so in a room.

## Volume 01 close pass — one line per chapter changed, 2026

Forty-one chapter files changed: thirty for the mechanical and structural repairs listed below, and **eleven more — Chapters 2, 3, 4, 8, 11, 15, 22, 23, 44, 45 and 46 — for the spelling pass alone.** **No summary above is invalidated: no plot beat, no locked number, no name and no Movement 5 date was altered.** The full record is in `state/continuity.md` under *The Volume 01 close pass* and in `reviews/volume-01.findings.md`.

- **12** — 4 closing quotation marks added.

- **20** — 3 closing marks; 3 day-clock violations repaired (the third day, the first, the twenty-seventh).

- **30** — 27 closing marks; **the closing field walk rewritten in its own words and re-dated to the Sunday crust**, removing six paragraphs cloned from Chapter 31; the unsupported "fourth time" and "nine weeks" dropped. Ch 30's date, the fourth arm check, the eleven-hundred-word statement and the form's fourteen days did not move.
- **31** — 4 closing marks.

## Volume 01 second audit of the close — one line per chapter changed

The review phase read `reviews/volume-01.findings.md` against the prose and returned six findings, all now applied, and three more were found while checking them. **Twenty chapter files changed. No summary above is invalidated, no plot beat moved, no number changed, and no Movement 5 date moved.** The record is in `state/continuity.md` under §8 of *The Volume 01 close pass* and in `reviews/volume-01.findings.md` §16.

- **1 to 10** — **1,886 curly quotation marks and apostrophes converted to straight marks**, a glyph-for-glyph pass with no word, number or date altered. The nested quotation in Ch 10 is now a straight single mark inside a straight double. **Ch 6 additionally has the compost figure made unambiguous: *two hundredweight* is now *two hundred hundredweight* in four places, because 200 × 4s is the £40 that is three fees and the old wording could be read as two hundredweight.**
- **20** — the first monthly report's date normalized from *the first of the month* to *the first of next month*, which is the form the volume uses for a day 31 that has not arrived.
- **21** — 1 day-clock violation repaired (the twenty-first, in the field log line); 2 day-31 references made *the first of next month*; *two hundred hundredweight* made unambiguous. **Three words out.**
- **23** — 3 day-clock violations repaired (the twenty-third, in a written entry, in a spoken instruction, and on the mill board). **Nine words out.**
- **25** — 2 day-clock violations repaired (the twenty-fifth in narration, the twenty-fourth day in the sub-office record); 1 day-31 reference made *the first of next month*. **Six words out.**
- **26** — **1 surplus quotation mark removed at the end of line 89**, so one speech now opens and closes once; 2 day-clock violations repaired (the sixteenth day, the twenty-sixth in the field record). **Six words out.**
- **27** — 1 day-clock violation repaired (the morning of the twenty-seventh). **Three words out.**
- **28** — 1 day-clock violation repaired (the twenty-first to the twenty-eighth, in the district schedule). **Three words out.**
- **30** — 2 day-clock violations repaired (the twenty-fourth in Sera Quill's own account of her reading). **Six words out.**
- **35** — **the opening mark of the form's own wording restored** on line 21, so the third quotation in that paragraph has both ends. The closing mark stays where it was.
- **9, 10, 16 and 26** — the weir girl of twenty-three is **a woman** in all of them, which is what Chapter 47 already said and what `state/continuity.md` records as applied. The three survivors of *girl* in the volume are other characters speaking about their own childhood and are correct.
- **37, 38, 41, 44 and 47** — **a five-word phrase is no longer called six words.** *So far as it goes* is five words, Ch 38 already said so, and these five chapters said six. Chapter 38's two instances are now consistent with each other.
- **47** — **the letter's date made right in the prose, which the first pass had recorded and not done**: the entry now reads *written on the nineteenth day of last month and it went out with the consignment on the twentieth*. It still comes back sealed and unopened on the nineteenth, and nobody in the fen writes down why it was not delivered. **Ten words in.**

---

# VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0001, CHAPTERS 50–59, MOVEMENT 1: WHAT THE CHARTER IS WORTH

**Ten chapters, season days 53–64, 31,337 words after the review repair pass (was 29,747; the increase is the corrected Chapter 53 register page, the Chapter 59 panel payoff, and two new scenes in Ch 54 and Ch 55). Volume 02 opens.** Days 1–30 are October and day 31 is the first of the next month, so days 53–60 are the twenty-third to the thirtieth of the month after October and days 61–64 are the first to the fourth of the month after that. **October is two months back throughout and is named nowhere in this batch; an October day is the bare number only.** The weekday chain runs from day 52's locked Tuesday. Full clock, the day-to-chapter map as used and its four departures, every number, the locks and the stage accounting are in `state/continuity.md` under *VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0001, CHAPTERS 50–59, MOVEMENT 1*.

**50. The Seventh Note** (day 53, the twenty-third, Wednesday, the seed-house table, the three acres, the north ditch, the well house, the gate, ~2,870)
The charter is two days old and clause two is already working on the cooperative. **The standing pressure log and the monthly report are charter conditions, which means the log is now evidence and the report is now due, and it took Dunmar Fitch an hour to see which way that cuts and Anneke Roos one sentence to say it: a book anybody may copy is a book anybody may use.** She has been trying to say it since the fourteenth; the room could not hear it because everybody was pleased that anybody at all could read them. **On the fourth of a month this body voted, thirty-five hands in a room of forty, to open its own book — a private act of the meeting, deliberately not a filing — and seventeen days later, on the twenty-first, a hearing officer adopted it as a condition at the body's own request.** Nobody in the yard had thought about what they had done. **They also think about taking it back, and cannot: a body cannot consent to the removal of its own record, because the removal is the consent**, and the mechanism put in the charter to protect the book from the district now protects it from them. Marek walks out onto the three acres — sour, and the ditch a crack with soft edges, and it takes the boot and does not hold the weight — and does the sum he has been not doing for nine days, and is told the honest figure twice: **the return main is a little over half of specification by the length and, by the bore, a quarter, and he has never written the second one down because it is not a number a man can put a rule to**: **the leak cannot be mended under sacking at load, the only way to take the load off is to stop the pump, and stopping the pump stops the bed.** Ilsa Kern makes him write the leak and not the arithmetic. **The seventh section-nine margin note**, dated the twenty-third at the seventh hour, closing with the same sentence, and **the second of the seven that is not about a cut, because the sixth was the compost, and the first of the seven that is a measurement, and the page says why the other five are about cuts: a cut is a thing that can be refused and a measurement is a thing that can only be answered with money.** They write the demand for the works-schedule extract in the open book with the hour, plus a rider asking the district to fix the date clause three leaves open and to say in fixing it what the specification is — **and the point is made out loud: a clause that leaves a date to somebody else is a clause that makes that somebody answerable to the district.** Anneke initialled three lines and refused two. **The answer comes on the twenty-seventh, four days later, which is faster than four days is.**

**51. Nobody Moves** (day 54, the twenty-fourth, Thursday, the seed-house table, the low corner, ~2,940)
The first of next month is seven days off and the return line is for something that has to be put in the return main, and there is nothing. **Four hundred hundredweight a month at four shillings is eighty pounds; the standing shortfall is three hundred and ninety-nine hundredweight, seventy-nine pounds sixteen.** The levy goes up on the same paper in the same hand **and nobody moves.** Four of the sixteen are out, two are in, the rest say nothing; the paper comes back with fourteen signatures and **four pounds five, the same sum and the same roster as ten days before, and nobody in the yard can stand at a table and explain the difference.** The paper is read out: two put nothing in and put nothing in again, the woman with the shilling wrote *and I am sorry* again in the same three words, two signed first this time, and **two did not sign and their names are not recorded because the paper forbade it and Lissa Vale wrote the forbidding there herself.** *No motion was moved* goes in the entry. **And the fact nobody had ever written in one line: sixteen houses on the levy, forty-one houses on the bill, and no form on which the two are written together.** Tova Reed's arithmetic is correct and is not a proposal, so it is refused by nobody; she then **refuses the Crown seed-route map, in her own hand and not the trust's**, and gives the reason: **a copy of the March's annex is in a public ledger nine hundred miles away and she has been reading her own document all morning, and the term she is relying on is **the annex's fourth term and not a fifth one, because the annex has four things in it and this is the fourth.** She keeps the map in a locked case with the key on her belt and will look at it every week of the season. At the seventh hour Marek measures the corner — **twelve days on from the fourteenth** — and **Jonas Byre makes him say a number instead of holding the wound**, and the number is the same, and the pit has four feet of iron water in it and is not up a quarter of an inch, and it goes in the book in those words because the other way round sounds like good news.

**52. Sixty Pounds** (day 55, the twenty-fifth, Friday, the gate, the seed-house table, ~2,660)
**Ruben Ashe**, forty-eight, a charter broker of Fennmere, on foot with a satchel and a good coat, and he says in the first four minutes what he is, unasked. He has bought thirty-one expiring conditional charters in nine years, held each through its term and sold the conditions on; **eleven holdings have gone to nobody and he bought four of them and does not say he saved any.** **Sixty pounds for the charter, forty pounds a year for the compost, and he will pay the schedule of damage.** His argument is correct and he makes it correctly: a charter that cannot be renewed is not a holding, it is paper with a date on it, and the end of the season was in the document before they signed it. **Four refusals for four correct reasons:** Lissa Vale on what compost is for; Tova Reed on *a buyer owns a thing that has been in the ground and cut*; Gael Senn on sixteen houses raising four pounds five on a levy while forty pounds a year is three times a season's whole levy and every quarter of it is more than one of them; and Marek on the number — **the broker's price is the same sixty pounds the district entered for water four wells in Fennmere did not get, and neither number is worth anything to him, and he says so, and says the water stopping is not on him, it is on the end of the season, and the document was on the door before he signed it and he has had it three days.** Dunmar enters *refused* and then, on his own initiative, the line nobody suggested: *the offer was named as made again at the end of the season, which is the expiry of the charter this body holds.* **And the fact underneath the whole chapter, which Lissa Vale surfaces at the eighth hour: the broker read every book in the holding — the log, the use log, the return line, the seven section-nine notes, the entry about the well and the rider that went to Fennmere on the twenty-third — correctly, with his thumb on the line he was on, and he is the first man outside the building to do it, and that is a consequence of the second day of a month.** He also says the thing the room has been avoiding, in his own trade's words and out of the annex: **the return main is half of specification by the length and a quarter by the bore, and the annex's fourth term says in terms that the seed described on it may not come up, and that term is a line in a public ledger in Cray that a man with capital could be read to this afternoon.** The long line is not in this chapter, is not named, and is not in the annex. He asks about the instrument on the table, does not ask to open it, and says it is the only table in the district where a man can find out what is being done to a node.

**53. What The District Owes** (day 56, the twenty-sixth, Saturday, the seed-house table, ~3,790)
The extract comes by hand on the afternoon of the twenty-sixth from a girl of sixteen in a district coat who will not come past the gate. **Perrin Osse's note is the door and it is one paragraph: the extract was not refused on its merits at any time, the ground of the refusal was *a body holding no charter*, there is a charter, the ground is gone, and nothing has been decided.** Anneke Roos asks for that in those words, because in six months a man in a room will say the cooperative was lucky, and lucky is a thing that happens to people. The extract is the extract — six columns, ninety rows, the district's own certified copy, unrecallable, **and not one head printed over any of the six**, which is Volume 01's reading of the same sheet and still is the right one — and Dunmar reads all ninety rows aloud without touching it. Then the hinge of the volume: **there is a caption printed in the line of type at the top of the sheet, in the same type as the entries, in the same line as the form number and the place for a date, and it lists the columns in their order, and the sixth word on it is *Crown-held*.** The sixth column is the one this yard has been calling a third execution since the spring, and it is a letter in forty-one rows and a letter in thirty-one and a dash in the other eighteen, and **it was not hidden: it was a letter, in a column of figures, on a table a hundred people have read, and a dash in a column of dates looks exactly like a date that has not happened yet.** N-2214/K is Y and has been since a month in the year before he went in, and **there is no column anywhere on that sheet that says what Y does.** With it comes **one certified page of a district register of authorisations** for the same item — eleven rows, **eight columns, a head printed over the first six and nothing whatever over the last two, not a rule and not a word** — and the seventh and the eighth are the two the yard had to name itself, *authorised by* and *work description*, **and those two names are entered as ours and not as the district's.** **No column for what the cut was for, because a register is not a record of a reason, it is a record of an instruction.** The order date is the first of a month **in the year before he went in**, and the page gives the day and the month as two bare numbers under a head with no word on it; the executions are 10, 24 and 27, two of them the district's and one cut on this holding with a pair of shears; the hold was released on a Monday. **A hand is not a name, a name is not a man, and a man is not a decision.** And then the dash rows: eleven of them carry a hold and a dash, nine have a date under the hold and two do not, both of the two say **suspended** in print and neither has been released, and the eleventh of the eleven is a colony whose bigger holding failed in the table's fourth year, eight years ago, and which the district put into the Crown's hand and then printed the same way in both issues. **Anneke Roos's sentence is the volume's: a district that lies can be caught; a district that prints a category cannot be caught at anything, because the category is true and the category is all you are given.**

**54. Sunday** (day 57, the twenty-seventh, Sunday, the seed-house table, the low corner, the seed-house door, ~2,820)
Nothing can be done on a Sunday and nine people look at a page with a hand on it for nine hours and get nowhere. **Gael Senn finds the thing nobody asked her to find at the second hour** — the authorising entries in row six and row nine are the same hand as each other, and it is a quick even hand, and a clerk's hand is even because the next clerk has to read it — **and says she did not want it and would have gone without it, and that a hand is the job and not the man.** Marek has been reading one line for nine hours and says so out loud twice. **And the Sunday is a choice and not a structure: Marek wants a second form to Perrin Osse on Monday, on the reasoning that there is always another form and the only thing wrong with asking twice is that asking twice tells an office that asking works — and Dunmar refuses, because the first form was answered in four days and a second one would produce nothing at all, on purpose.** Sunday's second job is the corner: **five-eighths, twice measured, two rules, in daylight, pump on, half an inch and a sixteenth on the fourteenth, which is one sixteenth in thirteen days — and Marek refuses to write a rate, because he has two readings thirteen days apart and the two ends of them are different kinds of day, and the day before the fourteenth there were nineteen hours of flood on the head of the same node, and the last thing put in that return main was twenty-one hundredweight and a quarter.** The reason is on the page: *a rate is a number somebody can use, and if anybody wants a number here they may come and take the third reading at a stated hour on a stated load, and it will cost somebody a license, and I will help them ask for it.* And the Sunday post brings **form C/4, addressed to the Salt March Dryfoot Trust of Cray**, because the seed annex is condition four, the trust is a named party, and the annex's own fourth term is a charter condition. **The district's first demand for records goes to a counting house nine hundred miles away, and offers in the same sentence to send that counting house a copy of anything it produces and to send the copy to any other party to the annex.** Dunmar refuses to write *threat*; Anneke enters her own refusal with her own initials and says she is not withdrawing the asking.

**55. A Shilling A Line** (day 58, the twenty-eighth, Monday, the seed-house table, the gateway, ~2,760)
**Vesta Lund**, forty-four, of the Ashcombe index — a printed list of landraces bought by nine hundred smallholders who cannot afford to find out anything else — comes **two hundred miles in a cart for an afternoon** and says in the first minute that she is not a Crown officer and not a licensed rootwright and does not read rootwood. **A shilling a line for every line of the ledger that names a variety, and the March's cart will carry the fen's material north at her cost.** She does not want the seed. Her argument is correct: a landrace in one book in one fen is one flood from gone, and in eleven years she has been in a village where a bean became a price and then stopped being one, and she did not cause it and did not notice for two years. **Tova Reed refuses alone, in her own name and not the trust's, and gives the reason, and answers the one question Lund is actually there for: *what happens to one of the four.* A struck-out line is in an appendix in four counties, on a plate nobody has looked at in six years, and in one man's field, and when that man dies there is nothing left of it anybody can put a name to. **She has lost none in eleven years and has four she has never indexed and never told anybody about, including the woman sitting in front of her.** The March has been indexed twice, both times as a service. The first, eleven years ago, in her first year, when she was twenty and had been a steward four months, was a Crown seed officer with a clerk and a list who copied the trust's lines out one at a time and put no mark on any of them; **he copied the Ashcombe black oat into the wrong column and printed four hundred copies, so the most widely believed name in this district is a man in Auberron's handwriting.** The second, six years ago, took four named lines out of the March's own book in a dated crossing-out that is not any of theirs. **Lund's two categories she does not print, and her reason for not printing them, and the answer she gets for asking a fair question unfairly: *if a line is held by one person and you print it you have told nine hundred people where it is; if a line is held by nobody and you print it you have told nine hundred people it can be had.*** Lissa Vale enters *refused to say whether she approves* because Tova did not say. And Lund leaves a **Crown seed-route map, form S/4, nine years old, in five colors, for seed houses to check their varieties against** — and it does not show the Weir Row branch. Tova asks for a pencil and writes three words on the face of a Crown map, *no line here*, and no longer than that. Lund: *I did not know that branch was there. I have been reading maps like that one since I was twenty-one and I have never once looked at what is not on one.*

**56. Unmapped Root Line, Maintained In Schedule** (day 59, the twenty-ninth, Tuesday, the seed-house table, the gate, ~2,880)
Two maps on one table, **and neither is altered.** The Crown's S/4 and **the low quarter's own lime-and-stick survey, made on the nineteenth and twentieth of a month at the low quarter's own hand and at the low quarter's own cost, asked for on the twenty-seventh and sent up the fen road on the Sunday post, uncharged, with a line in it that is not a clerk's: *by four of us, at our own cost, for our own use, and anybody who wants it to be a survey can go and pay somebody.*** The branch is on one and not the other, and the reason is printed on the other: **the legend's ninth mark is *unmapped root line, maintained in schedule* — six words, and the count matters twice in this batch — it is used four times on the sheet, and one of the four is the Weir Row branch.** Marek's discovery is that the mark is not a warning but **a handover — a line with no local holder is maintained by the district, on a schedule, and is drawn differently on a public map so that anybody can see that it is not the holder's** — and he writes it in the margin of the Crown sheet, because a handover only visible to a person who already knows what a handover is is a handover and not a fact. **Nobody writes *concealment* and nobody proposes it: Dunmar Fitch refuses the word on the ground that it says somebody knew and did not tell, and Marek asks that the refusal and his own proposal go on the page together with his name against the proposal.** Dunmar's own new question goes in last: **the words are the same in both issues and nothing in them has changed and nothing in them is required to change.** And at the gate, heard by Ilsa Kern and entered because she made him repeat it: **a cut is worth nothing against a note, a note is worth nothing against a cut, and a line with no holder is worth nothing against a schedule — and the legend is printed, and the legend is right, and the legend is precedent.** He stands at the gate and counts his own notes out loud for the first time since Volume 01 closed: **seven under section nine, five of the seven about a cut, and every one of the seven closing with the same seven words, and the district has said the same thing in a legend and got it printed.** Tova's seventh line against the trust goes on the face of the map under the legend: *Nothing on this sheet about this ground. Nine years of it. And I have sold into it eleven years* — and under it, that in nine years one of them will need the second half and nobody will remember the first, **and the half that makes us feel better is the half that is more dangerous.**

**57. A Bead That Answers For The Seed** (day 60, the thirtieth, Wednesday, the gate, the seed-house table, the trestle, the north annex, ~3,560)
**Coll Renner**, forty-one, **the newest of the three names on the Rootwright Assembly's roll for the Tarrow district**, nine men and a yard at Fennmere, comes at the eleventh hour on other business and stays until dark. He has read everything the fen publishes, and what he wants is **not a secret but the order of the work** — a book that says what a crew does, in what order, on a holding they have never seen, with a foreman who does not want to be told he is doing it wrong — and he offers **two hundred a year and the method under his own license, which means the liability is his and not Marek's, and he does not want Marek's name on it.** Then he puts a **provenance bead** on the table: a copper-collared living bead cut out of a grafted seed, which laid against a wetted seed lot makes the lot report where it was grown **in four seconds, without a ledger and without a clerk.** It works four times out of four. Renner's sentence, said unprompted and flat, is the volume's mechanism: **a graft keeps the thing you put in and loses the thing that was there, and the losing is not a fault, it is what the word means.** **Tova Reed asks for one thing before she will look at it again: to see it work on a line she knows is wrong** — the Ashcombe black oat, eleven years in the March's book, arrived in an unlabelled tin through a man who wanted to be helpful, not the Ashcombe oat and not anything anybody can name. It says **Ashcombe, and the season before last, and the ground was under a ley.** *It is wrong. It is a better answer than eleven years of arguing, and it is a lie, and it is not a lie you told, and that is the part I want written down.* **The batch's one Fieldbook panel is spent here**: the instrument is read at the copper seam on a trestle in the yard, by a licensed man, with Lissa Vale's use-log line written and read back first and the contact tested with a plain wire gauge. Three lines, the instrument's and not a person's — *A bead will hold what is grafted into it. It will not hold what was there first. Put your name on which of the two you are keeping.* Nobody quotes them in a document. **The second demonstration is on a flowerpot of the March's rye at twenty past five, with Renner's clip, Renner's license, the use-log line, Gael Senn as witness and Dunmar reading it back, and Marek does the hands.** The graft takes. **Between the sixth hour and the seventh hour a rootmark comes up on the inside of his forearm — four inches, branching twice, not straight, not following a vein, the color of a healed burn.** Lissa Vale sees it first, says *leave it out*, and makes the licensed man look at it and write it down before he goes; he writes four lines and the hour in his own book and hands it to the clerk, and **there is nothing in it about what a man has become, which is not an oversight: it is the fourth rule this yard has made and the only one nobody has ever argued with.** Renner at the gate in the dark: *I am not pleased. I have had one since I was thirty-one and I know what it is for. That is not a favour. That is the bill.* And then, for four minutes, the last scene of the batch's morning: **he admits he came for the order of the work and spent the day getting a reading instead, and Marek tells him why he is being refused — a book of the order of the work is a list of things that have been done, and there are eleven fen holdings in this district that cannot afford a cooperative, the return main is a quarter of what the work needs, and the reason nothing is done in two of them is not that nobody knew the order, it is that nobody could pay for the compost** — and hands him the book to read standing in the dark. *That is a bad book, and it is the first honest one anybody in this district has ever put in front of me.*

**58. The Second Wording** (day 61, the first of the month, Thursday, the low corner, the seed-house table, the well house door, the north annex, ~3,715)
**The first monthly examination under the charter**, and the whole chapter is the second wording working in both directions at once. **The leak: five-eighths of an inch, twice measured, two rules, in daylight at the seventh hour, pump on — and she asks for it in the book that the second rule read the smaller figure, because it always does on that corner.** The pit has four feet of iron water in it and is not up a quarter of an inch, and she writes that in those words. **The ninth beat: eleven in the quarter hour, two runs of four and one of three, the last three together, felt at the rail and not at the coping stone, cause not established, licensed person present none, and nothing written underneath it by her decision and with her name on the decision and not the thing's** — *because a woman who writes seepage under a thing she has not seen writes about herself.* **The full-draw load test is not performed, for the first time in a season when it could have been, and Marek is the reason**: there is a charter to hang it on and there was none on the twentieth; **a licensed engineer crossed the yard at the eleventh hour on his own way to Fennmere, read the morning's number off the examiner's page without being asked and without touching anything, and will not be there tomorrow, and Marek did not send for anybody** — *he would rather have the figure and not have it; he wrote the book against himself so that it could be used, and he is not going to be the man who hands the district the one number that would help him, because he has learned that a number travels and does not come back.* **Gael Senn enters the test as a thing that did not happen, in district charter law, in a book the district may read, in her hand, under her name, and if the district prefers a figure she is sorry and there is no figure.** The season-end form arrives with the packet: **one box, two words, *is maintained*, no third answer**, and a consequential sentence that ends a charter, **and at the foot, in the same hand, as though it were a note, *term closes with the closing of the sowing*** — which is the last day of the month after next, fifty-nine days from that morning, with the charter itself expiring on the twenty-ninth of that month, fifty-eight days out, and no appeal anywhere in the four clauses. **They say it out loud in a yard in daylight**, because a feeling about an expiry is worth nothing and the paper is the only thing in the room that is a fact about the future, and the man of about seventy asks for his initial on the sentence in case anybody in a room in a year asks what he was told. The compost did not go in and the reason is entered, **the bed is at three and a half degrees, the March's tin is forty-two days at risk**, and Ilsa Kern stands over four live willow sets and the 399 hundredweight shortfall and says she has been right twice this season and has nothing else in her. **Marek's eighth section-nine note does not close with the sentence, for the first time in eight notes, and the reason is on the page: the eighth note is a record and not an argument.** The return line goes to the district with **nothing in the return main, none available to put in, and the load test that was not performed** — and Dunmar notes that *to the body: nothing* has now said the word nothing in a district-readable book twice, once in an office in Fennmere with his words in it and unsigned, and once here with a different clerk.

**59. The Column With A Hand In It** (days 62–64, the second to the fourth, the seed-house table, the gate, ~3,330)
**The reader is required out of the district water record and the body may not name her**, at the holder's cost, and putting the request on the Thursday post takes two paragraphs at the counting-house table in Cray, because **the annex's own fourth term is a charter condition and the district has now asked a third party for everything it has about us.** **Osvalde Bream**, seventy-one, thirty-one years of the crop histories of eleven holdings out of a bookshop, **never licensed for anything and says so in the first minute**, comes two hundred miles for a day's cart, takes no fee, does not bring the books. She sets two conditions and keeps both: **she reads hands and not names, a hand is not a man and a name is not a decision; she will say who wrote it and will not say why and will not say whether anybody made him do it, and if a room asks any of those three she will get up and go back down the road.** She is wrong on a Sunday about a column and right on a Thursday and honest about both. **She reads the seventh column for eleven minutes and says: it is a clerk's hand — except in the fifth entry, where the second half of the word *schedule* leans four degrees to the left of the first half and the tail of the *c* is doubled back, and that is a man writing fast, or in a hurry, or writing a thing he is putting into a book he does not want to have written — and then: it is Aldren Vale's hand, on order paper for a hundred and forty rods of twelve inch, on a day in the eleventh month, and he made the W in *wire* the way he made the W in *weir*, and she has never in thirty-one years seen another hand make a W that way.** **No month is named anywhere in this chapter, because the district's form does not name one, and the scene turns on that: Dunmar has been calling the date the first of a month for four days because there is nothing else to call it, and Bream tells him he will have to learn to count — *nine* is the order on the row and *eleven* is a day she has not forgotten, and she has said both as plain numbers for thirty-one years, and every office she has ever kept a book for has done the same and not one of them noticed it was doing it.** He had seed orders from her for three seasons and she has not had one since the week before the shaft, and she came for a bad page of a district's handwriting and was given a day's cart for a man she buried. **Two dates, one hand, twenty-seven months apart**: the register's order date seven months before the shaft, the fieldbook's figure a year and eight months after it, and *probably a copying error, to be treated as one* still standing as the only protection he has, untested. **And the batch's one panel is spent on the wrong question, and the batch's last chapter says so.** On the third, with the reader in the yard a full day, Osvalde Bream looks at four inches of rootmark and asks the obvious question: **somebody in this room has been carrying a thing that answers questions and has not once
