Tova Reed: I am a party to six of the terms and I would be a person who received a thing at a place and did not write it down. **Lissa Vale stays because she is the reader and the reader reads.** Ilsa Kern, Jonas Byre and the man of about fifty go, over the first break, and sleep at the bothy of a mender of implements who asks whether the second break has been walked and is told it has. **Wrenlow is eleven plots in a ring about two hundred yards across on the low dry, a well at the low end and in, two rooms, a fence of thorn and hazel, and there is no gate because there has never been anything at Wrenlow worth closing.** Wenna Roke, thirty-nine, is in the ninth plot with a hoe and puts it down. **Her list is one sheet of lining paper with a stone on it and eleven lines, one for each household, and what the household will sow and what it had last season, and there is no column for where any of it came from** — because eleven households have come to her in nine years with a sack out of a cart through a man who wanted to be helpful, and not one of them said where it came from, and she did not ask, because on the day she asks the sacks stop coming. She has kept a short line alive in all eleven plots for two winters, because a line in one plot dies with a bad month. Then the two refusals, entered as the fact of a refusal and not its terms, with the line in the engineer's hand: **she will not keep a copy of the record and will not answer on it, because she has never signed a paper in her life and if she signs that one she is in it, and if she is in it then the day she wants out there is no day. And she will not put anything in the seventh column, because everything coming back down this road goes to Cray, and a thing that goes to Cray does not stop here on the way — so I am the end of a road, and the road is for somebody else, and I am not going to be the first thing in a column to make a fen's door look right.** Her condition: **leave my name off it; whoever is standing here when it comes can put a mark on it.** They come back on the twenty-eighth and it is read out three times. **And the terms were sent to Wrenlow unasked for — four sheets, Tova Reed's own fault, entered as a fault and not as good practice — and Wrenlow returned them, and the two sentences the carrier was given are entered in his words: don't send me anything with my name at the top of it, and if you want to keep a thing going, come back with the thing and not with a paper.** Anneke Roos enters that she had been asked to check the paper and had not been. The terms are in the fen's book and in no other book in the world.

**84. The Expiry** (days 119–120, the twenty-ninth and thirtieth, Sat–Sun, the yard, the well house step, the gate, ~3,119)
**The batch's midpoint, and the charter does not die badly, which is the thing nobody in the yard has been able to say out loud for a season.** Every box that could be filled was filled by the person the instrument names, on the first of the month, in daylight, without a rehearsal, and the term closed with the closing of the sowing, on a date nobody chose, with no appeal anywhere in the four clauses. **Nine items are read as nine items because Lissa Vale would not have a hand go up against a paragraph, and then the clerk finds two things.** The use log was never on the instrument at all. And **the return main to specification is withdrawn from the motion, in Marek's words, because a specification is a document of the district's and this body has no charter and cannot undertake to one, and a promise the fen could not make is the one thing this book has never had in it** — so it is entered as a fact: twenty-two rods of eight inch, and nobody will ever come and look at it again, and that is now correct, and it is not a defeat. Item nine stays blank. **Seven hands, four for, three against. Marek Vale against, for the engineer's reason and not a caricature: the book was worth the trouble because somebody was going to make us produce it, and there is nobody going to make us produce it now, and he would rather be found out in a year than have it found out in five — and Lissa Vale reads it back without softening a word, and then votes to keep it, and there it is: the reader of the book, reading a man's vote against it aloud in a yard and putting her own hand up.** Jonas Byre against on the sixth item, and it is the best objection in the yard and it is against the trust: item six puts every entry this holding makes into another body's hands for eleven years with nothing behind it and no box at the far end that can be filled in. The man of about fifty against with the worst reason in the batch: a body that is proud of being honest has stopped asking the question, and how a body is known is not how it is honest, it is how big it is. **The man of about fifty says a sentence at the gate that is not in the book and never was: we are not on anything now, and there is nothing over there that says what they are, and so we can be with them without it being a thing that is on a form anywhere.** Tova Reed then says the professional version without knowing she has said the sentence of the whole road between two places: **a body with no charter can be a party to nothing, and that is what a charter is, and it is the only thing it is, and a settlement with no charter cannot be a party to anything either — so the two ends of this route are the two ends in this district that can be party to each other at all, and there is no form that says so and nobody is going to write one.** Ruben Ashe comes up on the date the fen's own book named on the twenty-fifth of a month two months back — sixty-four days, and not eleven weeks — and offers the same sixty pounds for eleven items and an undertaking to keep no book and answer no officer, and is honest: he is not offering to maintain anything, and in four years the district will be keeping that node by a Crown schedule and nobody will have this holding's name on anything, and the three candidates in this district are the one that owes sixty pounds ten, a private charterholder, and a body with eleven years of books anybody may read. Refused. At the gate: **I have never seen a road in this district anybody could copy.** The sixteenth note, and it is a record, and it says underneath that it is a record and not an argument and that the closing sentence is off it for that reason.

**85. No Holder** (days 121–122, the first and second, Mon–Tue, the well house, the pit, the gate, ~2,630)
**The first month boundary with no charter behind it, and a form nobody had to give anybody.** Gael Senn takes the examination at the seventh hour in the same words she has used since the first of a month three months back. The leak at five-eighths, twice, two rules, pump on, into the housing and not the pit. **The beat is the twelfth: nine in the quarter hour, one run of four, at the rail, cause not established, licensed person present none, and nothing underneath it, which is her decision and hers and not the thing's.** Return line: nothing put in, none available, 399 hundredweight, seventy-nine pounds sixteen, carried forward and not discharged, third month running. **And then she signs for herself, because there is no examiner's appointment and no form for an examination nobody is required to hold, and it is the first entry in this holding's book with a signature and nothing behind it at all.** The report goes up the cart at the ninth hour, nine lines, and Dunmar Fitch enters that it went because she wrote it and not because anybody asked her to. **And then the thing the whole of the morning was about and nobody said until the second: the examination at which the third condition is tested is the first examination held after the term ends; that was this morning; it was held; and it was not described.** The reason in that order: there was no officer, there is no form, there is nothing to appoint an examiner under, and there is no charter. **The fen does not write and ask, and enters the reason — a second form produces nothing on purpose, because a body that asks twice has told a district office that asking works — and Marek says he would rather it were a hole we knew about, and the clerk enters the knowing and not the four years, because a body that enters what it is afraid of has started keeping records for somebody else.** The district notice comes on the second, four lines, **the node is not shown to be maintained and the district may at any time make arrangements for its maintenance** — the same language as the eighteenth of a month four months back, **a hundred and four days on** and not one word of it changed, and nobody in the yard is a villain about it, and the man of about seventy says put it on the same nails as the schedule, on the left, where a man can find it without reading past the schedule. Tova Reed works out what the four lines are for and asks for it entered: the seed house has no water, the bed is at three and a half degrees, and the tin is a hundred and two days at risk. **The pump keeps running, nothing is sealed, and the fen did not use the expiry to stop measuring the leak.** Then two men with a chalk cart stop at the gate and one asks for the number of the readings of the return column, and Lissa Vale reads it to him, and he asks **has anybody come from the office to hear it** — **and the chapter's two loose intervals are now true: the head has stood as it stands since the first of last month, when the full-draw load test ended and it came down, and the carter has been going past the door for a fortnight, which is since the schedule was nailed on the seventeenth**, and the answer is no. Tova Reed's answer to why: **we are the only two ends of this route that can be party to each other, because we have nothing, and the whole of this arrangement stands on the fact that nothing about it can be enforced — that is not a foundation, it is the absence of a floor — but it is the reason she can walk away without a reason and without a notice, and it is why clause five stands.** Lissa Vale enters the decision to go on reading it out on the first, the middle and the last, **whether anybody comes or not**, as a decision and not a mood.

**86. The Return** (days 123–124, the third and fourth, Wed–Thu, the seed house table, the bay, the gate, ~2,865)
**What a return costs, and the one the fen actually has.** The trust's question is answered: yes, for a season, and the fen has been holding it since the ninth of a month four months back without being asked. **And the arithmetic gets worse first:** the bed is at three and a half degrees on a figure Tova Reed cannot defend and uses anyway; the compost line has read nothing put in, none available, for three months; 399 hundredweight short at seventy-nine pounds sixteen and the specification is a Crown document this body has no standing to promise. **She writes the word *gift* in her own book and strikes it out, and gives the reason: a bed this holding cannot feed, given to a body that has asked nothing, is a favour, and a favour goes one way, and eleven households kept a dying line for two years and were never asked what they got back, and she let that be the shape of the thing and is proposing to do it again in a bed.** The woman with the shilling: **you have got my children's seed in that bed, and I am not asking you to take it out, I am asking that it be in the book in my words and that nobody come at me afterwards and say that I did not raise it.** The man of about fifty is honest about not wanting it to be true, because a beautiful speech about a return is a man who has been given a good day, and adds that **everything coming back down this road goes to Cray, so whatever goes in the seventh column is not for her, and if the schedule is read at that door by somebody in a year the woman at the other end will know exactly what it is for.** Jonas Byre says the other half: a road with crates on it is not a bad road, it is a road, and a body that has spent a season finding that out ought to be the last body in the world to invent a return to make the road look finished. **And the answer is that a return does not have to be compost: what comes back down a path is whatever goes along it and comes back — not a kindness, not a gift, not a mood, a weight, at two ends, on a day, with a name against it.** The March's dry line was sown on this fen's ground before the sowing closed; when it is lifted in the autumn the seed off it is the March's line grown in this fen, and it goes back to Cray in a crate with a sheet on it, weighed and signed at two ends. **Lissa Vale enters it on the third time she has given her rule out loud: there is nothing in that a man with a rule cannot look at twice, and she is not going to add anything to it.** Both halves of the entry go in, **including the line that it goes to Cray and does not stop at the second end, and that the second end's column is empty and stays empty this season, and that a person there said the road is for somebody else and she was right.** The bay's two boards and the number fifty-eight. And Ottoline Fray writes again because she said she would: **a cutting sheet, her own, the first she has ever written and held for a long time without knowing what to do with it, which she had said as *eleven weeks* and which is now undated, because nothing in Chapters 50–88 says when she wrote it and a number would have been an invention, and she has put in the third column what she has been told the third column is for and she wants to know whether she has got it right, and she would rather be corrected in a book than corrected nowhere.** The fen does not answer it that day and enters that nobody answered it.

**87. The Convoy** (days 125–126, the fifth and sixth, Fri–Sat, the seed house table, the first break, the low dry, Wrenlow, ~2,626; **the chapter's last three paragraphs are the seventh (127) and Ch 88 opens on the same day without repeating them** — see *New locks* in `state/continuity.md`)
**The batch's climax, and it moves, and it does not go well.** Twelve crates written down crate by crate. One to six: the seed house's own bean. Seven to twelve: **the March's line, out of the tin, sown in this seed house before the sowing closed, the eleventh of eleven cuttings — and the keeper's own words are *the March's line*, and a line is not a lot, and Tova Reed refuses to correct it because the column is the keeper's words.** Weights by hand: about eleven pounds for the six beans, about twenty-nine for the six trays in soil. **The order is date order, ruled before anybody knew what would be on it, and the fen does not reorder it, and the reason is that a body which has spent a season refusing to choose for other people does not get to choose which line reaches a settlement because of the date on a list. Tova Reed says she would have reordered it, and nobody argued with her and nobody said she was wrong.** Two carts and two men, fourteen shillings a day, second cart paid for whether it goes or not; plank and chain at the crossing, two. **The whole of Saturday was sixteen shillings, the same sixteen as the Monday before, and this time something moved.** One loaded cart crosses the first break on a plank with a chain to a mule from the mill and two men on the front wheels, in about eleven minutes. **The second cart does not go and stands in the yard from the eighth hour to the seventh with its shafts down, and the man who stood beside it all day, sixty, thirty-one years at it, says: because you paid for me to be here, and if I go home I have not been here, and I would like to be a man you remember that way.** **The route line on the form ends at the low dry and does not go on to the March road, and the reason is the second break, and it is the first fact about a seed-route bridge anybody in this fen has written down.** Marek goes, and the clerk enters the rule he quoted and the reason it did not reach him: **he is not collecting a survey, he is taking six crates of this holding's own bean to a place it has never been and is going to stand in front of a woman and say what is in them, and part of what is in them is wrong, and he would rather she heard it from him than off a piece of paper.** **A certificate on lining paper with no printed head, forty-one words, in his own hand, read to her aloud and then said again without the paper. And Wenna Roke says: I cannot read it. Not the words. I have never been handed a form in my life.** He asks one question: the name you do not know — whose name is on the tin you are going to sow it out of? **Mine, and a printer's, and a man in an office in a county I have never been to, and none of them are the seed.** She asks him to take the name off the tin, not off the book, and says a thing in a sack with the true name on it is worth more to her than a sack with a good name on it that a woman nine miles away has spent eleven years not being able to check. **She keeps the paper she cannot read, with a stone on it, because a man came eleven miles with a thing he did not have to tell her and told her anyway, and now there will be two sheets and a stone on each.** The fen enters the first thing a settlement with no charter has ever been asked to keep, and enters the not-knowing as well. Copies go up on the seventh, one nailed to the second board at the north gate, one nailed to a post inside a thorn fence on the low dry, **unsigned, and it says eleven households, keeper not named.** Six crates in eleven plots, six in a barn on this fen under four inches of green. **And the man of about fifty, who voted against the book eight days ago, reads the entry twice and says: she has got a stone on a piece of paper she cannot read, and that is worth more than the six crates, and I would like the fen to be careful about how pleased it is.**

**88. Two Ends** (days 127–129, the seventh to the ninth, Sun–Mon–Tue, the yard, the seed house table, the well house door, ~2,598)
**The batch's close, and the thing that must not be said out loud.** Lissa Vale reads the whole schedule out on the seventh, which is not the first or the middle or the last, **because a man with a chalk cart asked her a question about a column five days ago and she is not going to let anybody in this district ask her the same question twice.** One person comes, a man of about thirty with a dog, who does not give a name, and the fen does not keep a record of people who do not give one, and the number goes up anyway. The closing entry is read in full. **The Crown's answer arrives on the ninth, four days over four weeks after the return of the fourteenth of last month, four paragraphs and a clerk's signature, and it is correct: the seed-route sheet is not amended on the statement of a holder and is revised on a cycle this office does not fix and cannot state; the district enters this holding as a local holder of an unmapped root line in the district's book and not in the Crown's map layer, and the applicant is not entitled to the layer and has not asked; a route carrying seed over ground the district carries in schedule is a use of that ground, a use is a class of use, classes of use carry terms, those terms are not settled by this office and will be sent in the ordinary course and will name a rate and a class of use; the schedule is received and the office has no power to approve it, no power to stop it, and no duty to send anybody to hear it read. And it asks for no fee and says nothing about whether a fee will be asked for.** Nobody is pleased and nobody is angry, **and the clerk enters that the second of those is the first time in a season a Crown act has been received without anybody in this yard wanting to be a villain about it.** Anneke Roos makes the room say what point three is. **Tova Reed says it: point three is the answer to a question I asked on the fourteenth of last month without knowing that I was asking it, and the answer is not a form, it is a class of use, and a class of use is a thing that can be taxed and refused and appealed, and it exists because I put a holder on a piece of paper and went up a road, and I could have known and I sent it anyway. **Her fear of a form is older than her question: eleven weeks is the morning the conditional charter went up on the seed-house door, day 52, which is seventy-seven days before the ninth and is the one place in this batch where the phrase *eleven weeks* is used and means one thing, and she says she wrote to the office with the fear already in the letter.** The seventeenth note, an argument, and it carries the closing sentence. **And the last entry names the whole of it in the trade's words with the measured figure under the declared one, and then says that nobody in that yard has ever said those four words as a name for what this holding is, a licensed man said them at a gate about a trade, the title of the form carries one of them because a form is a form, and the fen has never once used them of itself and is not going to, and that is not modesty — six crates is the whole of it, and anything larger would be a lie, and this holding has entered nine wrongs in eleven months and has not got room in the same book for a tenth.** Ends on the lamp, the well house door with a schedule on the right and a notice on the left, the bay and its two boards and fifty-eight and a number still going up, the standing compost line, the bed, the tin at a hundred and ten days, and **the two ends of it: a body with no charter and a settlement with no charter, and a book in the middle of it that both of them can read and neither of them has signed.**

---

# VOLUME 02 — BATCH 0005, CHAPTERS 89–98, MOVEMENT 5: THE TAX ON A BRIDGE

**Ten chapters, season days 130–149, 28,783 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings (28,863 before a 108-word trim to Ch 96; 28,788 after the eighteen-site verification pass recorded at the end of this section; 28,783 after the review pass that followed it). Movement 5. The batch runs from the tenth through the twenty-ninth of the month that began on day 121, with day 150 the thirtieth and the closing of the sowing falling after the batch. Weekday chain anchored on day 121 = the first = Monday, day 129 = the ninth = Tuesday. The March's tin is day minus nineteen throughout and stands at 130 days on the last day. No month is named anywhere and no document carries one. No Fieldbook panel is spent. Staging moved: Ch 92 drafted in the yard and at the compost bay, Ch 95 at Wrenlow off the fen's ground, Ch 89's reading in the yard, Ch 98's ledger on the seed-house table with the well house door as the noticeboard.**

**89. A Class Of Use** (days 130–131, the tenth and eleventh, Wed–Thu, the gate, the yard, the well house door, ~2,869)
**The terms arrive on the district's own cart and every word is correct.** Four sheets, class of use 4: carriage of seed in crates over ground the district carries in schedule between keeper and keeper from a node in the district's book. Four parts: sent under the third point of the ninth; fourpence the crate on declared capacity because the office has no power to measure; conditional use — operator keeps declared capacity and produces the record on request, operator being the occupier of the first-end node; collected at point of use or office, appointed man. Number at head, six-figure number at foot, box for holding number. **Tova Reed reads all four aloud before anything else.** Nailed whole on the left of the door under the notice. The box gets the use number with the substitution entered. **Marek's finding: asking to be recalculated on six would hand the office the one fact it cannot get at, so the fen pays on thirty and enters the eightpence.** Ends on the return column and the rate in the same room.

**90. What Six Is Worth** (days 132–133, the twelfth and thirteenth, Fri–Sat, the yard, the seed-house bench, ~3,005)
**The rate against the measured capacity, and the batch's midpoint turn.** Eighteenth section-nine note (argument, carries sentence). Arithmetic on four sheets: fourpence on thirty is 120d, ten shillings, one shilling a month; on six is 24d, two shillings; difference eightpence; compost at four shillings against 399 short is seventy-nine pounds sixteen, a shilling buys a quarter hundredweight, gap closes in a hundred and thirty-three years, read twice. **Anneke Roos: a rate is not a bill and a bill is not a debt and the difference is who can ask the question.** Rate goes on top of the levy, entered with fear in same paragraph. Tin nine shillings to eight after payment, both figures in same breath. Seed-route ledger begun as second book, first entry entire, reader anybody who comes. Ends on the unread third part.

**91. Conditional Use** (days 134–135, the fourteenth and fifteenth, Sun–Mon, the yard, the well house door, ~2,674)
**What a condition does to a readable book.** Renner on a Sunday: it is not a trick, it is a key; operator is the first word the office ever had for the fen and duties are the price; record of the route is a door and the office has not said what is behind it. **Tova's refusal: produce schedule and ledger to any person at any hour, do not produce the fen's own book to anybody, one line inside the seed-house door.** Marek argues both halves — refusal invites a file, ledger publishes the small truth and drawers the large — and she has both entered against her own interest. Leak five-eighths at seventh hour, bed three and a half, no connection entered. Fifteenth reading, count nineteen. **Marek at the gate, said twice: a condition is only worth anything if there is a no behind it, and there is no no.**

**92. The Refusal Clause** (days 136–137, the sixteenth and seventeenth, Tue–Wed, the yard, the compost bay, the well house door, ~2,795)
**The volume's resolution, engineered not spoken.** Three finds: condition needs a no; no person in the book can say it, only anybody; Marek's draft with a definition of use is struck at its first sentence by Tova because two definitions make the numbered one govern and the door is the part remembered. Lissa: it must be sayable at a fence to a woman who cannot read. Final clause entire on same sheet under seven columns, no names, no signature, no reason, no appeal, copyable including blank, read at distance and corrected. Taken off and put back on same four nails, holes showing. Bay seventy-one days. Cost priced: any person at either end can stop the route forever and the fen can do nothing. No copy sent to far end; goes on post when a person goes. Nineteenth note (argument, carries sentence). Ends on two documents disagreeing what a use is.

**93. The First Toll** (days 138–139, the eighteenth and nineteenth, Thu–Fri, the gate, the well house step, ~2,686)
**The climax opens and the tax is paid and entered.** Hedd Marlow, appointed, at point of use at the gate: one shilling for the month on thirty, not per crossing. Lissa counts it on the step before two people and reads aloud; receipt with use number in holding box, not put on door — receipt in a book is for anybody who asks, on a door for anybody who comes. Marlow unasked: if route carried five hundred there would be five of him, ordinary course. Tin nine to eight. Toll in ledger and fen's book as toll, not bill, not debt, collector not creditor. **Dunmar: the fen has lost the last thing it could keep by not having it — nothing can be served on nobody.** Renner: a use he has wanted nine days, not seed, leaves return column alone, puts it in on the twentieth. Ends on a wanted use the return column cannot answer.

**94. A Use Nobody Asked About** (days 140–141, the twentieth and twenty-first, Sat–Sun, the gate, the yard, ~2,965)
**The third party.** Renner in writing to the clerk: eleven loads of lime and stone to the low quarter, second break made good for eleven pounds, eleven out and eleven back crate by crate, fourpence a hundredweight, either end stops on day's notice, not as keeper but as cart, leaves return column alone. **Marek: the fen cannot say no because declared thirty against measured six is already non-compliance, and refusing a paying use reads as failure of condition.** Renner: district cannot do it — no charter at far end, no number, honest answer cheaper; nine men and no work, entered as record not complaint. Marek on low quarter guilt entered. **Jonas: the four-day-old clause is no use — fen refusing is operator not operating, failure of Crown condition, not refusal under fen clause.** Tova: public schedule with return is a road anybody may use; only body with right to refuse is the one that wrote nothing enforceable at far end. Cart past gate lashed Nia's way, entered without why and without asking. Ends on the far end as the only no left.

**95. The Dry End Refuses** (days 142–143, the twenty-second and twenty-third, Mon–Tue, the yard, the first break, Wrenlow, ~2,620)
**The clause used once, by the far end, against an unasked use.** Fen votes standing not to send anybody to collect; Ilsa and Jonas go as work to stone the first break. Renner to the thorn fence, no gate. Wenna Roke at fence, hoe down: road would carry eleven down and eleven back past her fence with no column at her end, she would be the only person on a road not on paper — refusing to be the only person not on paper, not refusing him; answer no and no, same in a month. Renner: not arguing about a column, gone. Reports in yard in six sentences, declines terms — second man to keep mouth shut where a man cannot read; refusal carried by face not boy. Entry third of season as fact not terms, terms held by nobody, with price: route smaller by one use, second break not made good, low quarter four wells off, no person says refusal wrong. **Marek: two documents and nobody can say which governs.** Count twenty, middle and last readings. Ends on Crown use versus fen refuser undefined.

**96. The Arrangement That Is Not A Charter** (days 144–145, the twenty-fourth and twenty-fifth, Wed–Thu, the well house door, the north gate, ~3,063 after trim)
**Concrete resolution, not a win.** Five-clause arrangement entire: route to low dry not beyond; honors Crown terms, shilling paid, schedule and ledger produced; measured entered under declared every month in same hand; either end stops on day's notice; copy takable at this end, no third copy sent to far end — no fifth sheet; term to thirtieth, then new writing or stop, stopping is not failing. Anneke finds small lie in heading and keeps it with reason. Man of fifty: rather be small and know, not a conversion. Man of seventy: does arrangement attract rate — Marek in ninety seconds: route rated, arrangement not a use, if office says otherwise in writing fen pays on own document. Beat thirteenth, nothing under. Graft arithmetic longhand: designed four hundred, short three-ninety-nine, nineteen acres green, Longneck leaning eleven degrees, fed twentieth, true figure none, starving not visible from top. Sixty pounds ten not paid not remitted not reduced, file open. Twentieth note (record, sentence off). Ends on arithmetic a hand in four years can check.

**97. A Useful Engineer** (days 146–147, the twenty-sixth and twenty-seventh, Fri–Sat, the seed-house table, the pit, the gate, ~3,021)
**Reputation, not compliment.** Two letters same day because of readable book: district post three pounds a month with box seal counter-signature, deliver seed annex and eleven years for schedule check — refused in one line Thursday, same as inside door, polite man understood; printer: second printing, how many, separate ledger pamphlet, three men asked for number not truth, price not on sheet — Marek: write price on next one in body, favor is chain. **Tova sixth time on ledger against interest, stopped counting: readable is position not virtue, nobody thanked and cannot be.** At pit with pump on: ownership is claim on thing you cannot make again, method takable by watching four times, so nothing owned — bean, March line, flowerpot graft — only a line in a book; you own seed I own root, cannot same hand without loss, in four years printer decides who checks book. Entered question not answered, neither thanked, third time that line in eleven months. Cart past gate second time in season, lashed Nia's way, not stopped, count entered reason not, not asked, not written.

**98. The Ledger** (days 148–149, the twenty-eighth and twenty-ninth, Sun–Mon, the seed-house table, the yard, the well house door, the low dry, ~3,103)
**Volume close image, as outlined.** Three books out together for Cray copy, and the first time those three have ever been out together: fieldbook unopened this year, use log twelve lines contacts never readings and no fieldbook log ever, ledger nineteen pages begun thirteenth. Drawer shut with levy rent and sealed letter since nineteenth three seasons back, unopened undiscussed. Readings: schedule thirty over six, clause untestable used once, notice four lines, class terms with toll shilling and eightpence, arrangement to thirtieth, refusal third of season first used by fence occupier on the twenty-second against eleven carts, terms held nowhere, Tova six times stopped counting. Twenty-first note (argument, carries sentence) entire. At door Tova plain with no history: who changed maps — nine-year mark unmapped maintained, went out to a hundred and forty seed houses in a winter, and then on the fourteenth of last month this holding told a district office in writing it was a holder, and the answer came on the ninth of **this** month and was not a no, and the answer was a class of use and the rate on it came on the tenth; second half money who paid; seven things now shape not content: class, kept-off name, cutting sheet, sixth-load road, hole form, fourpence toll, recognized hand; printer set **two hundred** pages honest. **Tomorrow sowing closes, we do not know.** Lamp on living bulb to feed tonight, three books under it, door schedule right notice left, bay eighty-three days not full, cart twice past not stopped never asked, fence no gate eleven plots six crates wrong-named bean two stones two sheets, two ends no charter book unsigned in middle rate shilling on five-times figure true under it.

## Repairs applied to the drafted Volume 02 Batch 0005 on a verification pass

**The batch was audited before adoption and seventeen sites were wrong, all of the same class — a distance that was off. All seventeen are fixed in place. No scene was moved, no beat cut, no figure altered and no quotation touched. The batch was 28,788 words, range 2,619–3,108 at that point. Full item-by-item table in `state/continuity.md` under *A verification pass over the drafted Batch 0005*. A review pass then ran over the same ten chapters, found that two of these repairs had been made in the wrong direction, and corrected them; see *The Batch 0005 review pass — Volume 02* below.**

- **90** — the class was named on the ninth, so *two days ago* became *three days ago*; the levy is on **sixteen** houses and not forty, in both places it was named; Tova's tenure is **eleven years** and not eleven months.
- **92** — the strike of clause two is the twenty-second of **last month**.
- **93** — the reading rule is the **twentieth** of a month two months back, not the fourteenth.
- **94** — the measured six is the twenty-sixth of **last month**; the local-holder entry is the ninth of **this** month *(the first pass wrote last month and the review pass corrected it)*; the clause went on the door on the seventeenth, so it is **three** days old and had to wait three days to find out it could not be used; the strike is the twenty-second of **last month**.
- **95** — the loaded cart crossed the first break on the **sixth of this month** and it has been **sixteen days**; the rule is the **twentieth** of a month two months back; the chalk-cart reading was the seventh of **this month**, which is the Sunday.
- **96** — Anneke has been trying since the ninth of **this** month *(the first pass wrote last month and the review pass corrected it)*.
- **97** — the post was argued for **a day**, not a day and a half.
- **98** — the three books had never been out together before and the last floor reading of a like kind is the twenty-eighth of a month two months back; the young man of twenty has been coming into the district since he could walk; the map went out to a hundred and forty seed houses, the fen's letter is the fourteenth of last month, the answer the ninth of **this** month, and the rate came on the tenth. **The closing speech now carries no unanchored interval at all.** *(Corrected by the review pass: the answer was the ninth of *this* month, not of last month, and the book is two hundred pages, not four hundred.)*

## The Batch 0005 review pass — Volume 02

**A review ran over these ten chapters and the state, and six defects were applied in place. Four of them are one defect: the band for day 129. The month begins on day 121, so day 129 is the ninth of *this* month and day 99 is the ninth of last month; the first pass read the batch prompt's day table instead of its own day clock and put four chapters in the wrong month, one of them the volume's closing speech, where the district thereby answered the fen five days before the fen wrote. No scene was replaced, no plot moved, no beat cut.**

- **91, 94, 96** — an event of day 129 dated to *the ninth of last month*. All three now say **the ninth of this month**.
- **98** — the closing speech now runs: the fen's letter on the **fourteenth of last month**, the answer on the **ninth of this month**, the class of use on the ninth and **the rate on the tenth**, which is the order they happened and matches Ch 89.
- **98** — *a printer who has set four hundred pages of our book* is now **two hundred pages**. The book is two hundred pages in eight chapters and there is no second printing in the prose; four hundred copies is the Ashcombe table at Auberron.
- **57, 86, 87, 92** — six British spellings removed against the bible's standing rule: *favour* ×4, *defence*, *travelled*.
- **State** — the wrong band was canon in this file, in `state/continuity.md` and in `state/current.md`, and is corrected in all three. **October is four months back by day 130, not five**; this file and the batch prompt said five and the volume-close prompt was right.

**The batch is 28,783 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,620–3,103. Volume 02 is 141,818.**

# VOLUME 03 — BATCH 0001, CHAPTERS 99–108, MOVEMENT 1

**Volume 03, *The Memory Orchard*, opened here. The volume's outline is `outline/volume-03.md`; the batch's governing prompt is `workspace/volume-03/batch-0001/PROMPT.md`. Volume 01 and Volume 02 are closed; Volume 02's close is `reviews/volume-02.findings.md` and it was run one phase late, which is recorded there and in `state/current.md`. Ten chapters, days 151–170, **28,582 words, range 2,620–3,119** (28,570 after the Batch 0001 fix pass and 28,402 and 2,599–3,117 before it; the passes are `reviews/volume-03-batch-0001.findings.md`). One Fieldbook panel spent, in Chapter 101, and quoted by no document. Section-nine notes twenty-two to twenty-six counted, sentence off the twenty-fifth only. No month named in narration or in any document. No Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Continuity Office on the page, no history lecture. No romance. Nothing tender. The ending is untouched and no new enemy was introduced.**

- **99 — *A New Writing*** — days 151–152, the first and the second. Lissa Vale enters the fifth month's compost line and tells the yard she does not know whether she will write the same words again; Anneke Roos wants to ask and does not, and the wanting is visible. The arrangement's term ended with the closing of the sowing. **The road goes on by a new writing: six clauses on one page, shorter than the five they replace, with one new clause — the record of the route is one record, a copy to Cray and a copy to Cauldron Reach, and no person holding any part of it keeps a private copy of any part of it.** Carried 4–3 in daylight with the book shut, against the vote of the man of about fifty, whose reason is *afraid of the man, not of the one place*, and who asks for his initial against nothing. The seventh column goes to **twenty-one**. The examination is kept with no officer and no form, by choice. **At the fourth hour a woman comes up the fen road on foot with a slat crate and says at the gate what she came for, because Lissa Vale asks it in that order.** Note 22, a record, sentence off.
- **100 — *A Crate Off The Plateau*** — days 153–154. **Merrid Ollvar, about fifty-four, of Cauldron Reach**, four houses, eleven acres, thirty-one years, walked nine days down off the Glassward because nobody answered three letters. Not a member of the cooperative, not a villain, not redeemed. Thirty-one pears, sound, stalks on, picked on **the sixteenth of a month three months back** — day 76, and day 76 to the gate on the third is **eleven weeks exactly** — out of a row that fruited eleven weeks out of season. Her question: *is it a harvest or a fault.* **The day on the buying paper is the seventh of next month, day 187.** A buyer at a price the hundredweight, the same four seasons, who has never asked why the fruit is odd and who **has not been told**. Anneke Roos does the sum in the open and will not say the figure aloud in a yard with a stranger in it: the whole of it is more than this holding has taken in since the charter was made, and it is her money and not ours. Tova Reed kills the two easy explanations — a graft does not do that, and the buyer is not a trick, he is about to be tricked by a woman who did not mean to be. **Coll Renner: *a thing like that needs a licensed man and not a guess.*** Lissa Vale refuses the steward's figure and pays it inside four minutes and enters the refusal as the standing.
- **101 — *The Second Reading*** — day 155, a Sunday. Renner tests and writes the test down first, charges nothing for the day, does not touch the bead and says so aloud. **The Fieldbook panel is spent here and nowhere else in the batch** — three plain lines about a collar and a season's set, the instrument's own account, not a finding, quoted by nobody in any document for the rest of the book. Eleven seconds, the four inches of rootmark hot, the hand useless from the wrist down, entered as Stage 2 and as nothing more. Then the mechanism, stated as engineering: **the tree is not remembering, it is keeping order; a graft carries the thing put in and loses the thing that was there; the question is what it was told, and by whom, and whether the telling is still going on.** Tova puts *memory* off the table before a form can carry it. The use log goes to the **thirteenth** line, a contact and not a reading. The going is costed in money, in days, and in what is not being done here.
- **102 — *The Road Up*** — days 156–157. The party: Marek, Tova, Lissa, Renner. **Gael Senn stays and keeps the books, and the fen entered that no vote was taken and why, and that the reason has a person in it.** Nine days of horse goes into the compost line in the ordinary words and not as a fault, and the shortfall stops being three hundred and ninety-nine hundredweight and is not going to be next month either. **The fieldbook leaves the building for the first time since it came into it and a sub-office is told in person at Fennmere**, and Anneke Roos refuses to send anyone who is loaded or has a stake in it and then asks Lissa Vale to go, and enters both halves of that sentence in the order she said it. **Marek Vale does not ask her for it, and the entry carries her name and not his.** Ilsa Kern cuts ninety into both boards. The man of about fifty reads the fifth clause out at the gate as they go, to a road. Note 23, an argument, sentence carried.
- **103 — *Eleven Acres On A Shelf*** — days 158–159. **The Glassward, off the fen for the first time in ninety-eight chapters.** Exposed rootwood standing out of the ground in the open, a seam that stops dead in a row with clean ends, wind off the ice from the second hour. Against the north wall of the yard, **pressed rootwood in bales stacked higher than a man, rotted, that nobody has ever opened and that Lissa Vale will not open**, because a stack is a record and she has seen what a body does to a record it is frightened of. The fen reads the ledger aloud every evening in the barn for nine days and the arithmetic follows it four hundred miles. **The third row of pears: a cut, dressed and healed, dated at one season with a rule and a lens and a method said out loud first, and Marek's own error of the spring named so the reader knows the size of the tool. The row carries eleven cuts; nine were dated and two were left alone with his reason for leaving them.** Two answers available and the chapter does not choose: a bad cut, or a good cut with a reason, on a day somebody knew about beforehand. Tova: *a bad cut has a reason in the man's head, and this is nine trees with the same cut and nobody dressed a wound nine times by accident.* The reader is Merrid Ollvar, who asked for the date of the reading to be written at the foot, in her own hand and not the clerk's.
- **104 — *What A Cut Is*** — days 160–161. **Taken, dressed, closed, left alone long enough to close, taken again. Eleven of them, on one row, in one week, this season. That is not damage, that is a method.** The healing wood continuous with the limb and the limb carrying, and the growth *below* the cut greater than above it. **A smooth face with a bevel on one side only, the same side on all nine, and no chisel and no draw-knife on the district's hire sheet at the change of horses or on the district's own sheet at the foot of the plateau**; Renner has seen that bevel once and would rather not say whose in a barn, and was not asked again. The real middle of the batch: **Marek reads them as a man in a hurry, Tova reads them as a list, and neither softens it and the fen's book cannot hold two findings under one line.** He says aloud that he would rather it was the hurry, and that wanting it is a man preferring the answer that lets him off. **The money in the same day:** the price, the day, and a clause that warrants the weather and not the ground, read out loud in a barn by a woman who has read it to herself every morning for eleven weeks. **He does not tell them what to do with the pears.** Note 24, an argument, sentence carried.
- **105 — *The Graft*** — day 162. The third tree is dying and the face they came to read is **under** it, and a face you cannot reach is a face you cannot read. Four rules entered in the fen's book: **one was made in the spring and died in the spring and is not a link and is not counted, and two is counted — the collar of the second tree in the row, which is the tree in the pot, read on the fifth; so the scion attempted on the twelfth is three, and four fails, the number does not go up, and a fourth is not attempted because it fails and the reason is on a page from two months back and is not restated, because a man who restates it is a man who thinks he might be the exception.** The graft takes, and it takes at a quarter of what it should, and the cost comes out of the extension, and the row will hold that tree at about three-quarters and not the whole. Tova's question about who loses is answered with the scion: it is out of the second tree, and in four years nobody will be able to tell which is which from the ground, and **write it down, because a thing nobody wrote down is a thing nobody can check, including us.** Then the decision: Marek proposes to hold the tree's flow with a draw he has never done, **on the recorded objection of a licensed man who says a man who has never done it should not do it on a tree four houses drink from, and who says that a draw takes it out of somewhere.** The objection is entered entire with no reply, and the engineer is made to say three things: that he was told no by a man who knows more, that he is doing it on the objection and not over it, and that **if the well is shallower on the fourteenth it is entered as his cost and read aloud to four households whatever he is like at the time.** Tova: *you have just given away a well.*
- **106 — *The Draw*** — days 163–164. **The first draw in the book, and the first severe rootburn in the series.** Water, heat and rootlight, four hours, a living path, and it came back the way it came. The cost in the order it happened, from the licensed man's book and not the engineer's: heat past the four inches of the rootmark and into the elbow, fever inside two hours, three fingers gone, **taste gone entire and sour not back by the twentieth and no promise**, and an hour at about the fifth that is not there and that he does not intend to find out. He could not stop it by wanting to stop it. **At the sixth hour it stopped the way a man stops talking, because a licensed man holding a lamp did not ask and said instead that one of them had to be the wrong one about which.** The downstream: **the well two hundred yards downslope, nine feet on the sixteenth of last month on her own rule and her own nail — the rule is the sixteenth of every month, and the fourteenth was an extra reading she gave because he asked for it in front of witnesses — five and a half at the fourth hour of the fourteenth, and it does not come back.** Entered in the seed-route ledger, not the fen's book, because that was a cost and not an intention. **The man of about seventy writes the first rule this body has ever written about the engineer of record instead of about the ground: nothing is to be attempted again until a person has asked him in a room, on a day, what is the matter with his arm, and a licensed man has written it down. No number of days is entered, because a number of days would be a promise.**
- **107 — *The Inventory*** — days 164–165. A schedule of records held, fourth part, three sheets and a covering letter with a seal. **A list is not a copy. A part three record is held under a standing condition that it is unfit for evidentiary use: it exists and does not count, it may not be produced in any proceeding, letter, inventory return or dispute, and no officer of the district may remove the condition, including the officer who signs the letters. And a holder who cannot classify a record states so, and the statement is itself a classification — the way out of the form is the form.** The man of about fifty: *they have not asked for a copy, they have told us in writing a list is not a copy, and they have written down a way of making a record disappear by listing it.* **Sabin Roell**, records officer, about thirty-four, comes up herself, gives her name once, says three things in the order she says them, and **looks twice on purpose for a power she expects to find and does not find, and says so first, before anybody has accused her of anything.** Tova, from her experience of the fourth part: *a direction given to another person is a record in which somebody is told what to do by somebody who was not the one who did the work. It is not a reading. It is not a result. It is an order.* Tova: *then it is the order that matters, and not the work.* **And the answer: the district will hold copies of everything, in perpetuity, at no charge, under a signature — and Tova Reed says in one sentence that she has read the shape of a free offer with a signature attached before, is not going to take it, and is not going to say where she read it. Nobody asked her. The fen entered that the question was available to anybody in that yard and was taken by nobody, and that it is available again tomorrow, and that the fen has entered that it will not be taken.** This is the Chapter 4 plant of Volume 01 paying off with **no number, no date, no place, no name and no source given to it.** The offer is declined in writing in one line with the plain reason entered and the good reason left out. Note 25, a record, sentence off with the reason underneath.
- **108 — *The Fieldbook*** — days 166–170. On the sixteenth, in an empty yard four hundred miles off, **Ilsa Kern and Jonas Byre read the well house door out and the count of the readings of the seventh column goes to twenty-two, and nobody was in the yard, and it was said out loud before the counting, and the two ends of this body could not have checked each other.** On the fourteenth, at Lantern Reach, **the fourteenth beat is logged** — cause not established, licensed person present none, nothing written underneath — and nobody on the plateau hears of it for a week. **Lissa Vale opens the fieldbook.** Nobody asked her. The engineer did not ask, the steward did not ask, the licensed man did not ask, and the entry of the seventh of this month with her name on it and not his has been carried four hundred miles and obeyed. **There is no use-log line for the fieldbook, there never has been, there is not going to be, and nobody in that barn said why.** What is on the pages and nothing more: the column head and *confirmed — set — 4* and Aldren's last line of figures; an impossible year with a sentence in Lissa Vale's own later hand telling the reader to draw no inference, which she has never told anybody she wrote; and a work order on district paper, a sheet of three, an office named at the head and not explained, a direction to a named node in the year of the shaft, and a hand that makes the *W* the way it made it in *weir*. The line is read aloud: *Third week, Thursday. Take the branch at the upper collar, the whole of it, and dress. Flow at the head is not what the board says and the board is not mine. **Necessary.** Do not enter this sheet against the node. Enter it against me.* **Nobody in that barn sees a vision and nobody speaks to a dead man.** Marek: *that is my father, and he wrote necessary, and he gave no reason, and he told a clerk not to put it against the node. I cannot find out whether he was right and I am not going to be able to.* Tova: *we have a piece of paper and not a father.* Four copies, one of them **not the fen's** — inside a licensed man's coat, in his own name, and no office can call that a record of a holder — and the seventh column copied **as a line, including the blank**. The fieldbook is closed, put in a room with a lock at the first house, and **may not be opened again in this season**, and Lissa Vale's second reason is entered because it is the true one: **she did not open it once in nine years because she was afraid of it, and she has opened it once, this afternoon, and is not opening it again.** The book was last out of the press on the twenty-eighth of a month two months back, when it was put on a chair for a floor to be read out of the middle of a room, **and it was not opened then either** — forty-nine days. The well read at the fourth hour on the sixteenth and on the twentieth, **on her own rule and her own nail**, and no entry anywhere for it coming back. The man of about seventy asks for **eleven cuts and a word and a shelf** to be written on one line, and it fits on a line. Note 26, an argument, sentence carried.

**The batch is 28,582 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,620–3,119, inside 27,000–30,000 and inside 2,550–3,120 at every chapter. Ch 107 at 3,113 and Ch 108 at 3,119 are the two near a ceiling and a later writer must not lengthen either.** The fix pass added words to nine chapters and took them back out of Ch 108, which is why Ch 108 is the tightest it has been. Volume 03 stands at 28,582 words and Volume 02's 141,818 and Volume 01's 166,022 both stand.
