# VOLUME 03 — BATCH 0002, CHAPTERS 109–118, MOVEMENT 2

**Volume 03, *The Memory Orchard*, Movement 2, *The Thing In The Yard*. Ten chapters, days 171–190, **28,816 words, range 2,647–3,119** by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings. No Fieldbook panel spent; Batch 0002's one available panel was deliberately not spent on a sequence of dates. Section-nine notes twenty-seventh to thirty-first counted, sentence off the twenty-eighth only. No month named in narration or in any document. No Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Continuity Office on the page, no history lecture. No romance. Nothing tender. The ending is untouched and no new enemy was introduced.**

- **109 — *The Seventh Hour*** — days 171–172, the twenty-first and the twenty-second, a Tuesday. Three people in the fen yard at the seventh hour. **The leak measured for the first time in two years in front of another person: five-eighths of an inch, twice, two rules, the second the one that reads smaller on that corner, pump on, water into the housing and not into the pit, the pit not up.** The figure said aloud for the first time in two years, and the clerk enters that the last entry in the book about it is the seventh hour of day 138, **thirty-three days back**, that the figure in the book was thirty-three days old and had been carried forward as though it were current **and that nobody in that yard decided to carry it forward**, that the party leaving on the seventh is a **different interval of fourteen days and is not the reason**, that **eighteen days of the gap are days the whole of the body was standing in that yard**, and that **the fen cannot now say on which mornings it was measured and on which it was not, because a practice that was never written down leaves no account of itself and the absence of the account is not evidence of any day in particular.** Gael Senn enters that a figure which does not move is not an answer and not a relief. The man of about seventy is fetched at the ninth hour and asks **who owns it**; the answer is that nobody knew it needed an owner until somebody went looking for a figure and the book had not kept it. **The hinge sentence: a rule that has to be remembered by the person it was made for is not a rule, it is a habit with a number in it**, with the return-main story of the three men who remembered it, and Jonas Byre in the yard saying **I was the one who remembered it, and remembering it was the whole of the work**, and asking for his age and his ladder in the same book as the figure. The practice is entered with a name against it and no due date, proposed by the man of about seventy at a hard standing. The chapter ends on the man of about fifty: **what the road took out was the number of people a figure could be said to in front of.** Note 27, an argument, sentence carried.
- **110 — *What Is In The Stack*** — days 173–174, the twenty-third and the twenty-fourth. **Merrid Ollvar opens her own family's stack herself, with a knife, on the twenty-third, at about the eighth hour, and it takes five hours and her hands go.** Three people ask to be allowed to do it for her and are refused three times with three different reasons: Marek on the wind and her hands, Coll Renner on the ground that a record dug into is not a record taken out, and **the man at the second house, who is not being generous and says so in his first nine words, on the ground that in thirty years there will be a story in this valley about the day the valley let a stranger open the stack — which is the best argument in the chapter and is not an engineering argument at all.** Sixty-one bales, counted aloud twice by the man at the second house; pressed hot and green; the outside a gray pulp to the depth of a hand. Inside: sound wood, pale, in lengths of about a foot and a half, **every length within an inch of every other, and a single bevel of about an inch and a half on one edge of every cut end, the same edge on all of them.** **Marek Vale's one sentence, and the batch's turn: the cutting is done and the pressing is done by two different people or in two different trades, and a press that good is not a family press.** Lissa Vale's Ch 103 refusal is paid off: *A stack is a record. A body that opens a record it is frightened of is a body that will say so later and not sooner*, carried four hundred miles and obeyed, and she stands in a stranger's yard hearing about a thing she could have read and asks for it to be read twice. The fen's resistance: **a stack is not evidence until this body says it is, and the moment it does, a family's thirty-one years goes into a book four hundred miles off and an office in nine, and the fen says so out loud in the yard that it is treating it as wood.** No note.
- **111 — *The Maintenance Sequence*** — days 175–176, the twenty-fifth and the twenty-sixth. The sound wood dated by the same method as the cuts — a rule, a lens, a lamp, a layer a year, and **a layer on this shelf about a twentieth of an inch, which is an eighth of what a pear limb puts on here** — said out loud before it was used. The nine lengths read six, six, seven, five, six, seven, six, five, six. The core of the pile, where the press did not reach, **twenty-eight layers going back without a break, which is twenty-eight years and is inside Merrid Ollvar's own thirty-one and her father's time** — the place it came out is the one nobody expected. At four places in those twenty-eight there is a band where the layer is thicker and the wood is different, **at intervals of seven years, seven years and six years, the last of them about six years back, with bare years between in which nothing came off that seam at all.** **The maintenance sequence, and it is a hand's method and not a vision: a man who presses rootwood for a living takes outside first, then the wet, then the core, because a core cut live bleeds on the press and the bale goes at the seam; the order is the same every time and the order is in the wood.** Then the object, found by the woman and not the man: **three living grafts in three bales at the core, cut to cut, fitted, bound in bast, one living seam joined to another, put in with the waste, out of the fourth band, on a shelf with no nursery within a hundred miles.** Coll Renner's finding: *Yes, it is a graft. Somebody made this on purpose, they made it to keep, and they put it in with the waste. That is how a man hides it.* **Nobody in that yard says the word after him, and Merrid Ollvar says it and is not answered**, and the fen entered that it declined to ask and that the declining is the finding. And the door is kept open: **a graft in a press is nobody's hobby**, but **I cannot read intent in wood**, so neither hiding nor keeping is entered. Note 28, a record, sentence off with the reason underneath.
- **112 — *The Return*** — days 177–178, the twenty-seventh and the twenty-eighth. **The four leave the shelf at about the sixth hour on the twenty-seventh; four days down, one change of horses, arriving on the thirtieth.** Nobody narrates the road. **The going up was ninety-nine shillings and the coming down a hundred and four**, and the reason is entered: the last two days of a descent are the first two days of somebody else's ascent and the fare is not the same, and the trestle and the bench and the ledger four hundred miles each way go into the compost line. The reader of the body reads the fifth clause out at the gate on the way down, and then reads the entry of the twenty-eighth of last month out after it, and the fen enters that **a gate is the one place on a road where anybody going can hear a thing being read.** The locked room at the first house is not opened, the key does not change hands, and the book in it is not coming down. **The return of inventories is filled in at Lantern Reach by Gael Senn**, because the clerk is the only person who may write in the fen's book and the party is two days down a road, and **the six lines are given entire**: the door and the six clauses, part two; the seed-route ledger, part two, **with the count of readings under the seventh column decided to be a reading, by name, so a person in four years can disagree**; the three books, part two, **and there is a fourth thing in a shut drawer and it is not listed and nobody asked about it**; the fieldbook reading, part two, **described in a way that is true and incomplete**; the licensed man's copy, entered so that nobody reads the return and thinks the body hid a thing; and **the work order not listed, because it is a page in a book that is not the fen's.** The man of about fifty's position: they have asked for a list and not a copy, and this body is now deciding to tell an office **where** to write to, and the man of about seventy says the fen has decided this body's record is worth more than a woman's morning. Anneke Roos's sum is read out of the ledger four hundred miles off, and the man at the second house asks for it to be read out in the yard on the day they arrive, before anybody has said good morning. No note.
- **113 — *The Thirtieth*** — days 179–180, the twenty-ninth and the thirtieth, a Thursday. **The four come off the plateau road at about the sixth hour and the figures are read out in the yard before anybody has said good morning**, as the man at the second house asked, and the steward's nine sentences are that a hundred and four shillings of horse, thirteen weeks of a compost line, a well three and a half feet down, a locked room and eleven cuts — **and she asks for the number of things that are better now than on the sixth of this month, and nobody in either yard takes the question.** **The well, read at the fourth hour before they arrived, by the occupier, on her own rule and her own nail, said out loud before it was written down: five feet and two inches.** Nine feet on the sixteenth of last month, five feet five inches on the sixteenth, five feet and four inches on the twentieth; **shortfall three feet and ten inches; no rain worth the name in twenty-five days; the draw's three and a half feet against the draw and the other half inch against the ground, in a different hand, in the same ledger, on the same day**, and **no entry anywhere for that well coming back.** Marek's two sentences: three and a half is mine and the other half inch is not, and keep them as two figures. **Three options for four houses, each with a person in it, entered and none recommended**, plus the man of about seventy's fourth thing, which is not a choice: four houses drawing from one well three and a half feet short, a quarter out of the spring, and nobody to be short at. **The door read out whole at the tenth hour in a yard of about twenty, by the steward, with the reader and the clerk, the first time in eleven years, entered as a fact about a day and not as a moment, and the count of the readings of the seventh column goes to twenty-three.** **And one hundred and fourteen days cut into both boards and said out loud in a yard for the first time in eleven years**, at the man of about fifty's asking, and the man of about seventy: *nobody can make it go down, and a body with one thing it does not have to be right about.* **The compost line for the month is not entered and the reason is entered.** Note 29, an argument, sentence carried.
- **114 — *What Is In The Coat*** — days 181–182, the first and the second of next month, a Friday. **The leak at the seventh hour in the open at a hard standing with about fourteen people round it, with Jonas Byre appointed reader before the pump was touched: five-eighths, the second rule smaller, the pit not up.** The reader reads out the figure and **the forty-third day since the last entry in the book and forty-two mornings in between, of which the number that were measured cannot be given and is not given.** **Nobody in that yard says out loud that the number is the number and the room is different, and the reason it is not said is that the argument belongs to a woman at Lantern Reach making it to an empty yard since the flood with a chisel in her hand, four hundred miles off.** The door is read and the count goes to **twenty-four**. The examination is kept by Gael Senn at the eleventh hour with no officer and no form, by choice: **nothing found, and not *all well*.** **The compost line is entered by Gael Senn in the steward's book, in a hand that is not the steward's**: nothing put in, none available, four hundred hundredweight specified, **shortfall four hundred and fifty-two hundredweight, ninety pounds eight**, run six months and not softened. Anneke Roos gives the reason — going to look at a thing is not a fault, it is a cost — and the man of about fifty asks **whether the steward is going to be told what another person wrote in her book**, and is told yes, and that Gael Senn asked four months ago and it is the only page in eleven years not in the steward's hand, and the steward reads it back and says one sentence and it is not a complaint. **Second half, nine miles off, at a counter:** the sub-office copy was read twice by a clerk who had no reason to know it was not his; Perrin Osse enters the fact with the hour, has the envelope returned, and says the two things: **the record of an instrument is not the instrument**, and **nothing in this building will stop me except me** — the refusal clause does not stop her and never was going to. **Renner at the counter, one sentence: the copy in my coat is the safe one because it is mine, and that is exactly why it is the one that cannot be relied on, and I can lose the coat and the book and be run over on the fen road in a month.** No note.
- **115 — *The Asking*** — days 183–184, the third and the fourth of next month, a Sunday and a Monday. **The standing rule is satisfied in a room, on a day, with a licensed man writing it down** — the seed house, bay doors open, fourteen people, a Sunday, no form, no notice on the door, and the day named the previous day by the man of about fifty. **Tova Reed asks the question and says before she asks it that she is asking for the body and not for him and that nothing in it is between the two of them.** He answers in the plainest words: the mark does not hurt; the arm is not right above the elbow; four minutes to change hands; **a pail carried on the second of this month with two changes of hand and not reported then, reported now, because nobody had asked him**; **and that he does not know whether he could do it, that he did not say no, and that not knowing is the whole of the answer.** **The entry of the twentieth of last month stands entire and is not reconciled** — a man asked twice about his own arm gives two accounts and both are his, and choosing between them would be the body deciding which honest account it prefers. Renner's second entry is longer than the first and **is longer, not better**, and the recommendation is unchanged: **fit for ordinary work, fit for a scion and a graft, not answering for a draw.** And his reason: a man in a room with other people in it says what he can do, a man with a licensed man's book in front of him says what he will answer for, and the body has correctly arranged to ask both. **Then the argument: the rule is four lines and every one of them is about a stop, and there is no fifth line, and a body that obeys it for ever and never does a thing again looks exactly like a body being careful.** Marek's position: **he is not going to use the second draw, and the reason is not the rule and not the burn — it is that the man at the second house has not been angry with him yet, and a man who goes back up that road with a thing in his hands that moves water turns four households' decision into a decision against him.** The man of about seventy gives him his own Ch 109 sentence back and he does not answer it and does not try. No vote. No note.
- **116 — *The Household On The Shelf*** — days 185–186, the fifth and the sixth of next month. **The part three return comes back on the fifth, in seven days: six items entered as held, nothing classified, and a second line on the office's own sheet saying the word *held* is a fact about a holder and not about a record and that a holder who has not stated a part has not said anything about the record at all.** The man of about fifty: they have spent a fortnight of an officer's time to tell us in writing that they have not touched it, and nobody asked them to. **The sixth: the man of about fifty asks for the fieldbook in front of eight people with his reason stated first and the reason is bad and he says so, and Lissa Vale says no, in one word, with four seconds of silence that is entered and is not a reason.** He does not ask again. The two standing reasons are given and a third is added: **an offer and not an invitation — any person who wants to know what her husband wrote may come up the road on their own two feet and ask her in a barn with nobody in it.** Then a four-line message down off the shelf in Merrid Ollvar's hand with the man at the second house's name against three of the lines, and the hire in the compost line: **four houses on a day letter, one drawing each in turn, the first house a Tuesday because there are four people in it, for a month, to be looked at on the first of the month after at the well with whoever is standing there**, and **they did not ask the fen.** And the man at the second house's flat thing: **you spent a bucket**; he is **not angry yet** and would like that entered in those words; he will be in on **the fifteenth of next month, day 195**, on the grounds a woman of the fen gave him in a barn on the fourteenth of last month, and **the ninth day is in the book so that a man in nine miles cannot find out afterwards that it was never said.** Lissa Vale asks who loses and the answer is three and a half feet of a bucket in four households since the thirteenth of last month, and **both silences are entered — nobody said he should not have come and nobody said he should have told them first — and the fen entered that it knows which way it would have gone and that knowing is not the same as doing.** Note 30, an argument, sentence carried.
- **117 — *The Buyer Comes*** — days 187–188, the seventh and the eighth of next month, a Thursday and a Friday. **The buyer arrives at the seventh hour on the seventh, the day on his paper.** Before that, on the seventh at the fifth hour, **Marek Vale writes nine lines nobody asked him for** — a fact about a row and a date, and *I am not telling this household what to do with a hundred and forty-one trees* — and gives them to a hired man going up, and the man of about fifty reads it back twice and says **that sheet is not advice** and that it went up the road nine days behind a buyer who was at that moment in a yard with a paper on a bench. **The buyer's paper is four lines and a figure: nine shillings the hundredweight, the same four seasons, taken as it stands at picking and not warranted against any fault of the ground, for the season and does not return.** He gives the figure before he is asked. **At the eighth hour, unasked and before any figure is agreed, Merrid Ollvar tells him the truth in nine sentences** and says she is not asking him to change his price and is not asking him to promise to come back. And he says the sentence: **in four seasons nobody has ever given me a reason, and I have learned not to ask for one**; and then that **he will not come back after this season**, not a threat and not an opening. The weighing is in the yard with the count said out loud: **one hundred and nine hundredweight. Anneke Roos's sum, done four hundred miles off and read out of the ledger on the eighth: forty-nine pounds and one shilling, more than this holding has taken in since the charter was made** — and **the price is not in the fen's book**, because a price this body did not hear said by the person who owes it is a price it has guessed at, and she has asked for it on paper. **The man at the second house: so the biggest number anybody has said out loud in this fen for three years is a number this fen cannot check.** And the household decides at the fourth hour, in daylight, with the buyer in the yard, the fen four hundred miles off and not asked: **they sold all of it, and set aside a fifth for the four houses before anything came out of the stone room, and the reason was written on the back of the reading before the money was counted, and it is that a household of four that sells everything has nothing in the stone room on the eleventh, and that she wanted the money in a drawer with somebody else's name on the account.** No noble refusal and no lesson. **Nobody on the shelf told the buyer about the eleven cuts, and that silence is entered, and so is a hired rider's, and the fen has entered that it is going to have to learn to count its silences, because a body that enters some silences and not others has a habit and not a rule.** The reading, the crate, the row and the money all stay on the record, and the fifth clause is read out at the gate to a man going down four days of road. No note.
- **118 — *The Fourth Copy*** — days 189–190, the ninth and the tenth of next month. **The office has never been a villain and the answer is a wall built out of correctness**, and the fen will not be able to point at anybody in four years. **The answer is the fourth copy and it is not a better copy: it is a copy in a shape the schedule has no part for, and the shape is a sequence.** Two pages, eight numbered points — the eleven dated faces, the bevel, the healing, the rootwood in a maintenance order over twenty-eight years in four bands seven years apart, the same bevel on the ends, pressed at a mill that is not a family press, three grafts at the core, and the sheet of district paper with an office at the head, a signature at the foot, one word heavier than the others, and the two years that do not agree — **naming nothing and no one, addressed to nobody, with no *you* in it, and the four lines that tell a clerk what to enter not in it and not going to be in it.** Tova Reed: it is a work and not a result, not a reading, and not part three, because a date is not a reading and *necessary* is a reason a man writes for himself at four in the morning and does not send to anybody. **The man of about seventy: the form says a holder who cannot classify may state so, and the statement is itself a classification, so we cannot say we cannot classify it, and cannot send it, and cannot mention that it exists, and the return of six that went on the twenty-eighth is complete and correct.** The man of about fifty: **we have left a record off a return, and a return of inventories is a document and a document is a statement**; second time, fourth time in three years, and **in four years somebody in nine miles is going to work out how to ask for a shape.** Tova Reed refuses to be pleased: **a shape an instrument cannot reach is a good way to live and it is not a way to win.** Four copies into four hands, none headed by a holder, none carrying a part, none carrying a name, and **the seventh column not in them, entered as a loss.** Then the cost of the batch in the ordinary words on the tenth: the sixth month's compost line, the leak and the forty-third day and the mornings that cannot be counted, **the beat at the fifteenth and the column open and the fen has not opened it**, the boards at one hundred and twenty-four, the well and no entry for it coming back and four houses on a day letter, the March's tin at one hundred and seventy-one days with both undertakings standing, and **the standing rule in force with a second draw on a table that this body has not decided and has entered that it has not decided it for five days.** The man of about seventy: every figure on the list is worse than the one above it, except two, and he would like somebody to look at the two. **And then the question, once, plainly, in a barn with the lamp lit, asked by Tova Reed for herself and not for the body — he made a list before he started, and nobody knows whether it is still going on, or it is not. Which is it.** Nobody answered, nobody knew, and the question was not asked of the one man in the room who is dead. Note 31, an argument, sentence carried.

**The batch is 28,816 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,647–3,119, inside 27,000–30,000 and inside 2,550–3,120 at every chapter. Ch 114 at 3,099 and Ch 118 at 3,119 are the two near a ceiling and a later writer must not lengthen either.** Mechanical check on the drafted batch: quotation marks clean (no paragraph ends with a speech open and no closing mark lacks an opening mark); **no non-ASCII glyph at all, so no curly mark and no em dash**; no month named in narration or in any document; the word *seat* does not appear; no Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Continuity Office on the page, no Thornwild, no Sera Quill; no British spellings; **no metafictional intrusion** (nine were found on a sweep and all nine were replaced with the in-world referent the sentence needed); no duplicate paragraph and no duplicate sentence over ten words. **Volume 03 stands at 28,582 + 28,816 = 57,398 words across twenty chapters, and the manuscript at 336,422 + 28,816 = 365,238 across 118 chapters. Volume 02's 141,818 and Volume 01's 166,022 both stand.**

# VOLUME 03 — BATCH 0003, CHAPTERS 119–128, MOVEMENT 3

**Volume 03, *The Memory Orchard*, Movement 3, *What Sera Took Out*. Ten chapters, days 191–210, the eleventh through the thirtieth of the seventh month, **29,496 words, range 2,720–3,119** by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, as repaired by the twenty-six-finding review pass. No Fieldbook panel spent; Batch 0003's one available panel was deliberately not spent, and the reason is in the continuity file. Section-nine notes thirty-second to thirty-sixth counted, the standing sentence off the thirty-second only. No month named in narration or in any document. No Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Thornwild; **the name Brinewake is not on the page and is reserved to Volume 04, where it comes out of her own mouth.** The Continuity Office named once, on paper only, inside the statement. No romance. Nothing tender on the page. The ending is untouched and no new enemy was introduced.**

- **119 — *A Woman On The Fen Road*** — days 191–192, the eleventh and the twelfth, a Monday and a Tuesday. **Sera Quill, about fifty-six, retired root engineer, comes into Lantern Reach on foot at about the ninth hour**, off the Glassward road and down the fen road, gives her name once, and is entered by Dunmar Fitch with the hour. **The name is in the fen's own book in two places and neither is a document**, and she reads the second one twice and asks to be held as *the fourth one in a list of four* and not as a witness to a pear. **She looked at the four inches on the inside of his forearm for about as long as it takes to look at a sky and said nothing about it**, and the fen entered that she said nothing and did not enter why, and that the two accounts of that arm do not agree and were not reconciled. **The three things, in her order, and it is her standing from Ch 107 and not a courtesy: first, no fee, and what a fee was for — a paid witness is a witness somebody bought, and she would rather be argued with in public and by name; second, she is not a member of this body and is not asking to be one; third, she wants a room and not a hearing.** Entered: *a room is not a hearing, a hearing is not a room, and a room is not a record.* **Lissa Vale's fee, which is the friction of the day: not offered, entered, paid inside four minutes, read back with the hour — the identical act to the third of last month, and Lissa Vale says so out loud, that a body which does a thing twice and says nothing about it is a body that has not changed and only has not noticed.** **Anneke Roos's line: three shillings and eightpence for a fortnight's keep and eleven for the licensed man and twopence for a second blanket, fourteen shillings and tenpence entered on the day, about a tenth of the four pounds five in the tin, and her sentence — this holding refused a free offer in writing in one line on the fifteenth of last month and is now paying a stranger to tell it something, and both halves are in the same book and the fen does not get to be only one of them.** She names the seventeenth herself, for a working room and not a generous one, and will not be asked what she felt and will not be asked whether it was right. The twelfth: the second blanket, and a request at the gate for the six-season book anybody may read, granted inside a minute, with a clerk entering that nobody thought to ask why. The compost line for the month is not entered and the holding does not know its figure. **On the review pass the day's total was corrected from fifteen shillings and eightpence to fourteen and tenpence, which is the three items actually summed, and the figure she gives later for the whole fortnight is eighteen shillings and sixpence.**
- **120 — *What She Is Not Saying*** — days 193–194, the thirteenth and the fourteenth. **The clerk asks for a document and says the reason first, and she refuses in eleven words: *No, because a woman who keeps a copy keeps a reason*, and the clerk counts them and waits four seconds for an addition and there is none.** **She has kept nothing: no copy, no extract, no log, no note, no private ledger, no letter to anybody — and the reason is given, that a woman who keeps a copy keeps a reason and the reason is never the reason she gives, and in nineteen years there has never been a moment where the having of it would have helped anybody.** She announces a piece of a machine that is not a document and is not paper. **The terms, seven lines, in the steward's hand, on the fourteenth: what she did, in order, in a room, on a day she named, with a page held and a licensed man present; the figures read back as figures; two questions that will not be asked; a name that comes off a paper and no explanation; not a member; a room and not a hearing.** Read back once in a room with nobody in it and once with nine people in it, and the two agreed. **The fourth refusal, unnumbered: *who told you to do it*, asked in a yard by the reader of this body, not answered, entered as asked and not answered, with his own reason for asking written into the book so that it is a fact about him — he wanted an office, and offices answer correctly in seven days.** **The batch's first finding, his: the one person who is going to tell us the largest thing we are going to be told has brought nothing to publish, and that is not a defect in her — and she is right, and being right is the worst of it, because a thing a woman says in a room cannot be checked by anybody and cannot be checked by her.** The man of about seventy on a body with no practice at holding a thing it cannot check. The thirty-second note, dated the fourteenth, a record, the standing sentence off it with the reason underneath.
- **121 — *The Ninth Day*** — days 195–196, the fifteenth and the sixteenth, a Friday and a Saturday. **The man at the second house is in the yard at the tenth hour on the fifteenth**, nine days from a four-day road, having left the shelf on the sixth, and **the fen does not know who paid the four hundred miles and has decided not to ask, and has entered that**. **He is angry and it is entered as a fact: *you spent a bucket*, said once and not again that day, no reason asked for, nobody asked him to leave and nobody asked him to explain himself twice.** **Marek gives the answer, which is not an apology and has no word of one: four entries, in order — the draw of the thirteenth of last month with its cost, Coll Renner's *not answering for a draw*, the undertaking made standing in a barn on the twelfth of last month before the thing was done, and the thirtieth of last month's two figures, three and a half feet his and half an inch the ground's.** The clerk reads all four back with the hours; the man of about fifty reads after him. **The man at the second house says the decision was theirs, four houses on a day letter, the first house a Tuesday, begun on the sixth of this month, looked at on the first of next month, and then: *I did not accept what you have just read to me. I did not refuse it either.* Both entered in the order they were said in.** Then the sixteenth: **the well on the shelf read at the fourth hour by the occupier in her own yard, in her own hand and by her own nail, said out loud before it was written down — five feet and three inches, up an inch in sixteen days, shortfall three feet and nine inches of which three and a half is the draw's and half an inch is the ground's, and no entry anywhere for that well coming back.** And the door read whole at the tenth hour in a yard of about twenty with a stranger in it, **the count of the readings of the seventh column going to twenty-five, the first middle reading of the season made with the body in its own yard and with somebody in it who is not of the fen — and a count made in front of a stranger can be checked by a stranger, which is the first time in eleven years that has been true.** It is checked, unarranged, inside a minute and a half: she counts twenty-five off the board and notes the head of the column still says twenty-four; **he reads the whole column aloud with his finger, neither of them a clerk, and neither knew the other's name.** A man hired at the change of horses on the **twelfth** for the sheet — four days before the figure he was sent for exists, at four shillings a day for eight days of a four-day road, **thirty-two shillings in two parts, and the fen entered that it did not have thirty-two shillings and took them out of that line anyway.**
- **122 — *The Relay*** — days 197–198, the seventeenth and the eighteenth, a Sunday and a Monday. **The statement: nine lines in her own hand, read out whole in a room with the bay doors shut, a page held by the steward, a licensed man present with nothing on his page but a date, two witnesses appointed standing up (Jonas Byre and Gael Senn) with their reasons given.** It is **a statement of records and not of events and she says so on her own first line.** The **Continuity Office** is named once, on paper, as the office at the head of the district's index of relay designs, and she will not say it again and will not say what it means. **A Crown safety relay of her own design, to the western salt corridor, that could have isolated a pruning pulse before it reached the three heartroots; the red-line warning removed by her hand on the fourteenth of that month at about the second hour, alone, in eleven minutes; the maintenance log altered twice in the same hand and the words put in were *routine recalibration*; a report signed against salt creep and weather in which every figure is true and the corridor is not mentioned; four thousand two hundred died in the first winter and nine thousand displaced, and she will not have the two added together.** **Nine: she was not at the Quiet Break, it is a hundred and twenty-one years old, she was not born, and she tells the room to take it out again if it is set beside the four above.** The reader reads the figures back as figures, each number out loud before the words around it. **One question asked in a room by the engineer of record — *how long did it take you to decide* — and not answered, and the answer not to be asked for, and his finding that it is the first figure in two volumes this body has wanted more than anything and cannot have.** The man of about seventy has *died in the first winter* put in beside the first number and asks that nobody shorten a number into a word. **The cost entered as a cost: six days of a fortnight's living, no fee asked for and none entered and paid anyway, on the same line.** On the eighteenth the fen holds a statement it cannot corroborate and says why, and Lissa Vale names every figure on its own list — route, door, books, eleven cuts, stack, compost line, well, beat, leak, second draw — and **not one of them moved.** The thirty-third note, dated the eighteenth, argument.
- **123 — *The Red Line*** — days 199–200, the nineteenth and the twentieth. **Sera Quill gives Marek Vale the fragment and nothing else, and says in the first breath that she is not giving it to him because she trusts him.** A collar section of the length of a hand, steel and brass, an empty socket, a cut made from the edge with the metal pushed away from it, and a bead set in it that **Coll Renner says out loud, before touching anything, that he is not going to touch, and the clerk enters that he said so.** Four facts, no finding, and **a reading of a piece of metal is not a witness.** **Her name goes on the page first and the engineer's under it, with the reason: a licensed engineer's name on a thing is a promise that the thing is somebody's to answer for, and she has no employer, no district and no office that would stand in front of her at a hearing — and there is no certificate for a thing like this in any district in the world.** **Then the finding, found in public in four minutes: it cannot be copied.** A scribe's description refused by the steward; a tracing refused by the licensed man (*the shape of it is the least of it*); the engineer refuses to take it apart; and a letter written and not sent. **Anneke Roos's arithmetic in the yard, four times worse: a red line is the announcement of a stop; the western salt corridor a hundred and forty miles; a full-rate pulse seventy minutes; an isolation twenty minutes to close and four hours to hold; four thousand two hundred dead in ten weeks; nine thousand is a count of registrations and is a floor and not a total and she will not do a sum with a floor in it; the nearest high ground thirty-one miles off and about eleven hours on salt flat and not ten; so four hours against eleven and seven hours the difference — and she cannot finish it, because no version of the end of that sum reduces four thousand two hundred by a known amount. **The corridor's length, the pulse's rate, the twenty minutes, the four hours, the thirty-one miles and the ten weeks were hers and were said aloud in the room on the seventeenth and were not written down, and they are in none of the nine lines, and the chapter says so.** She asks for the figures to be sent and the clerk enters that she asked, and they have not come.** Her sentence: a figure in a yard that four people out of five were not in the room for, and the fifth is a licensed man with a clip. The well's sheet arrives on the twentieth, four days late, with the hire at thirty-two shillings in two parts.
- **124 — *The Sealed Record*** — days 201–202, the twenty-first and the twenty-second. **The compensation record is named as sealed and not opened, held by the office at the head of the district's index of relay designs and not said out loud in a yard since the eighteenth; people in it by name whose names are not on the page and are not going to be, and the reason is standing and not privacy.** A limit given last in the speaking and written last. **The new thing: the man of about fifty says out loud in a yard that she is the only living person who knows what is in it, is not asking her to confirm it, and lets the page be wrong if it is wrong; she neither confirms nor denies, because a confirmation and a denial would both be documents.** **The word *only* is the word doing the damage and the entry says so: a body cannot check this, cannot ask for it, and cannot cite the fact that it exists without making a claim it cannot support.** The cost is entered, and **the cost is that it grows.** **The question of what her life was like afterwards was available to everybody in that yard and was taken by nobody, and this is the third time this fen has entered that sentence and the count is three.** **A record nobody can produce cannot corroborate a statement and cannot be used to attack one: a wall on both sides, the same wall as the part three condition and the same wall as the shape the schedule has no part for, and the fen has now built three of them in three weeks and every one is correct.** The man of about seventy: a wall that is held is a wall, **a wall that grows is a debt with no date on it**, and a fourth one coming with no name and no number. Gael Senn reads out the four things the body is holding in the order they came in, none of them a document, and says her own standing cannot be exercised on any of them. The thirty-fourth note, dated the twenty-second, argument.
- **125 — *A Decision That Went Right*** — days 203–204, the twenty-third and the twenty-fourth, a Saturday and a Sunday. **She says the volume's turn in the flat voice she uses for reading a table: the pruning on the western salt corridor was not a mistake that went wrong, it was a decision that went right; the ground pruned hardest came back and the ground left alone did not; the people who did it had a reason that worked and the reason is not on anything that survives; and a man who cut eleven branches off one row in one week this season is not a mystery and was probably not a fool.** **The fen wants it to be a finding and it cannot be one: she kept nothing, the figures were asked for on the twentieth and have not come, and it stands as a woman's account of a table, in a district that does not answer to this one, given in a yard, on a Saturday, and it is not a finding.** **Marek puts the mechanism beside it: a graft carries the thing put in and loses the thing that was there; a tree does the work it was told in the order it was told; eleven cuts, nine read, one row, one week, this season, in an order, dressed eleven times by a man who came back on the sixth day; and the reading of the fifth of last month says the row went on being a tree, so the row is not damaged and is not a scar.** **The question changes: it was *is the telling still going on* and it is struck out of his own mouth in front of witnesses, and the question is now *whose order is it*, which is worse, and the fen has entered that it is worse.** He will not line the orders up in a yard. **Tova Reed's third statement of the same argument: a mechanism is not a verification; soundness is not a hand; the distance between a mechanism and a hand is the whole of this volume and nobody in that yard can close it — and eleven cuts has stopped being a mystery and started being an example, and an example is the cheapest thing in a book.** The reader refuses the finding and reads out the wording he will read instead, and the fen entered that it was offered a finding and did not take it, the second time. The man of about seventy on what the body has lost: the right to say it does not know, and the right to think the man was a fool. No note; the reason is that a question asked in public and not answered is not an argument.
- **126 — *Necessary*** — days 205–206, the twenty-fifth and the twenty-sixth, a Monday and a Tuesday. **The work order is put in front of her as a fact and not as a question, and the putting of it is Lissa Vale's and took six minutes and nobody was told.** **Four lines in the steward's hand, copied on the sixteenth of last month from a sheet of three in a barn on a plateau, and the page is in a locked room four hundred miles off and is not coming down that road this season**; and beside them, not read out, the paper, the office's name, the year of the shaft, and the hand that makes the letter *W* a way a licensed man has seen once. She reads it twice, goes back to the first line on the second reading, **chooses the four lines and not the copy of the handwriting, and is entered that she chose them and not entered why.** She says *yes, I have seen paper like that* and refuses three things — where, when, and the name of the office at the head of it — **and the clerk enters that they are three refusals and not one, in the order she said them, and that nobody was asked for any of them.** **Nobody asked her what it is or whose it is or whether the word was right; the question was available to everybody in that room and was taken by nobody, and this is the third time the fen has entered that sentence in three weeks.** Nobody looked at the steward for four minutes and she entered that herself. **Coll Renner: a hand can be described and cannot be dated by anybody who has not got the original, and he will go no further than *trade* and asks for the word in his own hand.** **Marek's position, which is the position of the volume: he entered on the eighteenth of last month that he is not going to be able to find out whether his father was right and he is not unsaying it; the room is shut and he is not going to make her hear the refusal twice; what he has instead is a sequence, a word, and a woman who has seen paper like that, entered in one line, and none of the three is a reason — and a body that cannot tell a father from a piece of paper will be told what it wants by the first man who comes up a road with a bag, and that man came down that road ten days ago with nothing in a bag, and the sentence stands harder than it did a month ago.** The second week of the keep, three shillings and eightpence, not voted on, entered on the morning of the twenty-fifth. Ilsa Kern's board at one hundred and forty, said out loud. No warmth.
- **127 — *Where It Lives*** — days 207–208, the twenty-seventh and the twenty-eighth, a Wednesday and a Thursday. **Lissa Vale in one sentence: the fifth clause says the record of this route is one record and no person holding any part of it keeps a private copy of any part of it, and a collar section is not the record of the route.** **Four places a record can go in this holding and the brass will go in none of them: not the seed house book, not a return of inventories, not Cray, and not the free safekeeping — whose one line of refusal is read out again in a yard because a body that forgets why it refused is a body that is about to accept.** **The man of about seventy gives three sentences in two months and says the third is the same mistake in a different noun: the second copy of anything has to be a person, a person is not a place and a person cannot be put on a return, and the reason it has to be a person is that a person can be asked and a drawer cannot.** He is brought out **for the seventh time in a season** and the fen entered that nobody thanked him for it. **Tova Reed connects the two decisions: her own refusal of the fifteenth of last month, entered as available and not taken, and the fen's new one, and the two are the same decision and neither is about the other, and she refuses to make anything of it in a room with nine people in it.** **She names a person for the second copy; the name is not in this body's book and will not be, the person is not of the fen and has not been asked on the grounds that a woman does not become a safe place because somebody has said a sentence to her in a yard, and the clerk writes *a person named, name not entered* and the reader reads the page back three times.** The fragment itself is in the engineer of record's keeping; the description is in a drawer in the well house and with a person, and **a copy held by a person whom nobody can name is not a copy anybody can find, and the fen wanted a thing it could find in nine years' time and has decided it would rather have one it cannot.** The fifth count, said out loud, not a victory. The thirty-fifth note, dated the twenty-eighth, argument.
- **128 — *The Thirtieth*** — days 209–210, the twenty-ninth and the thirtieth, a Friday and a Saturday. **The last reading day of the seventh month: the door read whole at the tenth hour in a yard of about twenty-five, the count of the readings of the seventh column going to twenty-six, the compost line not entered and the figure not in the book and the sixth month of a run not discharged in six, and the count having gone from twenty-four to twenty-six in twenty-nine days with neither of the two caused by anything said in a room.** **The standing figures in the ordinary words, none softened: the leak at five-eighths, last measured on the first of this month, no due date and none to be invented, no cause entered; the beat logged on the twenty-first of last month and not since, column open, and the fen entered that it has not opened it now with a witness in the yard; **the boards at one hundred and forty-four, day minus sixty-six**; the March's tin at one hundred and ninety-one days, unmoved, both undertakings standing, drawer not opened; the standing rule in force with **a second draw undecided for twenty-five days**; the well at five feet and three inches, three and a half feet against a man and half an inch against the ground in two hands. **And then the keep is closed: six days over a fortnight, entered that night for the first time at one shilling and sevenpence, with the reason they were not entered on the twenty-fifth, which is that on the twenty-fifth this body did not know she would still be in the yard on the thirtieth.** **The handoff, said at a gate: the seventh month's compost line and the day letter looked at in daylight at the well with whoever is standing there, both on the first of next month, and a third thing that is not a date — the man at the second house is in this yard since the fifteenth, and the fen does not know what he is doing here and has not asked and is not going to, and a man who has not asked for one thing in fifteen days is not a thing this body has got a form for, and the form does not exist.** He says at the gate that a man can be answered and not accept it, and go on being in a yard, and **that the bucket is three and a half feet shorter and is going to be three and a half feet shorter on the first of next month**, and that he has not moved the day he put in the fen's book in a barn before he came down here. The thirty-sixth note, dated the thirtieth, argument.

**The batch is 29,496 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,720–3,119, inside 27,000–30,000 and inside 2,550–3,120 at every chapter. Ch 123 at 3,119 and Ch 119 at 3,118 are the two near a ceiling and a later writer must not lengthen either.** Mechanical check on the batch as repaired: **quotation marks even in all ten files**; **no non-ASCII glyph at all, so no curly mark and no em dash**; no month named in narration or in any document, the only capitalized *March* being *the March's tin*; the word *seat* does not appear; no Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Thornwild, no Kellan Rusk; the Continuity Office named once and only on paper; **the Quiet Break once, as a refusal carrying an age and not as a dated refusal**; no British spellings over 29,496 words; **four duplicate sentences of ten words or more, and every one of them a standing formula of this manuscript rather than a slip — the well house door reading script in Chs 121 and 128, *it was taken by nobody and it is not going to be taken* in Chs 124 and 126, and the section-nine closing line *an argument, this is worth nothing against a cut* in Chs 122, 124, 127 and 128. The earlier claim that there were none but three door lines was false and is withdrawn; the three texts stand and are enumerated in the continuity file.** The word *eleven* stands at fifty-seven, was sixty, and every remaining instance is a separate standing figure. **Volume 03 stands at 28,582 + 28,816 + 29,496 = 86,894 words across thirty chapters, and the manuscript at 365,238 + 29,496 = 394,734 across 128 chapters. Volume 02's 141,818 and Volume 01's 166,022 both stand.**

**Volume 03, *The Memory Orchard*, Movement 4, *The Unfit Category*. Ten chapters, days 211–230, the first through the twentieth of the eighth month, **29,333 words, range 2,781–3,120** by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, re-measured after the Batch 0004 review pass. No Fieldbook panel spent. Section-nine notes thirty-seventh to thirty-ninth counted, the standing sentence off the thirty-seventh only. No month named in narration or in any document. No Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Thornwild; **the name Brinewake is nowhere in the batch and is reserved to Volume 04, where it comes out of her own mouth.** The Continuity Office is not named in the batch at all. No romance. Nothing tender on the page. The ending is untouched and no new enemy was introduced.**

- **129 — *The First Of The Eighth*** — day 211, the first, a Sunday. **The seventh month's compost line, entered at about the eleventh hour by the examiner, in the steward's book, in a hand that is not the steward's, the reader being the man of about fifty and not the steward.** Nothing put in, none available, specification four hundred hundredweight, **shortfall four hundred and two hundredweight, eighty pounds eight at four shillings the hundredweight, carried forward and not discharged, the run seven months not discharged in seven**; and against the same line, separately, the hire of the man who went up on the twelfth of last month, **thirty-two shillings, four on the twelfth and twenty-four on the sixteenth**, with the two four-shilling figures entered as two figures and not one. **And a zero, entered for the first time in this body's history, as a figure and not a sentence: a hundred and forty-one trees on a shelf, and not one hundredweight of anything has ever come back to this holding.** The yard works out what a zero is not: not a loss, not a fault, not a claim. **The figure is fifty hundredweight better than the sixth month's and Anneke Roos says so out loud and will not be pleased about it in a book; the reason is that two journeys are not in it and the next one will have a journey in it, and a month in which nothing came back is twice now and was once, and the eighth month's figure is not in the book and was asked for and was not had.** Gael Senn's examination is kept at the eleventh hour, by choice, **nothing found**, and the reason the line is in another hand is restated. **On the same day, narrated from outside: four households look at the day letter in daylight at the well at the fourth house, with the occupier of that house, the man of the second house and a third person who is not of the fen, and the sheet says days twenty-eight and pails carried up two thousand and ninety-six, counted by each house in its own pail, and a pail is not a measure. And the fen entered that it was not at that well, that it chose the compost line over the shelf, that the choice was not made in a yard, and that the reason is that a body cannot be in two places on the first of a month and the seventh month of a run does not wait for a road.**
- **130 — *Served, Not Answered*** — days 212–213, the second and the third. **The instrument is served in person by Sabin Roell and the arrival is the opposite of her arrival four months ago**: met at the foot of the plateau road and not at a gate, working, her name given once and not taken down a second time because she is already in the book, and the reason is hers and is correct and this body did not think of it. **Three things in her order: the form, the line, and the fact that she cannot take the line off and is not able to.** She stands at the north gate with the door behind her and says that nobody has ever asked her whether she has read the fourth part before, and the clerk enters it as it stands. **The man of about fifty's reading: we answered this form in seven days four months ago, we put *held* against six items, we classified nothing, the office wrote that *held* is a fact about a holder and not about a record, and that answer is now the answer that puts a class on our records without a word from anybody. Silence was never a refusal. Silence was never anything at all until a line said what it is.** **Lissa Vale asks who loses and finds two: this body, and a woman on a shelf four hundred miles off whose ledger is a record of a household and the only one there is. The fen did not write to her and decided not to, and the clerk entered that it decided not to.** The reader for the answer is appointed standing up on the second; the answer is not given on the second; **the day of production is the fourteenth and this holding has twelve days and has not decided what it will say in them.**
- **131 — *Four Hands And Two Roads*** — days 214–215, the fourth and the fifth. **The four copies go out on the fourth into four hands and down two roads, and the count is said out loud with the names on them: Cray; the sub-office at Fennmere; a hired man on the plateau road; inside a licensed man's coat. This body is not nine places.** The sequence goes in a second bundle tied with a second piece of string, and is not on the return of the fourteenth. **The description of the fragment goes nowhere: two places, a drawer in the well house and a person whose name is not entered, and the clerk was asked for a third in a room and could not find one and entered that the words *so far* are not on the page.** Tova Reed's rule, entered whole: **a readable thing survives in more places than one, and the rule says nothing about a thing that is not readable and does not apply to a woman's ledger or a piece of brass with a cut in it.** And then the best thing the reader of this body says in the volume and is not thanked for: **we are spreading a rumour of one in four places; a body that is in four places has four things to be wrong about and four that have to be right, and we cannot read four of them because we cannot read one. It is still the right thing to do, and the reason this body is doing this is not that it believes in the truth. It is that it is afraid, and being in four places is the only shape of safety available to it, and that is a reason and not a principle.** The clerk's count, read back: hands four in two bundles, roads two, days out, eight days on the road of the man, four shillings on the fourth and twenty-eight on the pay-off and thirty-two whole out of the compost line of this month anyway, **people who now hold one of the four: one**, and **people who could not be found with it, if it came to that: one, a man in a coat on a fen road who may be run over in a month.**
- **132 — *The Category*** — days 216–217, the sixth and the seventh. **The class is named, on a sheet with a heading and a definition, four lines, correct: records of an experimental character, made in a working district, not confirmed by a second reading at the time of making, are held to be unfit for evidentiary use; the word *experimental* is not defined, and a holder who says a record is experimental has made a statement, and the statement is itself a classification entered against the record.** The man of about fifty: **so the way to keep a record out of the class is to declare it experimental, and the way to declare it is a statement, and the way to make no statement is to have it put in by somebody else. We built a door and the door is a trapdoor and it was built by an office that is right, and the rightness is not going to be argued with by us in a yard.** The class reaches three things and the working is entered, not only the answer: the reading on a hired trestle, a copy of a page in a locked room, and **a maintenance order copied out by hand into a household's ledger, whether which exists is not known and was not asked and is not going to be asked**, the reason being that the fen decided on the second not to write to that household about a ledger. **The count of walls is now four, every one correct, and not one of them built by this body**, and the fen is not proud of the count and the count is the only thing it can do with them. **Coll Renner brings his own problem and does not want to be asked for it: that sheet does not reach him, a class of record of an experimental character is held against the record and not against the holder, and so the one copy no office can get at is the one a man can lose.** Lissa Vale asks who loses and finds **a licensed man of forty-one with a child of nine, and about nine months of the work of the most careful record in this district**, and writes it in her own hand and recommends nothing and leaves the next line blank. The man of about seventy is not fetched and the fen enters why.
- **133 — *What A Hundred And Forty-One Trees Are Worth*** — days 218–219, a Sunday and a Monday. **The first sum in eleven years about something this body does not own, and Anneke Roos says so before she does it and gives the reason, which is that an office in nine miles has started a clock and this is the wrong place and she is doing it anyway.** **The sum in two halves and both read out: a hundred and forty-one trees, eleven acres, one hundred and nine hundredweight at a barn door, a fifth of it about twenty-two hundredweight set aside for four houses, at nine shillings the hundredweight, being a price this body heard said and not a price this body holds, giving forty-nine pounds and one shilling; and then the same trees at a price nobody has sent, which is not zero and is *not held*. The two halves were not added, not averaged and not presented as one figure, and the honest total is the first half and the two words.** **The worst of it is not the number: this holding has spent eleven years publishing every figure it has and has never once published a figure about what a person four hundred miles off is losing, and the reason is that it was not our money, and the reason it is doing it now is that an office has started a clock.** Jonas Byre asks whether they could go and weigh it and is told no, and is given the bad reason. Lissa Vale's who-loses is **four houses, a woman of fifty-four, a man with a spade and nobody in this fen**, and she enters that the man of about fifty did not send a sheet about the buckets and that there was no hour in which either of them said a word to the other about it. **A sheet goes up the road on the ninth with one question and no advice: are the rows going to be worked next season or not.** The reader reads it back, does not like it, reads it back anyway, and says out loud that **a body which has no standing over a hundred and forty-one trees asked a question about their own ground and did not tell them what to do, and those are different acts and only one of them is in the book.**
- **134 — *The Rows Are Let Go*** — days 220–221, the tenth and the eleventh. **Narrated from outside, in daylight, in the yard at the first house: four households, about nineteen people, twenty minutes. No feeding, no pruning, no selling, and a hundred and forty-one trees of bearing left standing, the grass cut twice a year because the grass is what holds the slope.** The reason is Merrid Ollvar's, nine sentences, **not a virtue**, given before anything was signed and written on the back of the reading, which is a practice of thirty-one years: **a household of four that has been told a record of it can be read has to decide what it wants read; the orchard is the largest thing four houses have; the buyer is gone and will not come back; the next season's fruit has no market in it that anybody knows of; the four houses would rather lose the rows than be the reason a piece of paper was declared anything.** A man with a spade gives the half that is not a reason: **we cut eleven branches out of one row in one week and the row is carrying and nobody can tell me why. I would rather have the trees.** And the buyer, who told them every figure before he weighed anything. **The word for what they did was available in that yard all day and was used by nobody in it and is not on their sheet, and this body did not use it either, and the fen entered that a body four hundred miles off does not get to name a thing four houses did with their own ground.** **On the eleventh the fen enters that it did not know, did not ask, and that a question about another household's ground that has to travel four days each way is not a question but a thing sent to arrive after the answer: the sheet that went up on the ninth is two days into the road, will be at a windlass on the thirteenth, and an answer to it cannot be in this yard before the seventeenth.** Marek Vale's answer puts the same order of days in the book before the answer is known, in eight days and not four. Tova Reed's objection, the best argument in the batch against itself: **a row of trees that nobody works is not a record and it is not a sacrifice until something happens in it; a readable thing survives in more places than one is still true; and a rule can be right and be the thing that does the damage.** Marek Vale's answer: **I did not. They had a buyer who is gone and a paper they cannot be got at. I wrote nine lines they did not ask for and nine days too late. If this was my doing it was done before I knew their fruit existed.** Neither is a finding and both are in the book. **The cost of the road is entered on the ninth, the morning he was hired, at four shillings a day and thirty-two for eight days of a road that is four days, four in the ledger that day and twenty-eight when he is paid off on the seventeenth.**
- **135 — *The Head*** — days 222–223, the twelfth and the thirteenth. **The second draw, attempted, on this holding's own low ground, from about the second hour of the afternoon to about the sixth, with a licensed man holding a lamp, a steward holding a page, the reader, the clerk and about nine people.** The method said out loud first: **water, heat and rootlight together, over about four feet, for about four hours, through a living path, and returned. Two links, both on a shelf four hundred miles off, and no link of his own in this yard, and the path is the fen's.** **The standing rule is set out in full, four lines, every one of them about a stop, satisfied on the third of last month, and no fifth line and there never has been one. He used the gap in it, said out loud that he was using it, and asked for the four lines to be read out loud in a yard so that everybody there had heard them. Coll Renner calls the stop and the engineer of record does not and would not have been allowed to.** Renner, in the yard, unasked, **does not answer for it, and gives the reason he gave two months ago: a man in a room with other people in it will tell you what he can do and a man with a licensed man's book in front of him will tell you what he will answer for, and the two are in this yard and they are a foot apart and it is not his job to make them agree.** His page: the mark unchanged at four inches, branching twice, no burn running past it; **the account longer than the one he took on the third of last month and not better; the two accounts of the arm do not agree and this one does not reconcile them; and an hour at about the fourth that is not in the account, asked about once and not again.** **The cost: the head this holding draws off is down four inches and has not come back, sixteen households and this body are on it, and four inches is not a quarter of an inch and a quarter of an inch is where a licensed man stops calling it a weep.** Anneke Roos's figure, in a yard in front of the households: a household of four in a dry week is about twenty-eight pails, sixteen households is four hundred and forty-eight, water brought is a shilling a pail, **twenty-two pounds four for one dry week, ninety-six pounds for four weeks and two days, against a compost line of eighty pounds eight, and ninety-six pounds is worse and she says so out loud and is not pleased about it, and the number of dry weeks in a year is not known and no year is put on the figure.** **The leak, read on the morning of the thirteenth at the seventh hour, in public, with Jonas Byre appointed standing up before the pump was touched, five-eighths, and the fen entered nothing about whether it has moved and entered no cause and no interval.** The man of about seventy, eighth in a season: **you have done the thing the rule was written to stop you doing, and the rule did not stop you, and I said on the fourth of last month that it would not, and I would like somebody in this yard to be careful about how much comfort they take from that. And in eleven years I have watched about forty people in this yard say a number and not one of them said the half of it first.** The thirty-seventh note, dated the thirteenth, a record, the standing sentence off with the reason underneath.
- **136 — *The Day Of Production*** — days 224–225, a Saturday and a Sunday. **Three go up the plateau road to the office at the foot of the plateau, which is nine miles and a day's cart and not the four-day road, and produce six items, part two five times, the sixth being the licensed man's own copy, which is not this holder's record and had no part asked for against it and none offered:** the door and the six clauses; **the seed-route ledger with the seventh column's count given as a reading and not as a number off a board, twenty-four, twenty-five, twenty-six, with the day of each, so that a person in four years can count the column and disagree — and the count is twenty-six and not twenty-seven, because the twenty-seventh reading is made on the sixteenth, and the reader says on the page that the next one is not read because this body reads a door on a day it has appointed and had appointed none** — and when the officer asks for a number and then asks whether the three agree, the reader of this body gives the shape of it and is told it is the first time in a long while a holder has given him a figure he could check; the three books **and a fourth thing in a shut drawer, a description of a piece of a machine, produced and not classified and not on any return**; the fieldbook reading, **true and incomplete, the instrument itself not produced because it is in a locked room four hundred miles off**; the licensed man's own copy, **entered on purpose so that a person in four years does not read the return and think this body has hidden a thing**; and six months of compost lines in two hands. **Nothing about the sequence, and the reason said in the cart on the way down: a sequence is not a direction, a shape is not a part, and a statement that this body cannot classify it is itself a classification, and part three exists and does not count.** The officer asks in the ordinary way whether the holder holds any other record; the answer is the six words of the sixth line of a return four months back; **the officer is not satisfied, says so, says it is his right, and says that he is doing his job and nobody in that room will be obstructed by him.** **What comes back is a note, not an answer, four lines, correct: the holder produced six items, was asked whether it held any other record, answered that it did not say, no classification is entered against any item by this note, and the holder is not required to answer further.** The reader reads it out twice in that room and asks for it to be read back three times and gives his reason, and enters **that he does not know whether he is being careful or frightened.** Lissa Vale's who-loses: **what this body has done today is tell an office, in writing, on a form, that it holds something it will not name, and the nothing is now a line in a register in nine miles and is not going to be taken back, and it has been paid for in the compost line and in nothing else.** **The Sunday, entered: a day with nothing in it.** And on the fourteenth, the households' four lines come down the four-day road **on a rider the occupier hired at the change of horses on the tenth at the fourth house's own cost, four days one way, written on the back of her own reading before anything was signed, and this body did not arrange that, did not pay for it and has not been told what it cost, and the man this body hired on the ninth with a question is still on the road and is not back before the seventeenth, with twenty-eight of his thirty-two shillings in the ledger and not discharged.** The fourth line reads **you did not ask us before we did it and we would have liked you to and we are not going to make a thing of that, and you are four hundred miles off and you could not have come, and we know that** — read out twice and not answered once.
- **137 — *Unfit*** — days 226–227, a Monday and a Tuesday. **The door read out whole at the tenth hour in a full yard, the seventh column at twenty-seven, the compost line for this month not entered with the reason entered, and a clerk from the counter at Fennmere at the back of it counting the column off the board with his finger and saying twenty-seven, unannounced and unasked.** **A sheet from the sub-office on the ordinary post, four lines, correct: two records of this holder are within the class of experimental character, and neither record is named on this sheet.** **And the resistance: the class reaches four things this body has and names none, and a body that cannot tell which two of its own records are gone is a body that has lost two things and does not know what it is looking for. So the reader of this body enters two names in advance, in a yard, as his opinion and not a finding — the reading taken on the plateau, and a copy of a page in the locked room.** **Coll Renner: the licensed man's copy is not one of them, because the class is held against the record and not against the holder and does not reach a person's coat. And the reader of this body: that is the one I was going to hand over, and you have just made it the one I cannot.** Renner then says the useful half, that both of the reader's two are the kind of thing a person in four years can look at and say yes, and asks that it be entered that he said it and that nobody thanked him. **The well on the shelf, sixth ordinary reading, the fourth hour of the sixteenth, in her own yard, by her own nail, said out loud before it was written down: five feet and two inches. The shortfall three feet and ten inches, three and a half feet against a man and four inches against nobody, the ground's share half an inch a month ago, and no entry anywhere for that well coming back.** A man hired at the change of horses **on the twelfth**, four shillings that day, thirty-two whole, the rest not paid and not entered as paid because **he is not back before the twentieth**; and the reading the reader names first was taken **on the seventeenth of a month three months back** and was produced at the office two days before this. And the day letter's count, in four pails, which the fen cannot reduce. **The man of about seventy, ninth in a season: four, that is four in three weeks and every one of them was correct, and every one of these four has come from the other side of that gate and not one of them has come from us. A wall we built ourselves we can take down. Those four we cannot take down at all and we cannot see the top of and we are not going to be told by anybody. And I would like it entered that I said that in a yard on the sixteenth of a month and that nobody answered me, because I am seventy-one and I have learned what that means.** The thirty-eighth note, dated the seventeenth, argument.
- **138 — *What Came Up Through Them*** — days 228–230, the eighteenth, the nineteenth and the twentieth. **The batch's climax, narrated from outside on the shelf, in the plainest words anybody has used there: on the sixteenth, out of the collars of the trees that were let go six days before, a green none of the four households has seen. It is in the first row first, and the first row is the row of eleven faces. It is not in the next row, and the next row is the older row nobody has read. Nobody on that shelf knows what it is. Three of the four have said out loud that they have never seen the like of it and the fourth said nothing.** Not an attack and not a symbol, and the fen did not send anybody and did not write, and has entered that a body four hundred miles off which found out four days afterwards is not a body that kept out of it, **which is correct, because the green is on the sixteenth and the fen hears on the twentieth.** **The man hired on the twelfth comes down the four-day road, having been at a windlass on the sixteenth, and is in the fen at about the seventh hour of the twentieth, the last hour of the batch, with two sheets: four lines and a figure in Merrid Ollvar's hand, **written on the sixteenth on the shelf and put into his satchel by the occupier on the day the green came up**, figure six days from the tenth to the sixteenth, and a page in Coll Renner's own hand **written on the twelfth before the man went out and carried up and back in the same satchel**, because a licensed man's standing on a row ought to travel with the row. Read out in a yard of about twenty. **Marek Vale says what he can defend and what he cannot, in that order, and what he cannot is that he cannot tell from a page whether what came up through that row is a thing somebody told the row to do or a thing the row did because nobody did anything to it, and that this is the same sentence he said in a month three months back in a barn about eleven cuts, and the fen entered that he said it twice and entered the count because the count is the point.** **And then the question, asked out loud in a yard for the first time by somebody who is not of the fen: whose order is it.** Coll Renner says it standing up, in the plainest words, and then says three sentences before anybody can answer, of which the first is that he is not asking her and does not want it read back as though he had, the second that a piece of metal in a locked room is four hundred miles off and cannot tell anybody who held the blade, and the third that the question was available to twenty people and was taken by one. Nobody answers. **The count of times the volume's question has been taken by one person is entered and is two, and neither of the two has been answered. And the man at the second house is not in that yard and is not available to anybody in it, and this body did not look for him, and looking would have meant deciding something.** Then the standing figures of the month in the ordinary way, none softened: no compost line, a leak with no due date and nothing said about whether it has moved, a beat not logged since with the column open, **the boards at one hundred and sixty-four, day minus sixty-six, cut into both on the morning of the twentieth and said out loud**, **the March's tin at two hundred and eleven days, day minus nineteen, unmoved, both undertakings standing, the drawer not opened**, the standing rule in force and satisfied and **now used once on the twelfth, and the fen entered that it was used**, a draw on a table that is not forbidden and will not be decided this month, the well three and a half feet down against a man and four inches against nobody, **and a hundred and forty-one trees four hundred miles off with no entry anywhere for the row either.** The thirty-ninth note, dated the twentieth, argument.

**The batch is 29,333 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,781–3,120, inside 27,000–30,000 and inside 2,550–3,120 at every chapter, re-measured after the review pass.
