Ch 136 is at 3,119 and Ch 138 at 3,120 and a later writer must not lengthen either.** Mechanical check: **quotation marks even in all ten files**; **no non-ASCII glyph at all**, so no curly mark and no em dash; no month named in narration or in any document, the only capitalized *March* being *the March's tin*; the word *seat* does not appear; no Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Thornwild, no Kellan Rusk; the Continuity Office is not named in the batch; no British spellings. **Four duplicate sentences of ten words or more, in three places, and every one is a standing text of this manuscript** — the class definition in Chs 132 and 137, the zero in Ch 129, and the rows in Ch 134; and separately the three clerk's four-word observations in Chs 132, 133 and 137, which share a formula and are each never explained. **Seven deviations from the batch's own cards are recorded on the face of the continuity file and all seven are checkable, and the three largest are the road and were forced by the review pass: the man who comes down the road in Ch 138 was hired on the twelfth because the road is four days each way and a whole hire is eight; the growth came up on the sixteenth and not the eighteenth because a sheet written on the eighteenth cannot be in this fen on the twentieth; and the four lines came down on a rider the occupier hired at her own cost because the fen's man was on the road from the ninth to the seventeenth.** **The road rule those three produced is that a man hired on day X and out on day X is back in the fen on day X plus eight, at four shillings a day and thirty-two shillings a whole hire.** **Volume 03 stands at 116,227 words across forty chapters and the manuscript at 424,067 across 138 chapters.**

**Volume 03, *The Memory Orchard*, Movement 5, *Copies And A Migration*, and the volume close. Nine chapters, days 231–250, the twenty-first of the eighth month through the tenth of the ninth, **26,749 words, range 2,840–3,117** by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings. No Fieldbook panel spent. Section-nine notes fortieth to forty-second counted, the standing sentence carried on all three. No month named in narration or in any document, the only capitalized *March* being *the March's tin*, *the March road* and the *Salt March Dryfoot Trust, of Cray*. The word *seat* does not appear. No Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Thornwild; **the name Brinewake is nowhere in the batch and is reserved to Volume 04.** The Continuity Office is not named in the batch at all. No romance. Nothing tender on the page. The ending is untouched and no new enemy was introduced.**

- **139 — *The Twenty-First*** — days 231–232, the twenty-first and the twenty-second, a Saturday and a Sunday. **The door read out whole at the tenth hour in a full yard of about twenty-five, the seventh column at twenty-eight, and the compost line not entered with the reason entered for the second time in nine days.** **And the answer, which is nothing, in four reasons given in the order they were given in, the fourth being that anything this body does in the next four days is a thing an office in nine miles can read as a thing it did because it was told to**; the cost in one sentence, **a body that cannot tell which two of its own records are gone is a body that has lost two things and does not know what it is looking for, and the only honest thing to do about that is to be wrong in the open and to be found out having been wrong in the open**; and **no further copies were proposed and the count of places stayed four.** Coll Renner asks what happens on the twenty-fifth and is not answered, and the reason is that the reader entered it himself and the fen entered that the second half of the question was a standing and a standing cannot be given away in a yard. **Narrated from outside, on the shelf, five days on: nobody cut it, nobody dug about it, three of the four walked out and looked at it and came back, the man with the spade said out loud that he has stopped saying what he thinks it is, a second of the four said that is the same thing said twice, and a fourth said it is going to be a year. Nobody read it, nobody named it, nobody wrote it down, and the fen has none of the three sentences and did not ask and will not be sent them.** The Sunday entered as a day with nothing in it, and the entry that the four days cost four days and not one shilling, **which is the first time in eleven years a thing this body has decided has cost it nothing to say out loud.**
- **140 — *Nine Hundred Miles*** — days 233–234, the twenty-third and the twenty-fourth. **Narrated from outside, nine hundred miles off, at a counting house at Cray: four sets of this body's records are in that building and a fifth set that a person put there, whose name is not in this book's and is not going to be**, one of the nine in the room says so out loud and the trust's steward declines to say who **in a form that could be read back.** **One question, asked by a party and not by a holder** — the Salt March Dryfoot Trust, of Cray, is a party to the seed arrangements at Lantern Reach and the cooperative is not a party to anything — **and a rule of that house and not of any office, that a question about a record made four hundred miles off is not put before it before the ninth day of a month, and the trust waited for it to be in order.** **The March road is nine days and the answer will be in this yard on the second of next month.** The fen entered on the twenty-third, eight days before there is an answer, that if the answer is that such a record is produced only to the person named in it then the request is a thing one person can make, **and that the only person this fen has entered as able to make it is not on this fen and is not in this yard and this body cannot become that person and is not going to try**, and that the man of about seventy's rule that the second copy of anything has to be a person has now been applied to a request instead of to a piece of paper and does not work any better and was not supposed to. On the twenty-fourth the reader of this body asks who loses and finds four, **and the fourth is that nobody, because a trust with a standing put a question it was entitled to put into a room that will answer it correctly, and the worst of the nine days is not the answer but that this body has a question in a room nine hundred miles off and can do nothing about it and has not got a single thing to enter against anybody while it waits.** A man of the sixteen says **nine days is a fortnight less five and there is a man on this road with a sick leg who does not have a fortnight less five**, and the reader says he has not got an answer to it.
- **141 — *A Working Record*** — days 235–236, the twenty-fifth and the twenty-sixth. **Narrated from outside, in daylight, on a shelf, with no fen character in that yard and no advice given and none asked for: a row lifted and nine of the eleven faces read with a rule and a lens and two not read and not guessed at with a stake at each of the two; the grass cut; a stone put back into the west wall; and the green along the first row cut back off the collars with a sickle in one afternoon and the cuttings left on the ground.** Nobody dug, nobody weighed it, nothing came off the shelf in a vessel, and one of the four wanted to and did not. **The reason was given twice and the two reasons are different: the ground is ours and the green is not, and a thing that comes up out of the collar of a tree is not part of the tree the way fruit is; and we are not going to pretend we know what it is and we are not going to leave it up because it is interesting, and those are two different reasons and I am giving you both.** **On the twenty-sixth the fen enters the same shape of sentence for the third time in a season, the first two being on the fourteenth and the twentieth, four days each, and enters it with four blanks in it and says so, because a body that fills blanks in afterwards can be suspected of having fitted them.** The reader of this body asks the only question in that room nobody else could ask, **did anybody tell them to cut it**, and the answer is **no**, **and the answer is a fact about this body's own outgoing book and about nothing else, and he says he asked it in a yard in order to hear a no and said before he was thanked that he was not going to be thanked for it.** Jonas Byre asks whether a man could be sent up tonight, is answered in one word, and says the other half of his own question out loud first: a man up the plateau road is four days and thirty-two shillings, and they have a compost line to write on Tuesday that nobody can say, and he would arrive on the thirty-first in a yard with four people in it and learn what four people decided on the twenty-fifth.
- **142 — *Unsafe*** — day 237, the twenty-seventh, a Friday; the whole chapter sits on that day. **The declaration, four lines, correct, on the ordinary post from the sub-office at Fennmere, and it is the shortest document this body has been sent in a year and the worst thing in this book's history: records of this holder are of an experimental character and are declared unsafe for evidentiary use; a record so declared is not to be relied upon by this office, not to be produced by an officer of the district and not to be entered in any proceeding in which the office has a part; the declaration is made in the ordinary course and no part of it is a finding against the holder, and nothing in this line states that anything the holder has said is untrue; the holder is notified, and the holder may produce any record declared unsafe and may not be prevented from doing so.** The third line is read out three times, once in a room and twice in a yard of about twenty, **and the man of about fifty's answer is that the declaration is the reason this body cannot use its own record and it is also the reason nobody has to answer it, and a body that is relieved of having to answer anything is not the same body as one that was never asked** — and that for eleven years the only defense this body has had is that it publishes everything, that defense is one sentence, and a practice is not a record and cannot be classified, **and that an office in nine miles has now put on its own letterhead that the truth of what this holding says is not in question, and he would like somebody in four years to decide whether he should be glad or whether he has been sold, and he is not going to say which in a yard.** Sabin Roell comes nine miles on foot in the same boots, is not announced, squares the sheet with two fingers, says the four things she is there to say including that the holder may produce any record declared unsafe and may not be prevented, **offers to be asked a question in the room as an officer and not as a favour, and nobody puts anything to her, and the fen entered that it declined the courtesy as well as the question and does not know which it declined first.** The steward's who loses: **one, this body, which loses its record and not the books, and a thing nobody can use is not a thing anybody has taken; two, nobody on this head, the sixteen households and their pails unchanged, entered in those words so that nobody in that yard can use the fourth line of that sheet to frighten them; and three, nobody four hundred miles off, four people in that yard and this body can account for four and has standing over none of them** — and she is the one who sends a page to a counting house every month and this is the first month she has not known whether it arrived. **The man of about seventy, tenth in a season: a book that cannot be used is not a book anybody can put a thing in**, and he says nobody will answer him and that he has stopped expecting it, which he entered as the new thing and says is not a good thing.
- **143 — *The Thirtieth*** — days 239–240, the twenty-ninth and the thirtieth, a Sunday and a Monday. **The Sunday: a sheet written on the twenty-fifth at the fourth house at the household's own cost on a rider it hired itself came down the four-day road, the four blanks were filled from the sheet and from nothing else, the page exists in this book in two states and both states are in this book, and the fourth line of the households' sheet says that nobody told them to cut it and that they have been asked that twice in a fortnight by two men who were not of them and neither of whom had told them anything and both of whom wrote it down after they had** — and the fen let two answers to one question stand on two pages nearly five thousand words apart and did not make them agree. **The thirtieth: the door read out whole at the tenth hour in a full yard of about twenty-five, the seventh column at twenty-nine, the compost line not entered with the reason entered, and the count having been checked by a clerk from a counter on the sixteenth and by nobody today and the fen not minding.** **The two numbers are cut and the rule is read out loud before either figure, which is new and which nobody asked for: the boards at one hundred and seventy-four days, day minus sixty-six, two hundred and forty less sixty-six, and the March's tin at two hundred and twenty-one days, day minus nineteen, unmoved, both undertakings standing, the drawer not opened and not to be.** The standing rule read out again in full, four lines, because a rule read only when it is used is a habit, **used once on the twelfth of this month, and the rule permitted it and says nothing whatever about what comes after the stop, and no draw has been attempted since and this body has not decided whether one will be.** **The handoff at a gate with about twenty-five people in it, four things on a Tuesday, and this body has never in eleven years sent a handoff to anybody: the compost line, a cart that is not this body's, a question about a ledger four hundred miles off that this body decided on the second of this month not to ask, and a man at the second house with an entry with no answer against it, read back twice and a third time at his own asking.** And at the end, the reader asks what the fen would like to happen on Tuesday, and the steward answers in six words, **I would like one ordinary month**, and it is read back once and not a second time because the sentence is not a figure and there is nothing on a page that can be got wrong about it. The fortieth note, dated the thirtieth, argument.
- **144 — *The First Of The Ninth*** — days 241–243, the first, the second and the third of the ninth, a Tuesday, a Wednesday and a Thursday. **The eighth month's compost line, entered at about the eleventh hour by the examiner in a hand that is not the steward's, the reader being the man of about fifty: specification four hundred hundredweight, three journeys charged to this month at twenty-five hundredweight each, shortfall four hundred and seventy-five hundredweight, ninety-five pounds, the run eight months not discharged in eight** — and against the same line, separately, **one balance of twenty-four shillings against the man hired on the fourth whose pay-off day was never entered, two men hired this month and paid off inside it, six days of a woman's keep over a fortnight at one shilling and sevenpence, and a second blanket at twopence**, and a book that puts those in the shortfall column is a book that cannot be read in four years. **Anneke Roos says worse before the figure, and the difference against the seventh month out loud, and ninety-five is worse by fourteen pounds and twelve shillings.** **And then the one time in eight months a figure on this line has moved in this holding's favour: the price of a hundredweight of compost at Cray has come down by a penny, from four shillings to three shillings and eleven pence, on a sheet with a firm's name at the head, and on four hundred and seventy-five hundredweight that is four hundred and seventy-five pence, which is thirty-nine shillings and seven pence, which is one pound, nineteen shillings and sevenpence** — said before the figure and not pleased about, and **not a gift, not a kindness, nobody has told this body why, and the fen has entered that it does not know and has not written and asked, which is the first time in eleven years it has not written and asked about a price.** **And then a man in a yard at Fennmere with four hundred and seventy-five hundredweight at three shillings and ninepence, not tested, the man cannot say what is in it, the yard behind the shed is being drained: eighty-nine pounds, one shilling and threepence, against ninety-three pounds and five pence at Cray's new price, a difference of three pounds, nineteen shillings and twopence, a tin of four pounds and fivepence, and the difference against the tin leaves one shilling and threepence** — and the difference is not the point, and the point is that in three months this holding would have no yard, no money and no line. **The two halves were not added and not averaged.** The man of about fifty: **a compost line that improves for a reason nobody can find out is worse for this body than a compost line that does not improve at all.** The man of about seventy is in the yard and is not fetched, **the count is ten as of the twenty-seventh of last month and this was not a bringing out at all**, and he says the other half: **a thing that comes down a penny and nobody can say why is not a gift, a gift is a thing somebody gives you and you can shake hands about, and a body that puts a figure like that in a book has to be able to sleep on it, and a body that cannot account for a pound is a body that cannot account for the ninety-five either and it has to stop finding out** — and the reader reads it back and does not ask him what it was about. **On the second, nine days after the question at Cray, the four lines of the sealed record come down the March road: produced to the person named in them and to no other person and on that person's own request; a request by any other person has no effect and is not entered; the holder is not a party and has no standing to request one and a party has none; the office has entered that no such request has been received and does not intend to solicit one. Nothing was opened, nobody was named, no request was made.** The man of about fifty: **that is the most defensible instrument anybody has ever written** — said in the ordinary voice, without irony and without pleasure, and the count of the times the reader has said a thing an office has done is unanswerable is two. **And Tova Reed refuses, in a room with about nine people, to say she is the only person in the world who can make that request, to have the clerk write it, to confirm it, or to have the reader read it back; a thing that one person can do is not a thing a body can be asked to do; not to be thanked, said before she is thanked**; the clerk writes *a person who could make the request is not named in this entry, has not been asked, and has not been written to*, and the reader reads it back and does not ask who.
- **145 — *Copies And A Ledger*** — days 244–245, the fourth and the fifth. **The seed-route ledger copied entire, page by page, on the fourth, in two hands — Dunmar Fitch copying, Gael Senn checking page by page and signing for the number of pages and not for the contents — from about the seventh hour of the morning to about the fifth of the afternoon, with about nine people in the room and nobody in it reading a page.** **The count of places is five and it is a new count and it is not the four of the fourth of the eighth: Cray, the sub-office at Fennmere, inside a licensed man's coat with the coat and the shelf entered as one and not as two, a table in the yard at the seed house, and the well house door, which is not a copy and is entered as a place a person can read this body's figures in** — and the reader's two halves, **a table in a yard is not a safe place and it is the best place this holding has**, both entered in the same line and neither improved on, **and the two counts are on one page and are not to be added.** And then the last argument he makes in this volume, the same shape as the one he made on the fourth of the eighth with a limb grown on it: **we are not protecting a record any more, we are spreading a rumour of one, and that was true three weeks ago and it is truer now; and the limb is that the office has told us in writing that what we say is not in question, and a body that has been declared unsafe for evidentiary use can still be published, because being unsafe is a fact about an office and not about a page, and the day a copy of it is standing in five places the office can list the copies and cannot unwrite them** — **and he does not know whether that is a victory and is not going to call it one in a yard and asked the clerk to enter that he said no to it if anybody in the room said it, and nobody did.** And then the two sentences, initialed against the entry and not against nothing: **a thing which survives in more places than one office can hold is not safe. And it is not lost. And those are two different sentences, and this body is going to have to be able to say both of them out loud for the rest of its life, and on the day it can only say one of them it will have stopped being a body and started being a party.** Tova Reed, once, in a room with about nine people: **a mechanism is not a verification, soundness is not a hand, the distance between the two is the whole of this volume, the volume is ending and the distance is still there, and the distance being there on the last day is the correct condition and a book that closed it would be a worse book** — and nobody improved on a word of it. Marek Vale's half: **he gave the copies this body can check as two, the table and the door, and the copies it cannot as three, and it was wrong and he corrected it himself before the clerk had it down, because the door is not a copy and the reader had put it in the count of five while saying in the same breath that it was the only one of the five that was not a copy of anything; the count is one and four, entered and read back, and the fen has entered that it stood a day against its own figure without anybody noticing. There is no way to make those two numbers the same, and a copy is a thing that can be out of date.** And the clerk's fourth and last four-word observation, unasked and never explained: **which of the two he believes** — the fen has entered that it does not know, that it never will, and that it has not asked him.
- **146 — *A Movement And Not A Flight*** — days 246–247, the sixth and the seventh, a Sunday and a Monday. **Four carts and a hired waggon left Fennmere between the sixth and the seventh and came up the fen road in the rain and were in this yard on the seventh, and this body sent for none of them and knew of none of them and has no standing to stop any of them, and the waggon went on in the afternoon and this body does not know where and did not ask.** The figures are in this book's because **a miller nine miles off put them in a letter and the fen entered the letter and not the man: four wells off, forty-one houses which is what it was, four hundred and forty-one people against three hundred on the eleventh of a month five months back, and nine hundred and ten pails bought in at a shilling, which is forty-five pounds and ten shillings** — and the fen entered the letter and not the man, cannot check a word of it, has standing over none of it, and **the count of things this holding has been told no by this season is four and is entered and is not going to stop being entered.** **Nobody in that yard is a hero and nobody is a refugee, and the man of about fifty says the second of two things before the first, on purpose, with the reason entered first: a body that calls this a movement has found a way of being proud of a thing it did not do; a body that calls it a flight has found a way of being pleased about somebody else's cost; and the difference between the two words is not courage.** And then the third thing, which is the chapter: **two hundred and eighty households on this fen road that have not moved and are not going to and have said so out loud, and the fact that they have said so is not loyalty, is not obstinacy, and is not evidence that this holding is worth anything, and a book that puts the ones who went and the ones who stayed in one column is a book about a body and not about two hundred and eighty households.** **Lissa Vale asks who loses and gets the first answer this holding has ever given that it could not finish: we cannot tell, and the price of a migration is not a figure this holding is entitled to and is not going to be produced by it, and no recommendation is made and she has nothing to recommend anybody with** — and she asks for her initial to be put against nothing, **which is the man of about fifty's standing and not hers, and the clerk enters that the standing was used by somebody else in a yard and that nobody corrected it and that the reader read it back and said nothing to her about it.** **Three of the four carts have said where they are going; the fourth is going up the plateau road and nobody in this fen has asked it where, and the reason was given twice, once as a decision and once as a thing this body is not sure about: asking would have meant deciding something, and this body has been told no four times this season and has stopped opening its mouth, and the person who said it said that is not prudence.** The forty-first note, dated the seventh, argument.
- **147 — *The Fourth Line*** — days 248–250, the eighth, the ninth and the tenth, a Tuesday, a Wednesday and a Thursday. **The eighth: the ledger copy in the coat goes up the plateau road, and the day letter came off the stone at the well at the fourth house and did not go back on, and the fen knows of both and has been told of neither.** **The ninth: a fourth line is added to the standing list in the seed house with about eleven people in the room — what this body holds, what this body is owed, what this body does not know, and what this body is looking after and cannot reach** — read out and read back with the hour, **a line and not a sentence**, and the difference in about ten words: **not knowing is a hole, looking after is a debt, and the difference is a hand and a distance, and the figure on the page is four hundred miles and the distance is not four hundred miles.** And then the question, in this body's own words, **naming no office, no machine, no person and no year**: who told a row of trees on a shelf four hundred miles off to do a thing in a week; eleven cuts read with a rule and a lens, nine dated, **two not read and not guessed at and not going to be read by anybody in this holding this season**; this body cannot tell whether the telling is still going on and struck that question out of its own mouth in front of witnesses on the twenty-third of a month two months back and entered that the question in this book is the worse of the two; **the question is whose order it is; it was asked out loud in a yard on the twentieth of last month by a licensed man and it was not answered; the count of the times this body has asked it is two and neither has been answered** — and the last sentence of business, **this body is not going to find out, this body is going to keep asking, and a book that says that in its own hand is a book a person in four years can hold against it.** Nobody was asked to answer it and nobody answered it, and the count of the times the question has been available to a room and not been taken is **three**. Marek Vale: **I did not strike it out because I found a better question, and the question in that entry is worse than the question it replaced, and a person in four years is going to read the two lines and think the man who wrote them had an idea at the end and I did not have an idea at the end, I had a hole and then I had a worse hole.** **The tenth: the door read out whole at the tenth hour in a yard of about twenty, the seventh column at thirty, and the standing figures none of them softened** — no compost line, the ninth month of a run of eight not discharged in eight; **the leak at five-eighths with no due date and no cause and nothing said about whether it has moved and nothing said in this volume**; the beat not logged since with the column open; **the boards at one hundred and eighty-four days, day minus sixty-six**; **the March's tin at two hundred and thirty-one days, day minus nineteen**, unmoved, both undertakings standing, the drawer not opened and not to be; the standing rule in force, four lines, used once on the twelfth of the eighth, silent about what comes after the stop, and no draw attempted since and none decided; **the well at five feet and two inches with no entry anywhere for it coming back**; and **a row of a hundred and forty-one trees with no entry anywhere for that row either.** **The man of about seventy, eleventh in a season, given the fourth line and the question, asks for both to be repeated, and says four words: write it again next year** — the shortest thing he has said in this yard in eleven years, nobody explained it, he was not asked what it was about, and **the fen entered that it does not know what it means and is not going to find out this season.** The forty-second note, dated the tenth, argument, **and then one paragraph in the same hand which is the only paragraph in eleven years of this book that is not addressed to anybody and was not read back before it was initialled, because there was nobody to read it back to: the volume's own account of itself in the ordinary words, with no figure left out and none softened, and the reason the note is the last one in the chain is that a book with no charter can still go on being written, and a book that has no charter and no figures and no one to read them is a different thing.**

**The batch is 26,749 words by `wc -w` over the chapter files including their chapter headings, range 2,840–3,117, inside 24,300–27,000 and inside 2,550–3,120 at every chapter. Ch 145 at 3,117 and Ch 144 at 3,115 are the two near a ceiling and a later writer must not lengthen either. Ch 140 at 2,840 is the shortest and was filled with scene and not with summary.** Mechanical check: **quotation marks even in every file and zero paragraphs ending with a speech open, which is tighter than any earlier volume batch**; **no non-ASCII glyph at all**, so no curly mark and no em dash; no month named in narration or in any document, the only capitalized *March* being *the March's tin*, *the March road* and the *Salt March Dryfoot Trust, of Cray*; the word *seat* does not appear; no Crown Engine, no Iona Vey, no False Season, no Thornwild, no Kellan Rusk, no Quiet Break; **the name Brinewake is nowhere in the batch**; **the Continuity Office is not named in the batch at all**; no British spellings, one instance of *defence* drafted into Ch 142 and corrected. **Nine duplicate sentences of ten words or more, and every one of them a standing text of this manuscript** — the well house door reading script in four sentences in Chs 139, 143 and 147, the compost-line-not-entered prefix in Chs 139 and 143, **Not in question.** twice in Ch 142 being the line read three times, the standing-rule heading in Chs 143 and 147, **An argument. This is worth nothing against a cut** three times being the section-nine standing sentence, and **And it is not lost.** twice in Ch 145 being one of the two sentences read back. **There is no accidental prose clone in the batch.** The arithmetic was checked line by line and holds. **Nine deviations from the batch's own cards are recorded on the face of the continuity file and all nine are checkable, and four of them are arithmetic the card got wrong and the page got right: the seventh column is twenty-nine on the thirtieth and not twenty-eight, the boards are one hundred and seventy-four and one hundred and eighty-four and not one hundred and seventy-nine and one hundred and eighty-two, the tin is two hundred and twenty-one and two hundred and thirty-one and not two hundred and twenty and two hundred and twenty-eight, and the improvement is one pound nineteen shillings and sevenpence and not twenty-six pounds eight.** Volume 03 stands at 28,582 + 28,816 + 29,496 + 29,333 + 26,749 = **142,976 words across forty-nine chapters**, and the manuscript at 424,067 + 26,749 = **450,816 words across 147 chapters.** Volume 01's 166,022 and Volume 02's 141,818 both stand.

# VOLUME 03 — THE CLOSE, CHAPTERS 99–147

**Forty chapters were touched by the volume audit and nine were not. Nothing below is a change of scene, speech, beat, name, date of a scene, or figure's referent: twenty-three word-level repairs in seventeen chapters, a closing quotation mark at the end of 174 paragraphs in thirty-five chapters, and twenty-four words taken out of Chapter 138 to pay for two of them. The nine untouched chapters are 106, 108, 136, 139, 140, 141, 142, 145 and 147, and their entries above stand exactly as written. The full record, the reasons, the two defects this close was handed and the nineteen findings it deliberately did not repair are in `state/continuity.md` under *VOLUME 03 — THE CLOSE*, and the close-up is `reviews/volume-03.findings.md`. Volume 03 after the pass: 142,952 words, range 2,620–3,119, every chapter inside 2,550–3,120, the longest chapter at 3,119 reached twice, by Ch 123 and Ch 136, and the shortest Ch 106 at 2,620.**

### Chapters repaired in wording, and one figure or one frame each

- **118** — the well at nine feet was dated *the sixteenth of a month three months back*; day 136 is in days 121 to 150, which Batch 0002's table names *a month two months back*, and the frame is now that. Plus two closing quotation marks.
- **119** — Sera Quill's keep: the second half of the fortnight was entered on the twenty-fourth where three later chapters and the reason given with them all say the twenty-fifth, and it is the twenty-fifth. Plus ten closing quotation marks.
- **121** — the man at the second house's four entries: a balance of *twenty-four* on pay-off against a whole hire of thirty-two, and four plus twenty-eight is thirty-two, so the balance is twenty-eight. Plus six closing quotation marks.
- **122** — the same hire stated in the terms as *four shillings on the twelfth and twenty-four on the sixteenth, and the whole of it is thirty-two shillings*; the second part is twenty-eight. Plus two closing quotation marks.
- **123** — the same hire stated again in her own account, *four on the twelfth of this month and twenty-four on the sixteenth*; the second part is twenty-eight. Plus eight closing quotation marks.
- **124** — *In the **nine** days since this woman walked in through that gate*, where the eleventh to the twenty-first is ten days, and nine of it may have meant the day before she came. Ten. Plus six closing quotation marks.
- **126** — three repairs in one chapter, the largest of any: the four lines were written out on the **seventeenth** of last month and not the sixteenth, because Chapter 108 has the fieldbook opened and the direction read on the seventeenth; the copy of them was made on the seventeenth at about the **third** hour and not the fourth, for the same reason; and Lissa Vale's own reason for rewriting them in a clean hand, *the copy of the seventeenth of last month is a copy of a copy*, follows the same day and not the sixteenth. Plus twelve closing quotation marks.
- **128** — the closing note said the count of the seventh column moved by two *in fourteen days* where the block four lines above it in the same chapter says twenty-nine, and 210 less 181 is twenty-nine. Plus eight closing quotation marks.
- **129** — two repairs. The hire entered *four shillings on the twelfth and twenty-four shillings on the sixteenth* is four plus twenty-eight against a whole of thirty-two, so the second part is twenty-eight. And the fen entered four hundred miles of road in front of it on *the thirty-first of last month*, and no month in this manuscript has thirty-one days: days 181 to 210 are the seventh month and its last day is the thirtieth, which is the day the compost line and the handoff are dated to two paragraphs later in the same chapter.
- **131** — a person named once in a room on *the twenty-seventh of last month*, where the naming is entered at the fourth hour of the twenty-eighth of this month in the same chapter. **Chapter 127's own entry of the same fact already carried the right day, the twenty-eighth, was listed here in error by the close, and was not touched.** Plus twelve closing quotation marks.
- **133** — two frames, both about the price asked for on paper: *on paper in a month two months back* is *in last month*, because day 187 is in days 181 to 210, and *since the first of a month two months back* is *since the first of last month*, because day 181 is the first of last month. Plus ten closing quotation marks.
- **135** — Renner's own book entry, *on the twentieth of a month two months back*, where day 170 is in days 151 to 180, which Batch 0004's table names *a month three months back*. Plus two closing quotation marks.
- **137** — the draw's cost, *the thirteenth of last month*, where day 163 is in days 151 to 180 and the same list two lines above already says *the thirtieth of a month three months back* for day 180. **This is the chapter whose decomposition of the well's shortfall is the only one in the volume that closes, and it was not touched on that account.** Plus four closing quotation marks.
- **138** — the beat's log day, *the twenty-first of last month*, is now *the twenty-first of a month three months back*, and **the day did not move: it is day 171, which is what Batch 0003's table has said from the beginning.** This is the settlement of the first of the two open defects handed to the close. The repair added two words and took the chapter to 3,123, three over a ceiling a Batch 0004 pass had told a later writer not to cross, so **twenty-four words were taken out of this chapter's own restatement of the same sentence, twice** — the stray *now* in *in a month now three months back*, and a repeated *in two rooms, to about nine people in the first and about twenty in the second* that the paragraph above already says at greater length. Chapter 138 is 3,099 and a later writer may add up to twenty-one words to it.
- **143** — a balance of *twenty-four* shillings standing against a man hired on the fourth of this month, whose whole hire is thirty-two, so twenty-eight. One line, one figure.
- **144** — the same balance again, *a man hired on the fourth of this month whose balance of twenty-four shillings is not discharged*, is twenty-eight. One line, one figure.
- **146** — three repairs, all in the miller's letter this holding cannot check a word of: the three hundred held in the same quarter *on the eleventh of a month five months back* is *a month four months back*, because day 131 is in days 121 to 150, which Batch 0005's second table names *a month four months back*; *a hundred and forty-one more than were there five months ago* is *four months ago*, and day 246 less 131 is a hundred and fifteen days, which is under four months and over three; and the note's *three hundred five months back*, which is two figures run together, is *three hundred, four months back*. Three lines, three figures, and no fact changed.

### Chapters given a closing quotation mark and nothing else

**A speech that runs over more than one paragraph opens every paragraph and closes the last. In Batches 0001 to 0004 the convention applied was the other one, so narration and further speech stood inside an unclosed quotation. One mark at the end of each paragraph, after the closing `**` where the paragraph ends in bold, and not one word moved. The counts are the paragraphs repaired in that chapter.**

- **99** — two marks. The type case of the defect is here: the reader of this body's speech opens at *I am against the fifth clause*, has an internal close, re-opens, and the paragraph ends with no mark, so that three paragraphs of narration, a nine-word utterance and a paragraph of the steward's speech all stood inside it.
- **100** — four marks.
- **101** — four marks.
- **102** — four marks.
- **103** — three marks.
- **104** — six marks, the most in Movement 1.
- **105** — three marks.
- **107** — six marks.
- **109** — five marks.
- **110** — one mark.
- **111** — five marks.
- **112** — two marks.
- **113** — four marks.
- **114** — two marks.
- **115** — two marks.
- **116** — three marks.
- **117** — one mark.
- **120** — six marks.
- **125** — twelve marks, the most in the volume.
- **127** — six marks. **Worded as above, nothing else.**
- **130** — four marks.
- **132** — two marks.
- **134** — two marks.

**Thirty-five chapters, 174 marks, all in Batches 0001 to 0004, none in Batch 0005. Quotation marks are now even in all forty-nine files and no paragraph in any of the forty-nine ends with a speech open, which is the first time in the manuscript that a volume has closed with that property in every batch.**
